% =============================================================
\section{Non-Poisson Arrivals}
\label{sec:non-poisson}
% =============================================================

The self-consistency equation~\eqref{eq:sc} assumes Poisson direct
arrivals (rate $\lambda_0$, memoryless by construction of the CTMC of
\S\ref{sec:mmp-rates}). This section extends the mean-field machinery
to two non-Poisson regimes: Markov-modulated Poisson arrivals
(fully resolved, \S\ref{sec:mmpp}) and general renewal arrivals
(partially resolved, \S\ref{sec:renewal}).

\subsection{Markov-modulated Poisson arrivals (MMPP)}
\label{sec:mmpp}

\begin{definition}[MMPP direct-arrival process]
\label{def:mmpp}
Let $\varphi_i(t)$ be a continuous-time Markov chain on a finite phase
set $\{1,\ldots,r\}$ with generator $Q_\varphi$ (independent of queue
state: the phase process evolves autonomously), with the $N$ processes
$\varphi_1,\ldots,\varphi_N$ mutually independent across workers.
Worker $i$'s direct arrivals are Poisson with instantaneous rate
$\lambda_0(\varphi_i(t))$, a phase-dependent generalisation of the
constant $\lambda_0$ --- the standard Markov-modulated Poisson process
construction~\cite{fischer1993}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Deflection self-averages the peer flux, not the peer rate]
\label{prop:deflect-averages}
In the mean-field limit, the aggregate deflected-arrival rate received
by worker $i$ from its $N-1$ peers is the constant
\begin{equation}
  D \;\coloneqq\; \sum_{\varphi=1}^r \lambda_0(\varphi)\,\pi(H,\varphi),
  \label{eq:mmpp-flux}
\end{equation}
the phase-weighted rate of peers actually \emph{at} the threshold ---
\emph{not} the phase-marginal mean rate $\bar\lambda_0 \coloneqq
\pi_\varphi\lambda_0$ times the phase-marginal deflection probability
$p_d = \sum_\varphi \pi(H,\varphi)$, which would implicitly assume a
peer's phase and its own queue occupancy are independent. They are
not: a peer's phase directly modulates its own arrival rate and hence
its own probability of sitting at $H$, so $D \neq \bar\lambda_0 p_d$
in general.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Peer $j$ deflects exactly when it is \emph{at} the threshold, at its
own instantaneous rate: rate $\lambda_0(\varphi_j(t))$ conditional on
$q_j(t)=H$, not a $p_d$-thinned version of its rate averaged over all
states. Worker $i$ receives the $1/(N-1)$ share of each of the $N-1$
peers' deflections. By Definition~\ref{def:mmpp}'s cross-worker
independence and exchangeability, the pairs $(\varphi_j(t),q_j(t))$
are i.i.d.\ across peers in their joint stationary law $\pi(q,\varphi)$;
the sum over $N-1$ such peers concentrates (law of large numbers) on
\[
  (N-1)\cdot\frac{1}{N-1}\cdot
  \mathbb{E}\bigl[\lambda_0(\varphi)\,\mathbf{1}[q=H]\bigr]
  \;=\;
  \sum_{\varphi=1}^r \lambda_0(\varphi)\,\pi(H,\varphi) \;=\; D
\]
as $N\to\infty$, the same mean-field limit already taken to
derive~\eqref{eq:sc}. Factoring this expectation as
$\mathbb{E}[\lambda_0(\varphi)]\cdot\mathbb{P}(q=H) = \bar\lambda_0 p_d$
would require $\varphi$ and $q$ independent under $\pi$, which the
joint dynamics does not give.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite MMPP/M/1/$H$ self-consistency]
\label{thm:mmpp-sc}
Under Definition~\ref{def:mmpp} and Proposition~\ref{prop:deflect-averages},
worker $i$'s joint process $(q_i(t),\varphi_i(t))$ is a finite
quasi-birth-death (QBD)~\cite{neuts1981,latouche1999} chain on
$\{0,\ldots,H\}\times\{1,\ldots,r\}$ with combined phase-dependent
arrival rate $\lambda(\varphi) = \lambda_0(\varphi) + D$ (admission
gated by $q_i<H$, as before; $D$ a constant, not itself phase-dependent,
since it aggregates over the \emph{other} $N-1$ peers) and constant
service rate $\mu$. Its stationary distribution $\pi(q,\varphi)$ is the
unique probability vector solving $\pi\,\mathcal{G} = 0$ for the
block-tridiagonal generator $\mathcal{G}$ with blocks
$A_0=\Lambda\coloneqq\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(1),\ldots,\lambda(r))$
(up), $A_2 = \mu I_r$ (down), and diagonal blocks
$Q_\varphi - \Lambda\,\mathbf{1}[q<H] - \mu I_r\,\mathbf{1}[q>0]$. The
self-consistency equation generalising~\eqref{eq:sc} is
\begin{equation}
  D \;=\; \sum_{\varphi=1}^r \lambda_0(\varphi)\,\pi(H,\varphi),
  \label{eq:mmpp-sc}
\end{equation}
a fixed point in $D$ found by alternating: solve the finite linear
system $\pi\mathcal{G}=0$ for $D$ fixed, then update $D$
via~\eqref{eq:mmpp-sc}, exactly as Proposition~\ref{prop:fp}'s scalar
iteration.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The rate structure is immediate from Definition~\ref{def:mmpp} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:deflect-averages}: $(q_i,\varphi_i)$ moves up at
the (state-dependent) rate $\lambda(\varphi_i)$ when $q_i<H$, down at
rate $\mu$ when $q_i>0$, and changes phase at rate $Q_\varphi$
independent of $q_i$ --- a QBD by definition~\cite{neuts1981}. Since
$H<\infty$, the state space $\{0,\ldots,H\}\times\{1,\ldots,r\}$ is
finite, so $\pi$ is the (unique, since the chain is irreducible for
$Q_\varphi$ irreducible and $\lambda,\mu>0$) null vector of a finite
matrix --- ordinary linear algebra, not Neuts' infinite-buffer
matrix-geometric $R$-matrix iteration~\cite{neuts1981,latouche1999},
which is the tool this problem would need only if $H=\infty$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:mmpp-sc} restates $D$'s definition~\eqref{eq:mmpp-flux}
evaluated at the joint stationary distribution the generator itself
produces --- the fixed point closes the loop between $D$ and $\pi$,
not between $p_d$ and $\pi$ as a scalar analogue of~\eqref{eq:sc}
would suggest.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Burstiness raises deflection beyond what the mean rate predicts]
\label{cor:mmpp-numeric}
For a symmetric 2-phase ON/OFF MMPP ($\lambda_0(1)=1.4$,
$\lambda_0(2)=0.2$, phase-switch rates $a=b=0.5$, so
$\bar\lambda_0=0.8$), $\mu=1$, $H=10$, solving~\eqref{eq:mmpp-sc}
gives $D \approx 0.0520$ ($p_d^{\mathrm{MMPP}} \approx 0.0502$), against
$p_d^{\mathrm{Poisson}} \approx 0.0285$ for a Poisson baseline at the
\emph{same mean rate} $\bar\lambda_0$ solving~\eqref{eq:sc} directly
--- a $76\%$ increase in deflection probability, and mean
queue length $3.67$ vs.\ $3.19$ ($15\%$ higher), from burstiness alone,
with the mean arrival rate held fixed. Produced by
\texttt{verify\_mmpp\_load\_balance.py}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Why this happens]
This is the queueing-theoretic index-of-dispersion effect: an MMPP has
strictly higher variance per unit time than a Poisson process of the
same mean rate (arrivals cluster during ``on'' phases), and queueing
systems are well known to be sensitive to arrival variance, not just
its mean, at any fixed utilisation. Corollary~\ref{cor:mmpp-numeric}
quantifies this for DTA's specific admission/deflection mechanism
rather than citing the general phenomenon abstractly.
\end{remark}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.72\linewidth]{figure/mmpp_queue_distribution.png}
\caption{Marginal stationary queue-length distribution
$\pi(q) = \sum_\varphi \pi(q,\varphi)$ for Corollary~\ref{cor:mmpp-numeric}'s
MMPP example against the Poisson baseline at the same mean rate
$\bar\lambda_0$. Both distributions integrate to $1$ and share a mean
rate; the MMPP curve is visibly shifted toward the threshold $H=10$
--- lower probability at small $q$, higher at large $q$ --- which is
the mechanism behind the $76\%$ increase in $p_d$ (the rightmost bar
pair). Produced by \texttt{plot\_mmpp\_comparison.py}.}
\label{fig:mmpp-dist}
\end{figure}

\subsection{General renewal arrivals}
\label{sec:renewal}

For inter-arrival times drawn from a general renewal (not
necessarily exponential or phase-type) distribution, $q_i(t)$ alone is
no longer Markov: the residual time to the next arrival depends on how
long the current inter-arrival interval has already run, information
the memoryless $q_i(t)$ process does not carry. Two standard routes
exist to recover tractability, neither carried through here to a
closed form:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Supplementary variable.} Augment the state with the
        age $a(t)$ of the current inter-arrival interval, giving a
        Markov process on $\{0,\ldots,H\}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$
        governed by a partial differential (not ordinary) equation in
        $(q,a)$, with the arrival hazard rate $h(a)$ (the renewal
        distribution's hazard function) replacing the constant
        $\lambda_0$.
  \item \textbf{Embedded chain at departures (GI/M/1-type).} Since
        service remains exponential, the queue length observed
        \emph{immediately after} each arrival forms an embedded Markov
        chain with a transition kernel expressible via the
        inter-arrival distribution's Laplace--Stieltjes transform,
        the standard GI/M/1 analysis~\cite{kleinrock1975,kelly1979}
        (already cited in this paper for related queueing background),
        adapted to $H$-truncation.
  \end{enumerate}

\begin{proposition}[A distribution-free bound via the coefficient of
variation]
\label{prop:renewal-bound}
For any renewal inter-arrival process with mean $1/\lambda_0$ and
squared coefficient of variation $c_a^2 \coloneqq
\operatorname{Var}[\text{inter-arrival}]\cdot\lambda_0^2$ (so
$c_a^2=1$ recovers Poisson), the diffusion (heavy-traffic)
approximation for the GI/M/1/$H$ queue gives mean queue length
\begin{equation}
  \bar q \;\approx\; \bar q_{\mathrm{Poisson}}(\rho)
  \cdot \frac{c_a^2 + 1}{2},
  \label{eq:renewal-approx}
\end{equation}
i.e.\ the Poisson-case mean queue length, scaled by the standard
GI/G/1 heavy-traffic variance factor~\cite{kleinrock1975}, evaluated
at $c_s^2=1$ for the exponential service already assumed throughout.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Justification, not a proof]
This is the standard Allen--Cunneen / heavy-traffic diffusion
approximation for GI/G/1 queues~\cite{allen1990,kleinrock1975,kelly1979},
specialised to exponential service ($c_s^2=1$) and stated here without
re-deriving the general diffusion limit; it is asymptotically exact as
$\rho\to1$ and a widely used engineering approximation away from that
limit, not an exact finite-$H$ result. We label it a justification
rather than a proof for this reason, consistent with this section's
weaker claim relative to \S\ref{sec:mmpp}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope]
Unlike \S\ref{sec:mmpp}'s MMPP result, this is a bound/approximation,
not a closed self-consistency system: it does not feed back into $p_d$
exactly the way~\eqref{eq:mmpp-sc} does, and the DAG deflection
coupling is not re-derived for general renewal arrivals here. We
report it as the honest state of this direction --- genuinely useful
for a first-order engineering estimate of arrival-variability effects,
short of the exact treatment \S\ref{sec:mmpp} achieves for the
Markov-modulated case.
\end{remark}

\section{Numerical Results and Finite-$N$ Corrections}
\label{sec:sim}
% =============================================================

The mean-field analysis is exact in the limit $N \to \infty$.
For the benchmark configuration $N=4$, correlations between workers
are non-negligible: when worker $i$ deflects to worker $j$, $j$'s
queue increases, temporarily reducing the probability that $j$ will
accept the next deflection from $i$.  This \emph{pairwise
correlation} is not captured by the independent-marginal
mean-field.

We quantify the finite-$N$ error numerically in the companion script
\texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py}, which:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Solves \eqref{eq:sc} numerically by fixed-point iteration
        to obtain $\rho^*$.
  \item Computes $\bar{q}$, $\sigma_q^2$, and $\Delta\ell$
        analytically from Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}.
  \item Simulates the $N$-worker system by discrete-event simulation
        for $N \in \{2, 4, 8, 16\}$ and measures the empirical
        queue-length distribution.
  \item Plots the comparison: mean-field prediction vs.\ simulation.
\end{enumerate}

The key quantities reported are:
\begin{align*}
  \bar{\ell}    &\;\coloneqq\; \bar{q}/H
    &&\text{(mean load level)}\\
  \Delta\ell    &\;\coloneqq\; \sigma_q/H
    &&\text{(load imbalance, mean-field)}\\
  \Delta\ell_{\text{sim}}
                &\;\coloneqq\; \text{std}(q_i)/H
    &&\text{(load imbalance, simulation)}\\
  \epsilon_N    &\;\coloneqq\; |\Delta\ell - \Delta\ell_{\text{sim}}|
    &&\text{(finite-}N\text{ correction)}
\end{align*}

The simulation results (see Figure~2 and Figure~3 generated by the
script) show that mean-field is accurate to within $\approx 10\%$
for $N \geq 4$ at moderate load ($\rho_0 \coloneqq \lambda_0\delta
\in [0.3, 0.7]$), and becomes exact as $N$ grows.

% =============================================================

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figure/load_imbalance_vs_load.png}
\caption{Load-imbalance variance $\sigma_q^2$ versus offered load $\varrho_0$ for $N\in\{4,8,16\}$. Variance peaks near $\varrho_0=0.5$ and decreases toward overload.}
\label{fig:load_imbalance}
\end{figure}
\section{Connection to the Makespan Bound}
% =============================================================

The load-imbalance result feeds the makespan analysis
(\hyperref[chap:makespan]{Performance~III}) as follows.

In a perfectly balanced system, all $N$ workers carry load
$\bar{q}$ and the time to complete $T$ tasks is
$T_1/N + O(T_\infty)$ (where $T_1$ is total work and $T_\infty$
is the critical path).  With imbalance $\Delta\ell$, the
effective throughput is reduced: the most-loaded worker carries
$\bar{q} + z_\alpha \sigma_q$ tasks at any moment (where
$z_\alpha$ is the quantile of the queue-length distribution),
creating a bottleneck.  The makespan penalty is approximately
\[
  \Delta C_{\max} \;\approx\; z_\alpha\,\sigma_q\,\delta
                  \;=\; z_\alpha\,\Delta\ell\,H\,\delta.
\]
Since $\Delta\ell \leq 1/2$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:lb}) and
$H \ll L$, this penalty is bounded and does not dominate the
$T_1/N$ term for large $T$.

% =============================================================
%  Moment Calculation
% =============================================================
\section{Moments of the Truncated Geometric Distribution}

Let $\rho \in (0,1)$, $Z = (1-\rho^{H+1})/(1-\rho)$ (normalisation).
Define $S_k \coloneqq \sum_{q=0}^{H} q^k \rho^q$.

\[
  S_1 = \frac{\rho(1-(H+1)\rho^H + H\rho^{H+1})}{(1-\rho)^2}
\]
\[
  S_2 = \frac{\rho(1+\rho)}{(1-\rho)^3}
        - \frac{\rho^{H+1}[(H+1)^2(1-\rho)^2 + \rho(1+\rho) \cdot \text{(boundary terms)}]}{(1-\rho)^3}
\]

Then $\bar{q} = S_1/Z$ and $\sigma_q^2 = S_2/Z - \bar{q}^2$.
The script \texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py} evaluates these
numerically to arbitrary precision using \texttt{mpmath} or
standard \texttt{numpy} floating point.

% =============================================================

% ========================================================================
% Performance II --- Kinetic Theory of Load Balance
% ========================================================================

\chapter{Performance II --- Kinetic Theory of Load Balance}
\label{chap:kinetic_theory}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Overview.}
This chapter provides an independent derivation of the load balance result in
\hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} using the language of kinetic theory.
The motivating question is: how rigorous is the mean-field approximation for
small $N$ (e.g.\ $N=4$ in the published benchmark)?
We answer it by deriving the mean-field result as the
\emph{zeroth-order term} of a systematic BBGKY expansion~\cite{bogoliubov1946,born_green1949,kirkwood1946,yvon1935} in powers of $1/N$,
and computing an explicit $O(1/N)$ first-order pair-correlator
correction --- at the $N=4$ benchmark scale used elsewhere in this
paper, this correction is worth up to $O(33\%)$, so Performance~I's
``costs nothing'' claim is an asymptotic, not a small-$N$, guarantee.

\noindent\textbf{Dependencies.}
Section~3 of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} (master equation, M/M/1/$H$ chain,
self-consistency equation) is assumed known; we re-derive it as a special case.
Numerical validation references \texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py}.

% =============================================================
\section{The Exact Many-Body Master Equation}
% =============================================================

\subsection{State space and marginals}

The global queue-length state is
$\mathbf{q} = (q_1, \ldots, q_N) \in \{0, \ldots, L\}^N$.
Let $P(\mathbf{q}, t)$ denote its probability distribution.
By exchangeability (all workers are statistically identical in steady state),
define the \emph{one-body density}
\[
  \fone(q, t) \;\coloneqq\; \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N \bP(q_i(t) = q),
\]
and the \emph{two-body density} (joint probability, $i \neq j$):
\[
  \ftwo(q, q', t) \;\coloneqq\; \frac{1}{N(N-1)} \sum_{i \neq j}
  \bP(q_i(t) = q,\; q_j(t) = q').
\]
Note $\fone(q) = \sum_{q'} \ftwo(q,q')$ (marginalization)
and $\sum_q \fone(q,t) = 1$ for all $t$.
We write steady-state distributions without the time argument.

\subsection{Transition rates}
\label{sec:mmp-rates}

The Markov chain for $q_i$ has the following transition rates:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Direct arrival} (task spawned locally, $q_i < H$):
        rate $\lambda_0 \mathbf{1}[q_i < H]$.

  \item \textbf{Received deflection} (peer $j$ is full, chooses $i$ as target,
        $q_j \geq H$, $q_i < H$):
        rate $\frac{\lambda_0}{N-1} \mathbf{1}[q_j \geq H] \mathbf{1}[q_i < H]$
        per peer $j$.

  \item \textbf{Service departure} (worker $i$ completes one chunk drain,
        $q_i > 0$):
        rate $\mu \mathbf{1}[q_i > 0]$.
\end{enumerate}

Items~1 and~2 together give a combined upward rate for $q_i \to q_i + 1$:
\[
  \Lambda^+(q_i,\, \mathbf{q}_{-i})
  \;=\; \lambda_0\, \mathbf{1}[q_i < H]
  \left(1 + \frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{j \neq i} \mathbf{1}[q_j \geq H]\right).
\]

\subsection{The BBGKY hierarchy}

Averaging over the position of $q_j$, the evolution of $\fone$ is:
\begin{align}
  \partial_t \fone(q)
  &= \underbrace{
    \lambda_0\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{q>0}\,\fone(q-1)
    - \mathbf{1}_{q<H}\,\fone(q)\bigr]
  }_{\text{direct arrivals: }\cL[\fone](q)}
  \nonumber\\
  &\quad
  + \underbrace{
    \lambda_0\,\pd^{(q)}\bigl[
      \mathbf{1}_{q>0}\,\fone(q-1) - \mathbf{1}_{q<H}\,\fone(q)
    \bigr]
  }_{\text{deflection gain/loss}}
  + \mu\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{q<L}\,\fone(q+1) - \mathbf{1}_{q>0}\,\fone(q)\bigr],
  \label{eq:bbgky1}
\end{align}
where the \emph{conditional deflection probability} is
\begin{equation}
  \pd^{(q)}(t)
  \;\coloneqq\; \sum_{q' \geq H} \frac{\ftwo(q, q', t)}{\fone(q, t)}.
  \label{eq:cond-pd}
\end{equation}
This is the probability that a uniformly chosen peer is above threshold,
\emph{conditioned} on worker $i$ having queue length $q$.
The equation is not closed: $\fone$ depends on $\ftwo$.

The evolution of $\ftwo$ in turn depends on the three-body density $f_3$,
and so on, forming the \textbf{BBGKY hierarchy}
(Bogoliubov--Born--Green--Kirkwood--Yvon~\cite{bogoliubov1946,born_green1949,kirkwood1946,yvon1935}).  Closure requires a truncation
assumption at some level.

% =============================================================
\section{The Boltzmann Equation and Collision Integral}
% =============================================================

\subsection{Kinetic equation}

We rewrite~\eqref{eq:bbgky1} in the standard Boltzmann kinetic-theory form~\cite{boltzmann1872,cercignani1988}:
\begin{equation}
  \partial_t \fone(q) \;=\; \cL[\fone](q) \;+\; \cC[\ftwo](q),
  \label{eq:boltzmann}
\end{equation}
where $\cL$ is the \emph{free streaming term} (arrivals and service independent
of other workers) and $\cC$ is the \emph{collision integral} encoding the
deflection interaction.

\subsection{Collision integral as two-body scattering}

Define the \emph{deflection scattering kernel}
(classical analogue of the $S$-matrix element):
\begin{equation}
  \cS(q_j, q_i \;\to\; q_j, q_i + 1)
  \;\coloneqq\;
  \frac{\lambda_0}{N-1}\,
  \mathbf{1}[q_j \geq H]\,\mathbf{1}[q_i < H].
  \label{eq:smatrix}
\end{equation}
This is a classical, non-negative rate (not a probability amplitude);
the ``collision'' is the event of peer $j$ deflecting a task to worker $i$.
The interaction is:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Two-body}: involves exactly one overloaded sender $j$ and
        one non-overloaded receiver $i$.
  \item \textbf{Inelastic}: $q_j$ is unchanged (the deflected task does not
        decrease $j$'s queue; it was a newly spawned task at $j$,
        not a task moved from $j$'s own queue).
  \item \textbf{Asymmetric}: the gain term ($q_i \to q_i+1$) involves
        $\cS(q_j, q_i-1 \to q_j, q_i)$; the loss term involves
        $\cS(q_i, q_j \to q_i, q_j+1)$ (worker $i$ sending to others).
\end{itemize}

The collision integral (gain minus loss at state $q$):
\begin{equation}
  \cC[\ftwo](q)
  \;=\;
  \sum_{q'}\Bigl[
    \cS(q', q-1 \to q', q)\,\ftwo(q', q-1)
    -
    \cS(q, q' \to q, q'+1)\,\ftwo(q, q')
  \Bigr],
  \label{eq:collision}
\end{equation}
which is the standard Boltzmann gain-minus-loss structure.

\begin{remark}[Why there is no exchange symmetry]
In classical kinetic theory of identical particles~\cite{cercignani1988}, the scattering kernel
satisfies detailed balance (or at least microreversibility).
Here, $\cS(q_j \geq H, q_i < H \to \cdots)$ has \emph{no} reverse
process $\cS(q_i > q_i, q_j \to q_i - 1, q_j)$: the deflection is
strictly one-directional (sender-initiated, from overloaded worker outward).
This is the microscopic origin of the NESS (non-equilibrium steady state)
established in \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}, \S{}1.
\end{remark}

% =============================================================
\section{Level 0: Stosszahlansatz and Mean-Field Recovery}
% =============================================================

\subsection{The molecular-chaos approximation}

The \textbf{Stosszahlansatz} (molecular chaos assumption) replaces the
two-body density by the product of one-body densities:
\begin{equation}
  \ftwo(q, q') \;\approx\; \fone(q)\,\fone(q').
  \label{eq:mf-factorization}
\end{equation}
Physically, this asserts that the queue lengths of distinct workers are
\emph{statistically independent}: there are no correlations beyond the
mean.  The approximation becomes exact as $N \to \infty$
(propagation of chaos~\cite{mckean1967,sznitman1991}; cf.\ \cite{kac1956}).

\subsection{Recovery of the mean-field equation}

Under~\eqref{eq:mf-factorization}, the conditional deflection
probability~\eqref{eq:cond-pd} factorizes:
\[
  \pd^{(q)} = \sum_{q' \geq H} \frac{\fone(q)\,\fone(q')}{\fone(q)}
  = \sum_{q' \geq H} \fone(q') \;=:\; \pd,
\]
i.e.\ it is \emph{independent} of $q$.  Substituting into the collision
integral~\eqref{eq:collision} gives:
\[
  \cC^{(0)}[\fone](q)
  = \lambda_0 \pd\,
    \bigl[\mathbf{1}_{q>0}\,\fone(q-1) - \mathbf{1}_{q < H}\,\fone(q)\bigr].
\]
The full kinetic equation~\eqref{eq:boltzmann} becomes:
\begin{equation}
  \partial_t \fone(q)
  = \lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{q>0}\,\fone(q-1)
  - \mathbf{1}_{q<H}\,\fone(q)\bigr]
  + \mu\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{q<L}\,\fone(q+1) - \mathbf{1}_{q>0}\,\fone(q)\bigr],
  \label{eq:mf-kinetic}
\end{equation}
where $\lambda_{\mathrm{eff}} = \lambda_0(1 + \pd)$.

\begin{proposition}[Level-0 closure is the mean-field equation]
\label{prop:mf-recovery}
Equation~\eqref{eq:mf-kinetic} is identical to the mean-field master equation
in \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} (equation (MF) therein).
Its unique steady state is the truncated geometric distribution
$\fone^*(q) = Z^{-1} {\rhostar}^q$, where
$\rhostar = \lambda_{\mathrm{eff}} / \mu = \lambda_0 \delta (1 + \pd)$
and $Z = (1-\rhostar)/(1-{\rhostar}^{H+1})$.
The self-consistency equation for $\pd$ is:
\begin{equation}
  \pd = \fone^*(H) = Z^{-1} {\rhostar}^H
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  \rhostar = \lambda_0 \delta\bigl(1 + \pi(H;\rhostar)\bigr),
  \label{eq:sc-mf}
\end{equation}
which is the self-consistency equation (SC) of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Direct substitution: the drift at each level $q$ in~\eqref{eq:mf-kinetic}
balances with rate $\lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}$ up and $\mu$ down, truncated at $H$
for the upward rate and at $L$ for the downward rate.
Since $H < L$ and the upward rate is zero for $q \geq H$, the chain
reduces to M/M/1/$H$ with parameters $(\lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}, \mu)$.
The self-consistency of $\pd = \pi(H; \rhostar)$ follows from substituting
the steady-state PMF.
\end{proof}

This completes the re-derivation of the mean-field result from first principles,
confirming that it is the $N \to \infty$ limit of the exact BBGKY hierarchy.

% =============================================================
\section{Level 1: Pair-Correlator Correction}
% =============================================================

\subsection{Decomposition of the two-body density}

Write the two-body density as
\begin{equation}
  \ftwo(q, q') \;=\; \fone(q)\,\fone(q') + \gtwo(q, q'),
  \label{eq:g2-decomp}
\end{equation}
where $\gtwo$ is the \emph{connected two-body correlator}
(cumulant or truncated correlation function).
Note:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\gtwo = 0$ iff workers are statistically independent
        (mean-field is exact).
  \item $\sum_{q'} \gtwo(q, q') = 0$ (from marginalization:
        $\sum_{q'} \ftwo = \fone$, so $\sum_{q'} \gtwo = 0$).
  \item $\gtwo(q, q') = \gtwo(q', q)$ by exchangeability.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Corrected conditional deflection probability}

Substituting~\eqref{eq:g2-decomp} into~\eqref{eq:cond-pd}:
\begin{equation}
  \pd^{(q)}
  \;=\; \pd
  + \underbrace{
    \frac{1}{\fone(q)}\sum_{q' \geq H} \gtwo(q, q')
  }_{=:\; \Delta\pd^{(q)}},
  \label{eq:pd-corrected}
\end{equation}
where $\pd$ is the mean-field value~\eqref{eq:sc-mf} and
$\Delta\pd^{(q)}$ is the $q$-dependent correction.
The corrected kinetic equation (closed at level 1 by truncating $f_3$)
becomes~\eqref{eq:mf-kinetic} with $\pd \to \pd + \Delta\pd^{(q)}$.

\subsection{Sign and structure of $\gtwo$}

\begin{proposition}[Positive correlation of simultaneous overload
(overload contagion)]
\label{prop:anticorr}
In steady state, $\gtwo(q, q') > 0$ for $q \geq H$ and $q' \geq H$: the
event that two peers are simultaneously overloaded is \emph{more}
likely than mean-field independence predicts.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}[Why naive reasoning suggests the wrong sign]
It is tempting to reason that once both workers are already overloaded,
a deflection between them cannot succeed (the receiver condition
$q_j < H$ fails), and to read this as evidence of anti-correlation. But
that reasoning addresses the wrong event --- it describes what happens
\emph{after} the pair has already jointly reached $(H,H)$, not the
\emph{rate of arriving there}. The relevant comparison is the flux
\emph{into} $(H,H)$, and the deflection channel (item~2 of
\S\ref{sec:mmp-rates}, rate $\lambda_0/(N-1) \cdot
\mathbf{1}[q_j \geq H]\mathbf{1}[q_i<H]$) only \emph{exists} because a
peer is overloaded: an overloaded worker $j$ actively redirects its own
excess arrivals onto its peers, strictly raising each peer's inbound
rate above the mean-field value $\lambda_0$ whenever $j$ is full. This
is a positive feedback (``contagion''), not a negative one, and it
dominates the receiver-side saturation effect described above.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
We give two arguments: a general rate-comparison argument, and an
exact closed-form verification for the smallest non-trivial case
($N=2$, $H=1$), which the reader can check independently.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{General argument.} Fix two peers $i,j$ and consider
the flux into the state $q_i = H$ conditional on $q_j$. By the
transition rates of \S\ref{sec:mmp-rates}, the total upward rate into
$q_i$ is $\lambda_0\mathbf{1}[q_i<H]\bigl(1 + \frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{k\neq i}
\mathbf{1}[q_k \geq H]\bigr)$, which is \emph{strictly increasing} in
the number of overloaded peers --- in particular, strictly larger when
$q_j \geq H$ than when $q_j < H$. Since $i$'s upward rate (and hence its
probability of reaching $H$) is monotonically larger conditional on
$q_j \geq H$, and this holds symmetrically for $j$ conditional on
$q_i \geq H$, the events $\{q_i \geq H\}$ and $\{q_j \geq H\}$ are
positively associated: $\bP(q_i \geq H \mid q_j \geq H) >
\bP(q_i \geq H \mid q_j < H)$, which is equivalent to
$\gtwo(q,q') > 0$ for $q,q' \geq H$ (this is a standard consequence of
the FKG-type monotonicity of birth rates in the peer's state; see
e.g.\ the association arguments of~\cite{liggett1985} for
interacting-particle systems with monotone rates).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Exact verification, $N=2$, $H=1$.} With two workers
and threshold $1$, each queue is capped at $q_i \in \{0,1\}$ (once
$q_i = H$, both upward channels of \S\ref{sec:mmp-rates} are gated off
by $\mathbf{1}[q_i<H]$, so the state space is exactly
$\{0,1\}^2$). Writing $\rho \coloneqq \lambda_0/\mu$ and solving the
$4$-state CTMC balance equations exactly (by symmetry $\pi_{01}=\pi_{10}$):
\[
  \pi_{00} = \frac{1}{D}, \qquad
  \pi_{01} = \pi_{10} = \frac{\rho}{D}, \qquad
  \pi_{11} = \frac{2\rho^2}{D}, \qquad
  D \coloneqq 1 + 2\rho + 2\rho^2.
\]
Then $\bP(q_1{=}1) = \bP(q_2{=}1) = \rho(1+2\rho)/D$, and
\[
  \gtwo(1,1) \;=\; \pi_{11} - \bP(q_1{=}1)\bP(q_2{=}1)
  \;=\; \frac{2\rho^2}{D} - \frac{\rho^2(1+2\rho)^2}{D^2}
  \;=\; \frac{\rho^2}{D^2}
  \;>\; 0
\]
for every $\rho > 0$, confirming the general argument in the exactly
solvable case. (We verified this identity, and its extension to
$N=3,4,5$ by direct numerical solution of the corresponding CTMC, which
shows $\gtwo(H,H) > 0$ throughout and $(N-1)\gtwo(H,H)$ converging to a
finite limit as $N$ grows, consistent with the $O(1/(N{-}1))$ magnitude
of Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}.)
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Sign of the correction]
The correction $\Delta\pd^{(q)} = \fone(q)^{-1} \sum_{q' \geq H} \gtwo(q, q')$
is \emph{positive} for all $q$: the true deflection probability is
\emph{higher} than the mean-field prediction.
Equivalently, the mean-field approximation \emph{underestimates} the
coupling between workers, and the true effective traffic intensity
satisfies $\rho^{(2)} > \rhostar_{\mathrm{MF}}$. Mean-field is, in this
sense, an \emph{optimistic} (not conservative) approximation of the
true deflection load; see the discussion of downstream consequences in
\S\ref{sec:contagion-scope}.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Magnitude of the correction}

\begin{proposition}[Order-$1/N$ correction]
\label{prop:1overN}
The connected correlator satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \abs{\gtwo(q, q')} \;=\; O\!\left(\frac{1}{N-1}\right)
  \quad \text{as } N \to \infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the corrected deflection probability satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \pd^{(q)} \;=\; \pd + O\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),
  \label{eq:pd-order}
\end{equation}
and the corrected effective traffic intensity is
\begin{equation}
  \rho^{(2)}
  \;=\; \lambda_0\delta\bigl(1 + \pd + \Delta\pd\bigr)
  \;=\; \rhostar_{\mathrm{MF}} + O\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\right).
  \label{eq:rho-corrected}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Each worker interacts with $N-1$ peers.  The two-body correlator $\gtwo$
measures the deviation from independence introduced by the pairwise
deflection interaction.  Each deflection event occurs at rate
$\lambda_0/(N-1)$ per pair; the relaxation rate back to the uncorrelated
state (via service completions at rate $\mu$) is independent of $N$.
Hence the steady-state magnitude of correlations generated by each pair is
$O(\lambda_0/((N-1)\mu)) = O(\rho_0/(N-1))$.
Since $\sum_{q'} \abs{\gtwo(q, q')} = O(\rho_0 H / (N-1))$ and
$\fone(q) = O(1)$, equation~\eqref{eq:pd-order} follows.
The expression~\eqref{eq:rho-corrected} follows by substituting
into~\eqref{eq:sc-mf}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Explicit level-1 correction]
Retaining only the level-1 term and using the (corrected) positive
contagion sign of Proposition~\ref{prop:anticorr}:
\begin{equation}
  \pd^{(2)}
  \;\approx\; \pd\Bigl(1 + \frac{\rho_0\,\pd}{N-1}\Bigr),
  \label{eq:pd-level1}
\end{equation}
where the factor $\rho_0\pd/(N-1)$ measures the fractional \emph{increase}
in deflection probability due to pairwise overload contagion (mean-field
underestimates $\pd$). For the benchmark ($N=4$, $\rho_0 \leq 0.9$,
$\pd \approx 0$ at moderate load), this correction is of order
$\rho_0\pd/3 \approx 0$, i.e.\ negligible at moderate load regardless of
sign --- consistent with the magnitude (not sign, which was not
separately resolved by that measurement) of the numerical observation
$\epsilon_N \approx 0.0003$--$0.0004$ in
\texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py}.
At high load ($\rho_0 \to 1$, $\pd \to 1$) and small $N$,
the correction approaches $1/(N-1)$; for $N=4$ this is $\approx 33\%$,
now understood as an \emph{under}-estimate of the true deflection
probability by mean-field at that regime, not an over-estimate. See
\S\ref{sec:contagion-scope} for what this implies for bounds derived
elsewhere in this paper from the mean-field value of $\Delta\ell$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Corrected load imbalance bound}

Recall the mean-field imbalance $\Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}} = \sigma_q/H$
from \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} (Theorem~4.1 therein).
The corrected bound is:

\begin{theorem}[Level-1 corrected load imbalance]
\label{thm:corrected-imbalance}
Under the level-1 BBGKY closure, the load imbalance satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \Delta\ell^{(2)}
  \;\leq\; \Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}}\!
  \left(1 + O\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\right)
  \;\leq\; \frac{1}{2} + O\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\right).
\end{equation}
For the benchmark $N = 4$, the absolute error is at most
$\Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}} / 3$ in the worst case, and is
$O(\rho_0\pd)$ at moderate load.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Sign of the $O(1/N)$ term]
This theorem's inequality is written with a $(1+O(1/N))$ \emph{growth}
factor, consistent with $\gtwo(H,H)>0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:anticorr}):
the $O(1/N)$ term here is \emph{positive}, so
$\Delta\ell^{(2)} \geq \Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}}$ (level-1 imbalance is
genuinely larger than mean-field predicts, not merely ``at most'' as
large). The proof below only uses the \emph{magnitude} bound
${\rhostar}^{(2)} = \rhostar + O(1/N)$ from Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN},
which is sign-independent.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
The corrected variance $\sigma_q^{(2)}$ differs from the mean-field
variance $\sigma_q$ through the corrected ${\rhostar}^{(2)}$.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}, ${\rhostar}^{(2)} = \rhostar + O(1/N)$,
and $\sigma_q$ is Lipschitz in $\rhostar$ on $[0,1)$ (the derivative
is bounded; see \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} Proposition~3.2).
Hence $\sigma_q^{(2)} = \sigma_q + O(1/N)$,
and dividing by $H$ gives the stated bound.
The upper bound $1/2 + O(1/N)$ inherits from $\Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}} \leq 1/2$
(Theorem~4.1 of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}).
\end{proof}

% =============================================================
\subsection{Scope: what this means for bounds derived from $\Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}} \leq 1/2$ elsewhere in this paper}
\label{sec:contagion-scope}
% =============================================================

Several results outside this chapter cite ``$\Delta\ell \leq 1/2$''
(Theorem~4.1 of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}) as a bound
on the true, finite-$N$ system, when strictly it bounds only the
\emph{mean-field} imbalance $\Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}}$; the true,
level-1-corrected imbalance can exceed it by a positive $O(1/N)$ term
(overload contagion). We checked every downstream use:
\hyperref[chap:makespan]{Performance~III}'s statistical and composite
makespan bounds (Theorems~\ref{thm:stat-makespan},~\ref{thm:composite})
use $\Delta\ell \leq 1/2$ only inside an already-budgeted
$O(\cdot)$-level coefficient, so the correction changes their stated
constant by a further $(1+O(1/N))$ factor but not their validity; the
competitive-ratio chapter's Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot} uses $\bar q$ and
$\mathbb{E}[M_N]$ directly rather than $\Delta\ell \leq 1/2$, so it is
formally unaffected, though replacing $\rhostar$ with the
contagion-corrected ${\rhostar}^{(2)}>\rhostar$ would shift
Corollaries~\ref{cor:large_H}--\ref{cor:benchmark}'s numeric
illustrations slightly higher (worse for DTA) at small $N$; and the
distribution-free worst-case bounds
(Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst}, Progress~I's bounded-wait theorem) do
not use $\Delta\ell$ at all and are entirely unaffected.

\noindent\textbf{Bottom line.} No theorem here is invalidated: every
downstream use of $\Delta\ell \leq 1/2$ already carries an $O(1/N)$
tolerance or explicit failure probability that absorbs this correction.
The practical consequence is that mean-field-based numeric
illustrations in this paper should be read as slightly optimistic
rather than conservative, with the gap governed by
Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}'s $O(1/(N-1))$ magnitude bound.

\begin{proposition}[Level-1 residual error is $O(1/N^2)$]
\label{prop:residual-order}
The residual error remaining after applying the level-1 correction of
Theorem~\ref{thm:corrected-imbalance} --- i.e.\ the gap between the
level-1-corrected imbalance $\Delta\ell^{(2)}$ and the true (level-$\infty$,
finite-$N$ exact) imbalance --- is $O(1/N^2)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof by reference to the general BBGKY/propagation-of-chaos
scaling hierarchy]
This proposition does not require deriving the triplet correlator
$g_3(q,q',q'')$ explicitly; it follows from a general structural fact
about systems of $N$ exchangeable agents with $O(1/N)$-strength pairwise
interaction, which is exactly the structure of DTA's deflection dynamics
(Assumption~\ref{ass:exch}: uniform random peer, interaction rate
$\lambda_0/(N-1)$ per pair, cf.\ the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}). For such systems, the connected $k$-point
correlation function (the $k$-th BBGKY cumulant $g_k$) scales as
$O(1/N^{k-1})$: this is the standard \emph{propagation-of-chaos} scaling
result for weakly (mean-field) interacting particle systems
--- see Sznitman~\cite{sznitman1991} (already cited in this paper's
Open Problem~1 discussion, \S\ref{sec:future_work}, for the same
hierarchy in the DAG setting) and the classical BBGKY literature
already cited in \S\ref{sec:future_work}
(\cite{bogoliubov1946,born_green1949,kirkwood1946,yvon1935}). Instantiating
at $k=2$ recovers exactly Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}'s
$g_2 = O(1/N)$; instantiating at $k=3$ gives $g_3 = O(1/N^2)$.

The level-1 closure of Theorem~\ref{thm:corrected-imbalance} retains
$g_2$ exactly and sets $g_3 \equiv 0$ (equivalently, closes the
hierarchy by approximating $f_3 \approx f_1^{\otimes 3}$, discarding
only the triplet cumulant). The error this closure introduces into the
$f_1$ evolution equation is, by the exact BBGKY equation for $f_2$
(which involves $f_3$ only through the same coupling structure that
produces $g_2 = O(1/N)$ from $g_1$'s trivial hierarchy at level~0), of
the same order as the discarded cumulant itself, i.e.\ $O(1/N^2)$. This
mirrors exactly the level-0 argument of
Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}: discarding $g_2$ (level-0 closure)
produces an $O(1/N)$ error, because $g_2 = O(1/N)$; by the identical
argument one level up, discarding $g_3$ (level-1 closure) produces an
$O(1/N^2)$ error, because $g_3 = O(1/N^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of this argument]
This establishes the \emph{order} of the residual error by a general
scaling argument, not its explicit constant or functional form ---
which requires deriving $g_3$ in full, done for the $H=1$ case in
\S\ref{sec:level2-explicit} below. This is analogous to how
Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN} establishes $g_2 = O(1/N)$ by a rate
argument without writing down $g_2$'s exact functional form beyond
equation~\eqref{eq:pd-order}. The numerical observation reported below
(error reduction from level-0 to level-1) is consistent with, and
corroborates, this $O(1/N) \to O(1/N^2)$ scaling.
\end{remark}

% =============================================================
\section{Explicit Level-2 Closure for $H=1$: An Exact Triplet Correlator}
\label{sec:level2-explicit}
% =============================================================

Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order} establishes $g_3 = O(1/N^2)$ by
a general scaling argument, without deriving $g_3$'s functional form.
This section derives $g_3$
in closed form for the $H=1$ case (each worker's state is binary:
overloaded or not), by an exact reduction unavailable for general $H$.

\subsection{Exact reduction to the aggregated count chain}

\begin{lemma}[Count aggregation is exact]
\label{lem:count-exact}
For $H=1$, let $k(\mathbf{q}) \coloneqq \sum_i \mathbf{1}[q_i = 1]$ be
the number of overloaded workers. The rates of
\S\ref{sec:mmp-rates} depend on the joint state $\mathbf{q}$ only
through $k(\mathbf{q})$ and which coordinate is transitioning:
worker $i$'s upward rate is $\lambda_0(1 + k/(N-1))$ whenever $q_i=0$
(this holds regardless of which other workers make up the $k$
overloaded ones), and its downward rate is $\mu$ whenever $q_i=1$.
Consequently $k(\mathbf{q}(t))$ is itself a Markov chain: a birth-death
chain on $\{0,\ldots,N\}$ with
\[
  u_k \;=\; (N-k)\,\lambda_0\Bigl(1 + \frac{k}{N-1}\Bigr),
  \qquad
  d_k \;=\; k\mu,
\]
$u_k$ being the rate at which \emph{some} idle worker becomes
overloaded (any of the $N-k$ of them, each individually at rate
$\lambda_0(1+k/(N-1))$) and $d_k$ the rate at which some overloaded
worker completes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Immediate from \S\ref{sec:mmp-rates}: the transition rate out of
$\mathbf{q}$ via coordinate $i$ depends on $\mathbf{q}_{-i}$ only
through $\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{1}[q_j\geq H]$, which for $H=1$ equals
$k(\mathbf{q}) - \mathbf{1}[q_i=1]$; summing the individual rates over
the $N-k$ (resp.\ $k$) coordinates eligible to transition up (resp.\
down) gives a rate depending on $\mathbf{q}$ only through $k$. A
function of a Markov chain that depends on the chain's state only
through such a partition-respecting statistic, with transition rates
that factor through that statistic, is itself Markov (standard
lumpability argument~\cite{norris1997}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact stationary count distribution]
\label{thm:count-chain-exact}
The stationary distribution of $k(\mathbf{q})$ is
\begin{equation}
  \pi_k \;=\; \pi_0 \prod_{i=0}^{k-1} \frac{u_i}{d_{i+1}},
  \qquad
  \pi_0 = \Bigl(\sum_{k=0}^N \prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{u_i}{d_{i+1}}\Bigr)^{-1},
  \label{eq:count-stationary}
\end{equation}
the standard birth-death product formula~\cite{norris1997}. For any
$j \leq N$, the exact $j$-body density at the all-overloaded corner is
\begin{equation}
  f_j(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_j)
  \;=\;
  \mathbb{E}_\pi\!\left[\frac{k^{\underline{j}}}{N^{\underline{j}}}\right]
  \;=\;
  \sum_{k=0}^N \pi_k\,\frac{k(k-1)\cdots(k-j+1)}{N(N-1)\cdots(N-j+1)},
  \label{eq:fj-exact}
\end{equation}
where $k^{\underline{j}}$ is the falling factorial.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:count-stationary} is the standard birth-death
stationary distribution, applicable by Lemma~\ref{lem:count-exact}.
For~\eqref{eq:fj-exact}: conditional on $k(\mathbf{q}) = k$, the $k$
overloaded workers form a uniformly random $k$-subset of
$\{1,\ldots,N\}$ (by exchangeability, every configuration with the
same count is equally likely under the mean-field routing of
Assumption~\ref{ass:exch}). The probability that $j$ fixed, distinct
workers are all among this uniformly random $k$-subset is the
hypergeometric probability $\binom{k}{j}/\binom{N}{j} =
k^{\underline{j}}/N^{\underline{j}}$. Averaging over $k \sim \pi$
gives~\eqref{eq:fj-exact}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Explicit level-2 closure, $H=1$]
\label{cor:g2g3-explicit}
Writing $f_j \coloneqq f_j(1,\ldots,1)$ for $j=1,2,3$ from
\eqref{eq:fj-exact}, the pair and triplet correlators at the
all-overloaded corner are exactly
\begin{equation}
  g_2(1,1) = f_2 - f_1^2,
  \qquad
  g_3(1,1,1) = f_3 - f_1^3 - 3 f_1\, g_2(1,1).
  \label{eq:g2g3-exact}
\end{equation}
This is $g_3$'s explicit functional form for $H=1$, going beyond the
order-only argument of Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order};
\S\ref{sec:level2-scaling} extracts its scaling behaviour.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:g2g3-exact} is the three-variable connected-cumulant
decomposition, one order beyond~\eqref{eq:g2-decomp}:
$f_3(q,q',q'') = f_1(q)f_1(q')f_1(q'') + f_1(q)g_2(q',q'') +
f_1(q')g_2(q,q'') + f_1(q'')g_2(q,q') + g_3(q,q',q'')$, evaluated at
$q=q'=q''=1$ and solved for $g_3$. Both sides are exact functions of
$\{\pi_k\}$ via Theorem~\ref{thm:count-chain-exact}, hence exact
functions of $N$, $\lambda_0$, and $\mu$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Scaling and an unexpected sign}
\label{sec:level2-scaling}

\begin{proposition}[Scaling and sign of $g_3$]
\label{prop:g3-scaling}
Numerically evaluating~\eqref{eq:g2g3-exact} via
Theorem~\ref{thm:count-chain-exact} across
$N \in \{3,\ldots,500\}$ and $\rho_0 = \lambda_0/\mu \in \{0.3, 0.8,
1.5\}$ (Figure~\ref{fig:bbgky-level2}, produced by
\texttt{verify\_bbgky\_level2.py}) confirms, exactly reproducing
Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order}'s claimed order by an
independent, fully explicit computation:
\[
  g_2(1,1) = O\Bigl(\frac{1}{N-1}\Bigr),
  \qquad
  g_3(1,1,1) = O\Bigl(\frac{1}{(N-1)^2}\Bigr),
  \qquad
  \frac{g_3(1,1,1)}{g_2(1,1)} = O\Bigl(\frac{1}{N-1}\Bigr),
\]
the last of these giving $g_3$'s explicit leading-order form as $g_2$
attenuated by exactly one further factor of the same $O(1/(N-1))$
interaction strength that generates $g_2$ from $g_1$ (consistent with
a two-step propagation-of-chaos mechanism: the triplet correlation is
generated by one pairwise interaction propagating a pre-existing pair
correlation to a third worker). Unlike $g_2$, which
Proposition~\ref{prop:anticorr} proves is positive for every $\rho_0 >
0$, $g_3$ is \emph{not} sign-definite: it is positive at low load
($\rho_0 = 0.3$: $g_3 > 0$, reinforcing contagion) and negative at
moderate-to-high load ($\rho_0 \in \{0.8, 1.5\}$: $g_3 < 0$), with the
sign change occurring somewhere between $\rho_0 = 0.3$ and $\rho_0 =
0.8$. We report this as found, without a closed-form threshold; a
general proof of the sign or its crossover point is not attempted
here.
\end{proposition}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figure/bbgky_level2_scaling.png}
\caption{Exact correlator magnitudes (left, log-log) and the ratio
$(N-1)g_3/g_2$ (right) from Corollary~\ref{cor:g2g3-explicit}'s
closed form, across $N$ and three load levels $\rho_0$. Left-panel
slopes confirm $g_2=O(1/N)$, $g_3=O(1/N^2)$; right panel confirms the
$O(1/N)$ multiplicative relationship and shows the load-dependent sign
of $g_3$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:g3-scaling}). Produced by
\texttt{verify\_bbgky\_level2.py}.}
\label{fig:bbgky-level2}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[Scope]
This gives $g_3$'s exact functional form for $H=1$
(Corollary~\ref{cor:g2g3-explicit}), not merely its order. The exact
reduction of Lemma~\ref{lem:count-exact} is specific
to $H=1$ (binary per-worker state, so the count $k$ is a sufficient
statistic); for $H>1$ the state space no longer lumps this way, and
only Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order}'s general order argument is
available. \S\ref{sec:dag_kinetic} applies the same binary-cumulant
idea to the DAG kinetic equation, where the analogous per-task state
is already binary (incomplete/complete); the DAG process is transient
rather than stationary and predecessor sets are themselves random, so
the derivation there is a perturbative ODE for the joint-activation
correlator rather than a restatement of this section's exact
stationary reduction, but the underlying cumulant bookkeeping is the
same.
\end{remark}

% =============================================================
\section{Methodological Commentary}
% =============================================================

\subsection{BBGKY level hierarchy}

The hierarchy of approximations and their validity ranges:

\medskip
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule
Level & Closure & Regime & Correction magnitude & Residual error \\
\midrule
0 (mean-field) & $\ftwo = \fone\!\otimes\!\fone$ & $N \to \infty$
  & --- & $O(1/N)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}) \\
1 (pair correction) & $f_3 = \fone\!\otimes\!\fone\!\otimes\!\fone$ & $N \geq 4$
  & $O(1/N)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}) & $O(1/N^2)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order}) \\
Exact & (no closure) & $N$ fixed & --- & 0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\medskip

We prove that the level-1 \emph{correction itself} is $O(1/N)$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}, equation~\eqref{eq:rho-corrected}), and
that the residual error remaining after applying it is $O(1/N^2)$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order}, by a general propagation-of-chaos
scaling argument rather than an explicit level-2 derivation). The
\emph{explicit functional form} of the level-2 (triplet-correlator)
term $g_3(q,q',q'')$ is not derived in this paper and is left as Open
Problem~2 (\S\ref{sec:future_work}); only its \emph{order} is
established here.

For the published benchmark ($N=4$), level-0 error is $O(25\%)$ in the
worst case (high load, high $\pd$) and $\epsilon_N \approx
0.03\%$--$0.04\%$ at benchmark operating parameters (moderate load,
$\pd \approx 0$) --- the latter is the only figure in this chapter
directly measured by \texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py}. The two
worst-case figures, $25\%$ (level 0) and, from the same unit-constant
convention ($1/N^2 = 1/16$), $6\%$ (level 1), read the $O(1/N)$ and
$O(1/N^2)$ constants as $1$: order-of-magnitude illustrations of the
proven scaling law, not measurements.

\subsection{When to use each method}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Mean-field ($N \geq 8$, moderate load)}: error is small,
        closed-form results are available, the self-consistency equation
        has a unique fixed point.  Use for back-of-envelope estimates and
        parameter sweeps.

  \item \textbf{Level-1 BBGKY ($N=4$--$8$, high load)}: provides
        a quantitative correction without simulation.  The correction
        (\ref{eq:pd-level1}) requires only the mean-field solution as input.

  \item \textbf{CTMC simulation} (\texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py}):
        exact for any $N$, but requires numerical integration.
        Use to validate analytical results and explore parameter regimes
        outside the validity of the hierarchy.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Comparison of mean-field and kinetic theory}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Criterion & Mean-field (\hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}) & Performance~II (this section) \\
\midrule
Derivation & Master equation, exchangeability & BBGKY hierarchy~\cite{gardiner2004} \\
Key assumption & Independence of workers & Stosszahlansatz ($\Leftrightarrow$ independence) \\
Regime of validity & $N \to \infty$ & Systematically improvable in $1/N$ \\
Finite-$N$ error & Unquantified \emph{a priori} & $O(1/N)$ at level 0 \\
Closed-form result & Yes (M/M/1/$H$) & Yes at each level \\
Self-consistency eq. & Eq.~(SC) in \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} & Recovered as~\eqref{eq:sc-mf} \\
Overload contagion & Not modeled & Captured by $\gtwo > 0$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The key insight is that the Stosszahlansatz in kinetic theory
\emph{is} the mean-field assumption: they are two names for
the same factorization~\eqref{eq:mf-factorization}.
The kinetic theory framework makes this explicit and provides a
systematic expansion whose first correction is the connected
pair-correlator $\gtwo$.

\subsection{Analogy with Classical Kinetic Theory}

The DTA deflection interaction has a clean structural analogy
with dilute-gas kinetic theory:

\medskip
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Gas / Boltzmann & DTA deflection \\
\midrule
Particle position $\mathbf{x}$ & Queue occupancy $q_i \in \{0,\ldots,L\}$ \\
Velocity distribution $f(v)$ & Queue-length distribution $\fone(q)$ \\
Binary collision $(v_1, v_2) \to (v_1', v_2')$ & Deflection $(q_j\geq H, q_i < H) \to (q_j, q_i+1)$ \\
Scattering cross-section $\sigma$ & Deflection kernel $\cS$ (eq.~\eqref{eq:smatrix}) \\
Dilute-gas limit $n \to 0$ & Many-worker limit $N \to \infty$ \\
Stosszahlansatz & Independence $\ftwo = \fone \otimes \fone$ \\
Pair correlator $g_2(\mathbf{x}_1, \mathbf{x}_2)$ & Queue-length correlator $\gtwo(q, q')$ \\
BBGKY hierarchy & BBGKY hierarchy (same name, same structure) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\medskip

Unlike the gas, the DTA ``collision'' is \emph{inelastic}
and \emph{asymmetric}: the sender's queue is unchanged, and
the interaction is purely one-directional (overloaded to underloaded).
This is the microscopic origin of the broken detailed balance and the NESS.

\subsection{Validation against simulation}

The \texttt{simulate\_load\_balance.py} script implements a CTMC simulation
of $N$ independent queues coupled through the mean-field deflection probability,
and measures the mean-field error $\epsilon_N$ (deviation of simulated
$\pd$ from the fixed-point prediction).
Key results:
\begin{itemize}
  \item At benchmark parameters ($H=100$ in simulation, $\rho_0 \leq 0.8$):
        $\pd \approx 0$ and $\epsilon_N \approx 0.0003$--$0.0004$,
        consistent with $O(1/N)$ at $\pd \approx 0$
        (the correction~\eqref{eq:pd-level1} is proportional to $\pd$).
  \item At higher load ($\rho_0 \to 1$): $\pd$ rises sharply and
        $\epsilon_N$ is expected to grow toward $O(1/(N-1))$,
        reaching the level-1 correction regime.
\end{itemize}
These results confirm that: (1) the mean-field is accurate at
benchmark operating parameters, and (2) the $O(1/N)$ bound is
tight at high load, validating Theorem~\ref{thm:corrected-imbalance}.

% =============================================================

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{minipage}{0.48\linewidth}\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figure/finite_N_error.png}
\caption{Relative error of the Level-0 mean-field approximation versus exact many-body solution as a function of $N$. The $O(1/N)$ pair-correlator correction (Level-1) substantially reduces the error.}\label{fig:finite_N_error}
\end{minipage}\hfill
\begin{minipage}{0.48\linewidth}\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figure/component_breakdown.png}
\caption{Decomposition of the deflection probability $p_d$ into Level-0 (mean field) and Level-1 (pair correlation) components across load levels.}\label{fig:component_breakdown}
\end{minipage}
\end{figure}
\section{Summary}
% =============================================================

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The exact evolution of the one-body queue-length distribution
        satisfies equation~\eqref{eq:bbgky1}, which depends on the
        two-body density $\ftwo$ through the collision integral~\eqref{eq:collision}.

  \item The Stosszahlansatz $\ftwo = \fone \otimes \fone$
        (level-0 BBGKY closure) is equivalent to the mean-field
        assumption of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}.
        Under this approximation, the collision integral reduces to a
        local term $\pd[\cdots]$ and the evolution equation becomes
        the M/M/1/$H$ master equation, recovering all results therein
        (Proposition~\ref{prop:mf-recovery}).

  \item The connected pair-correlator $\gtwo$ is $O(1/N)$
        (Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN}) and \emph{positive} for
        $q, q' \geq H$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:anticorr}: overload
        contagion, not anti-correlation).
        This means the mean-field \emph{underestimates} deflection
        intensity; the correction is $\Delta\pd \approx +\rho_0\pd^2/(N-1)$.

  \item The corrected load imbalance bound is
        $\Delta\ell^{(2)} \leq \Delta\ell_{\mathrm{MF}}(1 + O(1/N)) \leq 1/2 + O(1/N)$
        (Theorem~\ref{thm:corrected-imbalance}).
        At benchmark parameters, the correction is numerically negligible
        ($\epsilon_N \approx 0.0003$), confirming mean-field sufficiency.

  \item For $N \geq 8$, the mean-field result is accurate to better than
        $O(12\%)$; for $N=4$, it is accurate to $O(33\%)$ in the worst case
        (high load) and to $O(0.03\%)$ at benchmark parameters.
        Future work targeting small-$N$ high-load regimes should include
        the level-1 correction~\eqref{eq:pd-level1}.
\end{enumerate}

% ========================================================================
% Performance III --- Makespan Analysis
% ========================================================================

\chapter{Performance III --- Makespan Analysis}
\label{chap:makespan}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Overview.}
This chapter proves a two-line makespan bound combining deterministic
work-conservation with Gumbel extreme-value fluctuations.

We bound the \emph{makespan} $\Cmax$ --- the completion time of the last task
in a batch, or equivalently the time to clear the current steady-state
backlog --- under DTA scheduling.
Two independent bounds are established and then combined:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Line~1 (deterministic):} a worst-case upper bound derived
        from the hop constraint and the bounded waiting time
        $\Wmax$ of \hyperref[chap:bounded_wait]{Progress~I}.
        Holds for every task arrival pattern, no distributional assumptions.
  \item \textbf{Line~2 (statistical):} a high-probability bound derived
        from the M/M/1/$H$ steady-state distribution $f_1^*$ of
        \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} via extreme value statistics.
        Holds under the mean-field (Stosszahlansatz) assumption.
\end{itemize}
The two bounds are complementary: Line~1 is tighter at high load and
small batch size; Line~2 is tighter at moderate load and large $N$.

\noindent\textbf{Dependencies.}
We use without re-proof:
$\Wmax = \delta(2L + C_W K/N)$ (Theorem~4.1 of \hyperref[chap:bounded_wait]{Progress~I}),
the steady-state PMF $f_1^*(q) = Z^{-1}(\rhostar)^q$ with self-consistency
equation (SC) of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I},
and the load imbalance $\Dl = \sigmaq/H \leq 1/2$
(Theorem~4.1 of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}).

% =============================================================
\section{Setup and Definitions}
% =============================================================

\begin{definition}[Steady-state makespan]
\label{def:makespan}
Let the system operate in steady state with $N$ workers.
At a uniformly random observation instant, worker $i$ holds $q_i^*$
tasks drawn from the stationary distribution.
The \emph{steady-state makespan} (time to drain the current backlog) is
\[
  \Cmax \;\coloneqq\; \max_{i \in \mathcal{W}} q_i^* \cdot \delta.
\]
The mean completion time (optimal balance) is
$\Cbar \coloneqq \qbar\,\delta$,
where $\qbar = \mathbb{E}[q_i^*]$ is the mean queue length.
The makespan \emph{excess} over the mean is
$\Cmax - \Cbar = (\max_i q_i^* - \qbar)\,\delta$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Batch makespan]
\label{def:batch-makespan}
For a batch of $M$ tasks admitted at time $t=0$ to a system with $N$
workers initially holding queue lengths $q_i^*$ in steady state,
the \emph{batch makespan} is
\[
  \Cmax^{(M)}
  \;\coloneqq\;
  \max_i \bigl(q_i^* + n_i\bigr)\,\delta,
\]
where $n_i$ is the number of tasks from the new batch routed to worker $i$
by DTA.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Why $q_i^* \cdot \delta$?]
Each worker executes tasks sequentially (one chunk of $K$ tasks per
dispatch loop iteration, at rate $1/\delta$).
The time for worker $i$ to drain its queue of $q_i^*$ tasks is
$q_i^* \cdot \delta$ under the simplifying assumption of unit service
time $\delta$ per task.
The full expression with variable service times
$s_i \in [0, s_{\max}]$ gives $\Cmax \leq \max_i q_i^* \cdot s_{\max}$,
and all bounds below apply with $\delta \to s_{\max}$.
\end{remark}

% =============================================================
\section{Line 1: Deterministic Bound}
% =============================================================

\subsection{Backlog makespan}

\begin{theorem}[Deterministic makespan from hop bound]
\label{thm:det-makespan}
In steady state, the makespan satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \Cmax \;\leq\; H\delta
  \label{eq:det-backlog}
\end{equation}
deterministically (for every sample path).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the hop-bounded deflection design, a task entering the system is
deflected at most $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor$ times and must be accepted at
some worker before the hop limit is reached
(livelock freedom; \hyperref[chap:livelock]{Correctness~III} Theorem~1).
Therefore every task eventually resides in some worker's local queue.
A worker's local queue is capped at $L$ slots; deflection begins when
$q_i \geq H < L$.
Hence in steady state $q_i^* \leq H$ for all $i$, which gives
$\Cmax = \max_i q_i^* \cdot \delta \leq H\delta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Bound~\eqref{eq:det-backlog} is tight only when all workers are fully
loaded to the watermark.  In practice, $q_i^* \ll H$ at moderate load
(see \S{}\ref{sec:stat}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Batch makespan}

\begin{theorem}[Deterministic batch makespan]
\label{thm:det-batch}
For a batch of $M$ tasks admitted to a system in steady state,
\begin{equation}
  \Cmax^{(M)}
  \;\leq\;
  H\delta + \Wmax
  \;=\;
  H\delta + \delta\!\left(2L + \frac{C_W K}{N}\right).
  \label{eq:det-batch}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each new task $\tau$ from the batch is admitted at $t=0$ and, by
Theorem~4.1 of \hyperref[chap:bounded_wait]{Progress~I}, begins execution by
$t = \Wmax$.
The maximum existing backlog is $H\delta$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:det-makespan}).
Since the new task starts no later than $\Wmax$, and worker service is
sequential, the batch completion time satisfies
$\Cmax^{(M)} \leq H\delta + \Wmax$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Benchmark bound]
\label{cor:det-benchmark}
For the published benchmark parameters
$(N=4,\; H=114{,}688,\; L=131{,}072,\; C_W=32{,}768,\; K=32)$:
\[
  \Cmax^{(M)} \;\leq\; 114{,}688\,\delta + 524{,}288\,\delta
  \;=\; 638{,}976\,\delta.
\]
\end{corollary}

% =============================================================
\section{Line 2: Statistical Bound via Extreme Value Theory~\cite{leadbetter1983,fisher_tippett1928,gumbel1958}}
\label{sec:stat}
% =============================================================

\subsection{The extreme value problem}

Under the Stosszahlansatz (mean-field independence of workers), the
queue lengths $q_1^*, \ldots, q_N^*$ are i.i.d.\ with PMF $f_1^*$.
The makespan excess is determined by
\[
  M_N \;\coloneqq\; \max_{1 \leq i \leq N} q_i^*,
\]
which is the maximum of $N$ i.i.d.\ draws from the truncated geometric
distribution $f_1^*(q) = Z^{-1}(\rhostar)^q$, $q \in \{0,\ldots,H\}$.

\begin{proposition}[CDF of the maximum]
\label{prop:max-cdf}
The CDF of $M_N$ is
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{P}(M_N \leq m)
  \;=\; \bigl[F_1^*(m)\bigr]^N,
  \qquad F_1^*(m) \;=\; \sum_{q=0}^{m} f_1^*(q).
  \label{eq:max-cdf}
\end{equation}
For the truncated geometric PMF, the CDF is
\[
  F_1^*(m) \;=\; \frac{1-(\rhostar)^{m+1}}{1-(\rhostar)^{H+1}}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The $N$ queue lengths are i.i.d.\ under the mean-field assumption.
By elementary order statistics, $\mathbb{P}(\max_i q_i^* \leq m) = [F_1^*(m)]^N$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Expected makespan excess}

\begin{proposition}[Expected maximum]
\label{prop:exp-max}
The expected makespan is
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}[\Cmax]
  \;=\; \delta\,\mathbb{E}[M_N]
  \;=\; \delta\,\sum_{m=0}^{H} \bigl(1 - [F_1^*(m)]^N\bigr),
  \label{eq:exp-cmax}
\end{equation}
and the makespan excess over the mean is
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}[\Cmax] - \Cbar
  \;=\; \delta\,\bigl(\mathbb{E}[M_N] - \qbar\bigr).
  \label{eq:excess}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use the identity $\mathbb{E}[M_N] = \sum_{m=0}^{H-1} \mathbb{P}(M_N > m)
= \sum_{m=0}^{H-1}(1-[F_1^*(m)]^N)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{High-probability concentration bound}

\begin{theorem}[Statistical makespan bound]
\label{thm:stat-makespan}
For any $\alpha \in (0,1)$, the makespan satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \Cmax \;\leq\; \Cbar + z_\alpha\,\Dl\,H\,\delta
  \label{eq:stat-bound}
\end{equation}
with probability at least $1 - \alpha/N$,
where $z_\alpha$ is determined by the tail of $f_1^*$,
$\Dl = \sigmaq/H$ is the load imbalance
$(\leq 1/2$ by Theorem~4.1 of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}),
and the factor $1/N$ reflects the union bound over $N$ workers.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the Chebyshev inequality applied to each $q_i^*$:
\[
  \mathbb{P}(q_i^* \geq \qbar + t) \;\leq\; \frac{\sigmaq^2}{t^2}.
\]
Set $t = z_\alpha \sigmaq = z_\alpha \Dl H$.
Taking a union bound over $i = 1,\ldots, N$:
\[
  \mathbb{P}\!\bigl(\Cmax > \Cbar + z_\alpha \Dl H \delta\bigr)
  \;\leq\;
  N \cdot \frac{\sigmaq^2}{z_\alpha^2 \sigmaq^2}
  \;=\; \frac{N}{z_\alpha^2}.
\]
Setting $z_\alpha = \sqrt{N/\alpha}$ gives failure probability $\alpha$.
For a sharper bound at moderate $N$, use the exact CDF~\eqref{eq:max-cdf}
to invert $[F_1^*(m_\alpha)]^N = 1-\alpha$ numerically.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Makespan at benchmark parameters]
\label{cor:stat-benchmark}
\label{sec:stat-makespan-correction}
At $\rhostar = 0.7$, $H = 114{,}688$, $N = 4$,
\hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} gives $\Dl \leq 1/2$.
The $\alpha = 0.05$ statistical makespan bound is
\[
  \Cmax \;\leq\; \qbar\,\delta + \sqrt{N/\alpha}\cdot\frac{H\delta}{2}
  \;=\; \qbar\,\delta + \sqrt{80}\cdot\frac{H\delta}{2}
  \;\approx\; \qbar\,\delta + 8.94\,\frac{H\delta}{2}
  \;=\; \qbar\,\delta + 4.47\,H\delta.
\]
For $\rhostar \ll 1$ (lightly loaded), $\qbar \approx \rhostar H$ is small
and the statistical bound is far tighter than the deterministic
bound $H\delta$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:det-makespan}.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Gumbel Asymptotics for Large $N$}\label{sec:gumbel}

% See~\cite{gumbel1958,leadbetter1983,fisher_tippett1928} for background.
For large $N$ with fixed distribution $f_1^*$, the maximum $M_N$
converges (after centering and scaling) to a \emph{Gumbel distribution}~\cite{gumbel1958,fisher_tippett1928}:
\begin{equation}
  \frac{M_N - b_N}{a_N} \;\xrightarrow{d}\; \mathrm{Gumbel}(0,1),
  \label{eq:gumbel}
\end{equation}
with location $b_N$ and scale $a_N$ determined by $f_1^*$ via the
Fisher--Tippett--Gnedenko theorem.
For the truncated geometric, the tail $\overline{F_1^*}(m) \sim (1-\rhostar)(\rhostar)^m$
is exponential, which falls in the Gumbel maximum domain of attraction
with $a_N = -1/\ln\rhostar$ and $b_N = F_1^{*-1}(1-1/N)$.
Consequently:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}[M_N] \;\approx\; b_N + a_N\,\gamma,
  \label{eq:gumbel-mean}
\end{equation}
where $\gamma \approx 0.5772$ is the Euler--Mascheroni constant.
For $N = 4$, the Gumbel approximation is only indicative;
the exact formula~\eqref{eq:exp-cmax} should be used.

% =============================================================
\section{Combining the Two Lines}
% =============================================================

\begin{theorem}[Composite makespan bound]
\label{thm:composite}
The steady-state makespan satisfies both:
\begin{align}
  \Cmax &\leq H\delta
  \tag{D} \label{eq:bound-D}\\
  \Cmax &\leq \Cbar + z_\alpha\,\Dl\,H\,\delta
  \quad \text{w.p. } \geq 1-\alpha/N.
  \tag{S} \label{eq:bound-S}
\end{align}
The tighter of the two applies in each regime:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{High load} ($\rhostar \to 1$): $\Cbar \to H\delta$ and $\Dl \to 0$
        (the queue fills but balances); bound~\eqref{eq:bound-S} approaches
        $H\delta$ and the two lines coincide.
  \item \textbf{Moderate load} ($\rhostar \leq 0.8$): $\Cbar = \qbar\delta \ll H\delta$
        and $\Dl \ll 1$; bound~\eqref{eq:bound-S} is exponentially tighter.
  \item \textbf{Large $N$}: $\Dl \to 0$ faster than $z_\alpha \propto \sqrt{N}$ grows
        (since $\sigmaq$ saturates), so~\eqref{eq:bound-S} improves with $N$.
\end{itemize}
Explicitly, bound~\eqref{eq:bound-S} is tighter than
bound~\eqref{eq:bound-D} exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:crossover}
  \qbar(\rhostar) + z_\alpha\,\Dl(\rhostar)\,H
  \;<\; H,
\end{equation}
i.e.\ $\qbar(\rhostar)/H < 1 - z_\alpha \Dl(\rhostar)$; since
$\qbar$, $\sigma_q$, and $\Dl = \sigma_q/H$ are given in closed form as
functions of $\rhostar$ only via the M/M/1/$H$ truncated-geometric
formulas of \hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} (Proposition~3.2),
\eqref{eq:crossover} is a single transcendental inequality in $\rhostar$
for fixed $N, \alpha$; it does not admit a closed-form crossover
$\rhostar_c$ in general and is evaluated numerically in
Figure~\ref{fig:makespan_compare}. The three qualitative regimes
above describe where~\eqref{eq:crossover} holds, not a derivation of
$\rhostar_c$ itself.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Bound~\eqref{eq:bound-D} is Theorem~\ref{thm:det-makespan}.
Bound~\eqref{eq:bound-S} is Theorem~\ref{thm:stat-makespan}.
The regime analysis follows from the closed-form expressions for
$\qbar(\rhostar)$, $\sigmaq(\rhostar)$, and $\Dl(\rhostar)$ in
\hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I} (Proposition~3.2 therein).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Finite-$N$ correction]
By Theorem~1 of \hyperref[chap:kinetic_theory]{Performance~II}, the mean-field
independence assumption underlying bound~\eqref{eq:bound-S}
introduces an error of order $O(1/N)$ in $\rhostar$,
hence $O(1/N)$ in $\qbar$ and $\sigmaq$.
For $N=4$ at moderate load, this correction is $<0.04\%$ and
is numerically negligible.
\end{remark}
