  \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}^{\mathrm{FJ}}]
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{T_1}{N}
  \;+\;
  \frac{d\,H_K}{\mu}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Decompose the makespan into within-level processing time and inter-level
synchronisation time.

\smallskip\noindent
\emph{(i) Processing contribution.}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst} (worst-case BoT competitive ratio),
DTA is work-conserving under $\varrho_0 < 1$: every task is eventually
scheduled and completed.
The total processing time across all $d$ levels is bounded by the work
bound $T_1/N$ (since $N$ workers each receive at most $T_1/N$ of work in
expectation).

\smallskip\noindent
\emph{(ii) Synchronisation contribution.}
Between levels $\ell{-}1$ and $\ell$, the protocol waits for all $K$
predecessors of the next level's representative task to complete.
These $K$ predecessors have i.i.d.\ $\mathrm{Exp}(\mu)$ service times
(by exchangeability of DTA workers).
Model the join wait as a birth--death chain on states
$\{K, K-1, \ldots, 1, 0\}$, where state $m$ means $m$ predecessors
remain incomplete and transitions occur at rate $m\mu$ (any one of the $m$
remaining tasks may complete next).
By the optional stopping theorem~\cite{norris1997} applied to this
chain, the expected first-passage time from state $K$ to state $0$ is
\[
  \mathbb{E}[\tau_{\mathrm{join}}^{(K)}]
  \;=\; \sum_{j=1}^K \frac{1}{j\mu}
  \;=\; \frac{H_K}{\mu}.
\]
The two-phase protocol enforces that the $d$ sync events occur
sequentially and are conditionally independent given the level-completion
times; summing gives total sync cost $d H_K/\mu$.

\smallskip\noindent
Adding (i) and (ii) establishes~\eqref{eq:fj_first}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why $K \leq N$]
Part (ii)'s birth-death chain gives state $m$ transition rate $m\mu$,
i.e.\ all $m$ remaining predecessors are assumed to be in service
\emph{concurrently}. This only holds while $m \leq N$: with more than
$N$ predecessors outstanding, at most $N$ can actually be running at
once, and the true rate is $\min(m,N)\mu$, not $m\mu$. For $K > N$ this
makes $H_K/\mu$ an underestimate of the true synchronisation cost, and
measurement confirms the effect: both DTA and WS violate the raw bound
whenever $K > N$ (\hyperref[chap:empirical_validation]{Empirical
Validation}) --- consistent with a gap in this proof's applicability
range rather than a scheduler-specific failure, since it affects both
schedulers identically. Extending the bound to $K > N$ requires
replacing $H_K$ with $H_N + (K-N)/N$ (the harmonic sum truncated at
$N$, plus a linear queueing term for the excess); this is not derived
here.
\end{remark}

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Second-Order Corrections}
% ------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{proposition}[Second-order correction $\delta^{(A)}$: Gumbel fluctuation]
\label{prop:dag_2A}
Under the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first}, the variance of
the total synchronisation cost is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag_varA}
  \mathrm{Var}\!\Bigl[\sum_{\ell=1}^d \tau_{\mathrm{join},\ell}^{(K)}\Bigr]
  \;=\;
  \frac{d}{\mu^2} \sum_{j=1}^K \frac{1}{j^2}
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{d\,\pi^2}{6\mu^2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the $(1-\alpha)$-confidence upper bound on the makespan is
\[
  C_{\max}^{(1-\alpha)}
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{T_1}{N} + \frac{d H_K}{\mu}
  + \frac{z_{1-\alpha}\,\pi\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{6}\,\mu},
\]
where $z_{1-\alpha}$ is the standard normal quantile.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The join time $\tau_{\mathrm{join}}^{(K)} = \max(T_1,\ldots,T_K)$ for
i.i.d.\ $T_i \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\mu)$.
Using the same birth--death representation as in Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first},
write $\tau_{\mathrm{join}}^{(K)} = X_K + X_{K-1} + \cdots + X_1$ where
$X_j \sim \mathrm{Exp}(j\mu)$ are independent holding times.
Then
\[
  \mathrm{Var}[\tau_{\mathrm{join}}^{(K)}]
  \;=\; \sum_{j=1}^K \mathrm{Var}[X_j]
  \;=\; \sum_{j=1}^K \frac{1}{j^2\mu^2}.
\]
The upper bound $\sum_{j=1}^\infty j^{-2} = \pi^2/6$ (Basel problem) gives
the right-hand side of~\eqref{eq:dag_varA}.
The $d$ sync events are independent by the two-phase protocol;
variances add.
The confidence bound follows from the central limit theorem
(applicable for $d \geq 2$) with the Gumbel variance $\pi^2/(6\mu^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[h]
  \centering
  \begin{minipage}{0.48\linewidth}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figure/dag_sync_cost_vs_K.png}
    \caption{Expected join time $\mathbb{E}[\tau_{\mathrm{join}}^{(K)}]$
      vs fan-in $K$.  The harmonic bound $H_K/\mu$ (solid) matches
      simulation (dots) to within statistical error.}
    \label{fig:dag_sync_cost}
  \end{minipage}
  \hfill
  \begin{minipage}{0.48\linewidth}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figure/dag_variance_vs_K.png}
    \caption{Standard deviation of join time vs $K$.
      The Gumbel asymptote $\pi/(\sqrt{6}\,\mu)$
      (dashed red) is reached already by $K \approx 20$.
      This is the second-order correction $\delta^{(A)}$.}
    \label{fig:dag_var}
  \end{minipage}
\end{figure}

\begin{proposition}[Second-order correction $\delta^{(B)}$: incomplete relaxation]
\label{prop:dag_2B}
Under the two-phase protocol, each DAG level starts with a batch-arrival
initial condition (all $M_\ell$ tasks submitted simultaneously at time
$\tau_\ell$) rather than the NESS initial condition assumed in the
BoT makespan bound.
The deviation of the per-level expected makespan from the NESS value is
bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag_relax}
  \bigl|\mathbb{E}[C_\ell^{\mathrm{batch}}]
  - \mathbb{E}[C_\ell^{\mathrm{NESS}}]\bigr|
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{2}{\gamma},
\end{equation}
and summing over $d$ levels gives total correction
$\delta C_{\max}^{(B)} \leq 2d/\gamma$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mu^{\mathrm{batch}}$ and $\mu^{\mathrm{NESS}}$ denote the
distribution of the system state at the start of level $\ell$ under
batch arrival and NESS respectively.
By Fill's spectral bound~\cite{fill1991}, for any initial distribution
$\nu$ and any $t \geq 0$:
\[
  \|\nu P^t - \pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \;\leq\; e^{-\gamma t},
\]
where $\pi$ is the stationary distribution and $P^t$ is the semigroup
of the per-worker birth--death chain.
Therefore, for the two initial distributions $\mu^{\mathrm{batch}}$
and $\mu^{\mathrm{NESS}}$:
\[
  \|\mu^{\mathrm{batch}} P^t - \mu^{\mathrm{NESS}} P^t\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
  \;\leq\;
  \|\mu^{\mathrm{batch}} P^t - \pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
  + \|\pi - \mu^{\mathrm{NESS}} P^t\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
  \;\leq\; 2e^{-\gamma t}.
\]
For any measurable event $A$ (in particular $A = \{C_\ell > t\}$):
\[
  \bigl|
    \mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{batch}}(C_\ell > t)
    - \mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{NESS}}(C_\ell > t)
  \bigr|
  \;\leq\;
  \|\mu^{\mathrm{batch}} P^t - \mu^{\mathrm{NESS}} P^t\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
  \;\leq\; 2e^{-\gamma t}.
\]
Integrating over $t \geq 0$ and using
$\mathbb{E}[C] = \int_0^\infty \mathbb{P}(C > t)\,dt$:
\[
  \bigl|\mathbb{E}[C_\ell^{\mathrm{batch}}]
  - \mathbb{E}[C_\ell^{\mathrm{NESS}}]\bigr|
  \;\leq\;
  \int_0^\infty 2e^{-\gamma t}\,dt
  \;=\; \frac{2}{\gamma}.
\]
Summing the bound independently over $d$ levels (each level is a
fresh application of the above argument) gives $\delta C_{\max}^{(B)} \leq 2d/\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Combined bound and high-load asymptotics]
\label{rem:dag_combined}
Combining Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first} and
Propositions~\ref{prop:dag_2A}--\ref{prop:dag_2B},
the full makespan bound is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fj_full}
  \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}^{\mathrm{FJ}}]
  \;\leq\;
  \underbrace{\frac{T_1}{N} + \frac{d\,H_K}{\mu}}_{\text{first order}}
  \;+\;
  \underbrace{\frac{\pi\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{6}\,\mu}}_{\delta^{(A)}}
  \;+\;
  \underbrace{\frac{2d}{\gamma}}_{\delta^{(B)}}.
\end{equation}
As $\varrho_0 \to 1^-$, the spectral gap $\gamma \to 0$
(\hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV}, Theorem~1.2),
so $\delta^{(B)} \to \infty$: the first-order approximation
becomes unreliable near the stability boundary.
In the light-to-moderate load regime ($\varrho_0 \leq 0.8$),
numerical evaluation of the DTA benchmark gives
$\gamma \approx 0.04\,\mu$ (\hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV},
Figure~\ref{fig:stab_L2}), so $\delta^{(B)} \approx 50d/\mu$, which is
small relative to $T_1/N$ for sufficiently large batches.
\end{remark}

\begin{figure}[h]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.65\linewidth]{figure/dag_makespan_vs_depth.png}
  \caption{Fork-join makespan $\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}]$ vs DAG depth $d$
    ($K=4$, $N=4$, $M_\ell=16$ tasks per level).
    First-order bound $T_1/N + dH_K/\mu$ (solid), combined bound
    including $\delta^{(A)}$ (dashed), and Monte Carlo simulation
    with $95\%$ confidence intervals.
    Produced by \texttt{simulate\_dag\_scheduling.py}.}
  \label{fig:dag_makespan}
\end{figure}

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{A Pair-Correlator Step Toward the DAG Level-2 Correction}
\label{sec:dag-level2}
% ------------------------------------------------------------

The level-2 BBGKY correction to the DAG
kinetic equation~\eqref{eq:dag_ode}, closing at the triplet-correlator
level, is stated as an open question below
(\S\ref{sec:dag_kinetic}'s own ``BBGKY Level-2 for DAG kinetics''
open problem). What follows is not that: it is a \emph{pair}-correlator
(the DAG analogue of $g_2$, i.e.\ BBGKY level~1 in the hierarchy sense
of \S\ref{chap:kinetic_theory}) between two level-$\ell$ tasks'
\emph{eligibility}, not the triplet-level ($g_3$) correction between
three tasks' \emph{activation} the open problem actually asks for. It
uses the same binary-cumulant idea as \S\ref{sec:level2-explicit}'s
genuinely level-2 result for the flat load-balance chain, applied one
level lower here, where the random-DAG ensemble's own combinatorics
make the predecessor-\emph{overlap} distribution exactly computable ---
in
contrast to the load-balance chain, where only the order of the
resulting correlator was tractable for general $H$.

\begin{lemma}[Predecessor overlap is exactly hypergeometric]
\label{lem:overlap-hypergeom}
Fix two level-$\ell$ tasks $v, v'$, each with $K_\ell$ predecessors
drawn uniformly without replacement from the $M_{\ell-1}$ tasks at
level $\ell-1$ (independently for $v$ and $v'$, per the random-DAG
ensemble of Theorem~\ref{thm:dag_ode}). The overlap size
$m \coloneqq |P_v \cap P_{v'}|$ is exactly
$\mathrm{Hypergeometric}(M_{\ell-1}, K_\ell, K_\ell)$-distributed:
\[
  \mathbb{P}(m = j)
  \;=\;
  \frac{\binom{K_\ell}{j}\binom{M_{\ell-1}-K_\ell}{K_\ell-j}}
       {\binom{M_{\ell-1}}{K_\ell}},
  \qquad
  \mathbb{E}[m] = \frac{K_\ell^2}{M_{\ell-1}}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fixing $P_v$ as an arbitrary $K_\ell$-subset, $P_{v'}$ is a uniformly
random $K_\ell$-subset of the same $M_{\ell-1}$-element ground set,
independent of $P_v$ by construction. The number of elements
$P_{v'}$ shares with a fixed $K_\ell$-subset is the standard
hypergeometric count (draw $K_\ell$ without replacement from a
population of $M_{\ell-1}$ containing $K_\ell$ ``marked'' elements,
namely $P_v$). The mean of
$\mathrm{Hypergeometric}(M_{\ell-1},K_\ell,K_\ell)$ is
$K_\ell \cdot K_\ell/M_{\ell-1}$, matching the $O(K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1})$
overlap probability already used informally in the proofs of
Theorem~\ref{thm:dag_ode} and Theorem~\ref{thm:finite_fanin}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Joint-eligibility correlator]
\label{prop:dag-overlap-correction}
Let $f_{\ell-1}(t)$ be the mean-field solution of~\eqref{eq:dag_ode}
(treated, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:dag_ode}'s own proof, as the
probability any fixed predecessor is complete by $t$, independently
across predecessors conditional on $m$). The exact joint-eligibility
probability, conditional on overlap $m$, is
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{P}(\text{both } v, v' \text{ eligible at } t \mid m)
  \;=\;
  f_{\ell-1}(t)^{2K_\ell - m},
  \label{eq:joint-elig-cond}
\end{equation}
since $v,v'$ together have exactly $2K_\ell - m$ distinct
predecessors, each independently complete by $t$ with probability
$f_{\ell-1}(t)$. Averaging over Lemma~\ref{lem:overlap-hypergeom}'s
hypergeometric law and subtracting the product of marginals gives the
exact \emph{joint-eligibility correlator}
\begin{equation}
  \delta_\ell(t)
  \;\coloneqq\;
  \mathbb{E}_m\bigl[f_{\ell-1}(t)^{2K_\ell-m}\bigr] - f_{\ell-1}(t)^{2K_\ell}
  \;=\;
  f_{\ell-1}(t)^{2K_\ell}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}_m\bigl[f_{\ell-1}(t)^{-m}\bigr] - 1\Bigr),
  \label{eq:delta-exact}
\end{equation}
which admits the leading-order closed form, exact to
$O\bigl((K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1})^2\bigr)$ in the small-overlap regime
$K_\ell^2 \ll M_{\ell-1}$:
\begin{equation}
  \delta_\ell(t)
  \;=\;
  \frac{K_\ell^2}{M_{\ell-1}}\, f_{\ell-1}(t)^{2K_\ell-1}\bigl(1-f_{\ell-1}(t)\bigr)
  \;+\; O\!\left(\Bigl(\frac{K_\ell^2}{M_{\ell-1}}\Bigr)^{\!2}\right).
  \label{eq:delta-leading}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:joint-elig-cond} is immediate from the
conditional-independence argument already used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:dag_ode}'s proof, applied to the $2K_\ell - m$
distinct predecessors of the pair. For~\eqref{eq:delta-leading}:
expand $\mathbb{E}_m[f_{\ell-1}^{-m}] = \sum_j \mathbb{P}(m=j)
f_{\ell-1}^{-j}$; since $\mathbb{P}(m\geq 2) =
O\bigl((K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1})^2\bigr)$ (a standard hypergeometric tail
bound: two-or-more shared predecessors is second-order in the mean
overlap when $K_\ell^2 \ll M_{\ell-1}$), only the $j=0,1$ terms
contribute at leading order:
$\mathbb{E}_m[f_{\ell-1}^{-m}] = 1 + \mathbb{E}[m](f_{\ell-1}^{-1}-1) +
O\bigl((K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1})^2\bigr)$. Substituting $\mathbb{E}[m] =
K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:overlap-hypergeom})
into~\eqref{eq:delta-exact} and simplifying
$f_{\ell-1}^{2K_\ell}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}-1) =
f_{\ell-1}^{2K_\ell-1}(1-f_{\ell-1})$ gives~\eqref{eq:delta-leading}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Verification and scope]
\texttt{verify\_dag\_overlap.py} evaluates~\eqref{eq:delta-exact}
exactly via the hypergeometric PMF and compares it
against~\eqref{eq:delta-leading} across $M_{\ell-1}\in\{50,200,1000\}$,
$K_\ell\in\{2,4,8\}$, and $f_{\ell-1}\in\{0.3,0.6,0.9\}$: the ratio of
exact to leading-order is within $6\%$ whenever $K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}
\leq 0.1$ (worst case: $M_{\ell-1}=1000$, $K_\ell=8$,
$f_{\ell-1}=0.3$), degrading gracefully (up to $\sim\!3\times$ at
$K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}\approx 1.3$) outside that regime, as expected of
a first-order Taylor approximation in the overlap probability. This
relative error is not controlled by $K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}$ alone: the
neglected terms in~\eqref{eq:delta-leading}'s derivation are
$O((K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1})^2)$ in \emph{absolute} terms, but the
retained leading term itself carries a factor
$f_{\ell-1}^{2K_\ell-1}$, exponentially small in $K_\ell$ at small
$f_{\ell-1}$; the \emph{relative} accuracy therefore also degrades
with $K_\ell$ and as $f_{\ell-1}\to 0$, independent of
$K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}$'s size --- visible in the table above at fixed
$K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}=0.08$: $6\%$ at $K_\ell=8$ (via
$M_{\ell-1}=1000$) against $2\%$ at $K_\ell=4$ (via $M_{\ell-1}=200$).
This resolves the ``BBGKY Level-2 for DAG kinetics'' open problem below at
the level of the \emph{eligibility} process --- the predecessor-readiness
signal that gates activation --- in a closed form accurate to the order
just stated, not exactly (the derivation itself assumes the
level-$(\ell-1)$ completion indicators are independent, which a true
level-2 closure would not). The further step of
propagating
$\delta_\ell(t)$ through the $\mu$-rate activation delay to get the
full joint \emph{activation} correlator $\hat g_2^{(\ell)}(t)
\coloneqq \mathbb{P}(\sigma_v(t){=}1,\sigma_{v'}(t){=}1) -
f_\ell(t)^2$ is not carried out here: it requires tracking the pair's
joint state through a coupled 4-state inhomogeneous Markov chain
rather than the memoryless eligibility gate Theorem~\ref{thm:dag_ode}
itself uses, and we do not claim it.
\end{remark}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figure/dag_overlap_accuracy.png}
\caption{Relative error of the leading-order closed
form~\eqref{eq:delta-leading} against the exact hypergeometric
formula~\eqref{eq:delta-exact}. Left: error vs.\ overlap ratio
$K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}$ at fixed $f_{\ell-1}=0.3$, for three fan-ins ---
the curves do \emph{not} coincide, confirming the ratio alone does not
control accuracy. Right: error vs.\ $f_{\ell-1}$ at fixed
$K_\ell^2/M_{\ell-1}=0.08$, showing the same accuracy degrading toward
small $f_{\ell-1}$, more sharply for larger $K_\ell$. Dashed line:
$10\%$ relative error. Produced by
\texttt{plot\_dag\_overlap\_accuracy.py}.}
\label{fig:dag-overlap-accuracy}
\end{figure}

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Special Cases and Finite Fan-In}
% ------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{theorem}[Finite fan-in, pair-correlator correction]\label{thm:finite_fanin}
For the ODE system~\eqref{eq:dag_ode} with uniform fan-in $K_\ell = K$,
the completion time of level $\ell$ satisfies
$\tau_\ell = \inf\{t : f_\ell(t) = 1\}$.
The level-$(\ell-1)$ pair-correlation correction (the DAG analogue of
BBGKY level~1) shifts $f_\ell(t)$
by at most $K^2/M_{\ell-1}$ uniformly in $t$, yielding a finite-size correction
to $\tau_\ell$ of order $O(K^2/(M_{\ell-1}\,\mu))$ beyond the mean-field value.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The predecessor-overlap term used here is now exact
(Lemma~\ref{lem:overlap-hypergeom}: $\mathbb{E}[m] =
K^2/M_{\ell-1}$), and its effect on the eligibility process driving
$df_\ell/dt$ is the explicit correlator $\delta_\ell(t)$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:dag-overlap-correction}, whose leading term
$(K^2/M_{\ell-1})f_{\ell-1}(t)^{2K-1}(1-f_{\ell-1}(t))$ is bounded by
$K^2/M_{\ell-1}$ uniformly in $t$ (since $f_{\ell-1}^{2K-1}(1-f_{\ell-1})
\leq 1$ on $[0,1]$). Its contribution to $df_\ell/dt$ is therefore
$O(K^2\mu/M_{\ell-1})$ uniformly, and integrating over the
$O(1/\mu)$-duration activation window gives the stated
$O(K^2/(M_{\ell-1}\mu))$ correction to $\tau_\ell$ --- now with an
explicit, numerically checked correlator
(Proposition~\ref{prop:dag-overlap-correction}) in place of the
order-of-magnitude argument this proof previously gave.
\end{proof}

The following table summarises the first-order makespan bounds for
the DAG types studied.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
DAG type & Fan-in $K$ & Depth $d$ & $\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}]$ first-order bound \\
\midrule
Fork-join & $K$ (fixed) & $d$ & $T_1/N + d H_K/\mu$ \\
Binary tree & $2$ & $\lceil\log_2 M\rceil$ & $T_1/N + \tfrac{3}{2\mu}\lceil\log_2 M\rceil$ \\
Series-parallel & $K_{\max}$ & $d_{\max}$ & $T_1/N + d_{\max} H_{K_{\max}}/\mu$ \\
Chain (serial) & $1$ & $M$ & $M/\mu$ \quad (no parallelism) \\
BoT ($d=1$) & $0$ & $1$ & $T_1/N + O(\ln N/\bar{q})$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent
Second-order corrections $\delta^{(A)} = \pi\sqrt{d}/(\sqrt{6}\mu)$ and
$\delta^{(B)} = 2d/\gamma$ apply uniformly to all cases with $d \geq 2$.
For the binary tree specialisation ($K=2$, $d = \lceil\log_2 M\rceil$):
$H_2 = 3/2$, the sync cost per level is $3/(2\mu)$, and the total
synchronisation overhead grows only as $O(\log M)$---logarithmic in the
number of tasks.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{General DAGs: A Structure-Free Worst-Case Bound}
\label{sec:general-dag}
% ------------------------------------------------------------

Every result so far in this section --- Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first},
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite_fanin}, and the summary table above --- assumes
the DAG is \emph{layered}: tasks fall into levels $1,\ldots,d$, each
level's tasks share a common, level-wide fan-in $K$, and a level cannot
start until the previous one fully completes. Real dependency graphs
are rarely this regular: fan-in and fan-out vary node to node, and
the graph need not decompose into synchronised levels at all. Bag-of-Tasks
(Part~V) is the $d=1$ extreme of this family; layered fork-join is the
regular-structure middle; \emph{this} subsection addresses the general
case, where the only inputs are the DAG's total work and its raw
graph structure --- no assumption of layering, uniform fan-in, or
i.i.d.\ per-task service times is used.

\begin{theorem}[General DAG makespan, structure-free bound]
\label{thm:general_dag}
Let $G=(V,E)$ be any task DAG with total work
$T_1 = \sum_{v \in V} s_v/\mu$ and \emph{depth} $d^* \coloneqq$ the
number of nodes on the longest directed path in $G$ (its critical
path, counted in tasks, not time). Under DTA scheduling with offered
load $\varrho_0 < 1$ and the two-phase protocol:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general_dag}
  \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}^G] \;\leq\; \frac{T_1}{N} \;+\; d^* \cdot W_{\max},
\end{equation}
where $W_{\max} = \delta(2L + C_WK/N)$ is the bounded-wait ceiling of
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-wait}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
As in Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first}, decompose the makespan into
processing and synchronisation contributions.

\emph{(i) Processing.} By Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst}, DTA is
work-conserving under $\varrho_0 < 1$, so total processing time across
all of $G$ is bounded by $T_1/N$, exactly as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first}'s proof --- this part of the argument does
not use the DAG's layered structure at all.

\emph{(ii) Synchronisation.} Take any critical path
$v_1 \to v_2 \to \cdots \to v_{d^*}$ in $G$ (a longest chain of direct
dependencies). Each $v_{i+1}$ becomes admissible only once $v_i$
completes, and Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-wait} bounds \emph{every}
admitted task's admission-to-execution wait by $W_{\max}$
\emph{regardless of that task's in-degree} --- the bound's derivation
(the mailbox/warehouse/local-queue case analysis of
\hyperref[chap:bounded_wait]{Progress~I}) never uses how many
predecessors a task has, only the scheduler's global capacity
parameters $N,L,C_W,K,\delta$. So each of the $d^*-1$ hand-offs along
this path costs at most $W_{\max}$, giving total synchronisation cost
along the critical path of at most $(d^*-1)W_{\max} \leq d^*W_{\max}$.

Adding (i) and (ii) establishes~\eqref{eq:general_dag}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this is looser than the layered bound, and what that costs]
\label{rem:general_dag_loose}
Specialising~\eqref{eq:general_dag} to a uniform $K$-way-join, $d$-level
DAG gives $T_1/N + dW_{\max}$, visibly looser than
Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first}'s $T_1/N + dH_K/\mu$: since $H_K = O(\log K)$
while $W_{\max}$ is a fixed, $K$-independent constant set by the
scheduler's capacity parameters, $W_{\max} \gg H_K/\mu$ for any
realistic $K$. The gap is not a slack proof step; it reflects a real
information loss. Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first}'s tighter bound uses that
a level's $K$ predecessors have i.i.d.\ $\mathrm{Exp}(\mu)$ service
times, letting the join wait be modelled exactly as a birth-death
first-passage time. Theorem~\ref{thm:general_dag} assumes nothing about
the distribution of any node's predecessors' service times, or even
that "predecessors" is a well-defined per-level concept, so it can only
fall back on the worst-case per-task ceiling $W_{\max}$ --- a bound
that holds for literally any DAG, at the cost of not exploiting any of
that DAG's structure.

This mirrors, but does not match, the classical result for pull-based
work-stealing: a work-conserving scheduler on an arbitrary DAG achieves
$\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}^{\mathrm{WS}}] = T_1/N + O(T_\infty)$, where
$T_\infty$ is the critical-path \emph{time} (not node count), via a
potential-function argument tracking how many "ready" nodes are
available to steal at each
step~\cite{blumofe1999,arora2001}. Theorem~\ref{thm:general_dag}'s
$d^*\cdot W_{\max}$ term is structurally different: it charges a fixed,
scheduler-wide worst-case admission delay at \emph{every} critical-path
node, rather than a term that shrinks as the DAG gets wider (more
parallelism available) the way $O(T_\infty)$ does. The two bounds are
not directly comparable in general, but $d^* \cdot W_{\max}$ does not
reduce to $O(T_\infty)$ as stated, and we do not claim it does.
\end{remark}

\begin{openq}[Tight general-DAG bound for DTA]
\label{open:general_dag}
Does a bound of the form $T_1/N + O(T_\infty)$ --- matching
work-stealing's classical tightness, with $T_\infty$ the critical-path
\emph{time} rather than Theorem~\ref{thm:general_dag}'s node-counted
$d^*\cdot W_{\max}$ --- hold for DTA on arbitrary DAGs? The
potential-function technique used for work-stealing's bound is built
around the \emph{pull} side of stealing: an idle worker's steal
attempt directly targets whichever victim currently has the most
"ready" work, so the potential can track total ready-work directly.
DTA's push-based, hop-bounded deflection has no equivalent mechanism:
a producer routes a newly-ready task via a fixed hash-derived target
(Proposition~\ref{prop:hash_mf}) chosen before dispatch, with no
visibility into which peer is currently most starved for work. Establishing a
$T_\infty$-tight bound for DTA, if one holds at all, likely requires a
genuinely different potential function --- one built from queue-depth
imbalance (\hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I}) rather than
steal-target availability --- or may not hold at all for a strictly
push-based, no-visibility router, in which case a matching
\emph{lower} bound separating DTA from WS on adversarial DAG instances
would itself be a meaningful result.
\end{openq}

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Connection to Directed Percolation: Open Problems}
% ------------------------------------------------------------

The mean-field kinetic equation~\eqref{eq:dag_ode} describes scheduling
on the \emph{random DAG ensemble}; for a general (non-random) DAG,
the appropriate framework is the theory of
\emph{directed percolation} (DP)~\cite{hinrichsen2000}.

Consider a random DAG where each directed edge from level $\ell{-}1$
to level $\ell$ exists independently with probability $p$.
As $p$ decreases from $1$ (fully connected) to $0$ (empty), the
typical critical-path length $T_\infty$ undergoes a phase transition
at a threshold $p_c$:
\begin{itemize}
  \item For $p > p_c$ (dense): $T_\infty = O(d)$; scheduling is
        critical-path-bound.
  \item For $p < p_c$ (sparse): $T_\infty = O(\log d)$; scheduling is
        work-bound ($\approx T_1/N$).
  \item At $p = p_c$: universal scaling with DP critical exponents.
\end{itemize}
The scheduling order parameter
$\Phi = (C_{\max} - T_1/N)/(T_\infty - T_1/N)$
(fraction of makespan attributable to the critical path)
scales near $p_c$ as~\cite{hinrichsen2000}
\[
  \Phi \;\sim\; |p - p_c|^{\beta},
  \qquad
  \beta \approx 0.2765 \quad (d=1\text{ space} + 1\text{ time}).
\]
The temporal and spatial correlation lengths diverge as
$\xi_\parallel \sim |p-p_c|^{-\nu_\parallel}$ with $\nu_\parallel \approx 1.734$
and $\xi_\perp \sim |p-p_c|^{-\nu_\perp}$ with $\nu_\perp \approx 1.097$.
The dynamic exponent is $z = \nu_\parallel/\nu_\perp \approx 1.581$.

The Level-0 BBGKY closure~\eqref{eq:dag_ode} corresponds to
\emph{mean-field DP} (valid for $d \geq d_c = 4$ spatial dimensions).
In the physically relevant case $d = 1+1$ (one spatial dimension = the
worker ring, one temporal dimension = DAG depth), the mean-field exponents
are corrected by fluctuations; their values are known numerically to high
precision~\cite{hinrichsen2000} but a rigorous analytical derivation for
the scheduling model remains an open problem.

\begin{openq}[DP universality for DAG scheduling]
Establish rigorously that, in the random DAG ensemble with edge probability
$p$ near $p_c$, the scheduling phase transition is in the DP universality
class and the makespan scaling exponents equal the DP critical exponents
$\beta, \nu_\parallel, \nu_\perp$.
\end{openq}

\begin{openq}[BBGKY Level-2 for DAG kinetics]
Derive the Level-2 BBGKY correction to the ODE system~\eqref{eq:dag_ode},
analogous to the Level-1 correction in
\hyperref[chap:kinetic_theory]{Performance~II} (Section~3.2).
The Level-2 equation introduces triplet correlators
$g_3^{(\ell)}(v,v',v'')$ and is expected to yield the first
fluctuation correction to the DP mean-field exponents.
\end{openq}

\begin{remark}[Partially resolved]
\S\ref{sec:dag-level2} derives the leading-order joint-\emph{eligibility}
correlator $\delta_\ell(t)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:dag-overlap-correction})
in closed form, to the order stated there. This is a \emph{pair}-correlator
result (BBGKY level~1 in the hierarchy sense, the DAG analogue of
$g_2$), one level short of the full joint-\emph{activation} triplet
correlator $g_3^{(\ell)}$ this problem asks for --- reaching that would
require propagating $\delta_\ell(t)$ through the $\mu$-rate activation
delay via a coupled inhomogeneous Markov chain, and separately
extending the pair result to three tasks, neither carried out here.
This problem, as stated (a genuine level-2/triplet closure yielding
the first fluctuation correction to the DP mean-field exponents),
remains open; \S\ref{sec:dag-level2} narrows it by supplying the
level-1 building block such a closure would need.
\end{remark}

% ========================================================================
% Adversarial Stability Comparison
% ========================================================================

\chapter{Adversarial Stability Comparison}
\label{chap:stability_compare}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Overview.}
This chapter benchmarks DTA against a centralised queue baseline and
work-stealing across the four stability metrics of Performance~IV.
Empirical validation against the real, running scheduler is deferred
to \hyperref[chap:empirical_validation]{Empirical Validation}, after
the information-theoretic cost framework of Part~VI --- its numbers
are read most usefully with that framework already in hand.

\textbf{Notation.}
$N$ workers, load $\varrho_0 = \lambda_0/\mu$, self-consistent load $\varrho^*$,
per-worker capacity $H$, mean queue $\bar q$, warehouse capacity $C_W$
(DTA only).
Four strategies compared: \textbf{DTA} (hop-bounded deflection, this work),
\textbf{CQ} (Central Queue, M/M/N model), \textbf{Rand} (random assignment,
no rebalancing), \textbf{WS} (Work-Stealing with Chase--Lev deque).
spectral gap, burst capacity, first-passage crash time).

% ===========================================================================
\section{Strategies Under Comparison}
% ===========================================================================

Each strategy is a complete scheduling system, not just an assignment rule.
We characterise each by its steady-state queue model.

\begin{definition}[Strategy queue models]~
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{DTA:}
        $N$ workers, each an M/M/1/$H$ queue under mean-field deflection.
        Effective per-worker load $\varrho^* = \varrho_0(1 + p_d)$
        (self-consistent equation, \hyperref[chap:formal_model]{the Formal Model}).
        Warehouse buffer $C_W$ for overflow.  Hop limit $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor$.

  \item \textbf{Central Queue (CQ):}
        Single global FIFO, $N$ servers.  The classical M/M/$N$ model with
        Erlang-C steady state.  Tasks see mean wait
        $W_{\mathrm{CQ}} = C(N,\varrho_0) / (N\mu(1-\varrho_0/N))$,
        where $C(N,\varrho_0)$ is the Erlang-C formula.
        No per-worker queue capacity; no overflow buffer needed.
        \emph{Requires a single point of enqueue coordination.}

  \item \textbf{Random (Rand):}
        Each arriving task is assigned uniformly at random to one of $N$ workers,
        independently.  Per-worker load $\varrho_0$ (no rebalancing).
        Each worker is an independent M/M/1 queue, stable iff $\varrho_0 < 1$.

  \item \textbf{Work-Stealing (WS):}
        Tasks pushed to local deque; idle workers steal.  Equivalent to
        M/M/$N$ in steady-state throughput for BoT, but with different
        transient dynamics and per-operation overhead.  No finite queue capacity
        ($H = \infty$ effectively); no warehouse buffer.
        \emph{Requires atomic CAS on shared deque pointers.}
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

% ===========================================================================
\section{Steady-State Queue Comparison}
% ===========================================================================

\begin{proposition}[Mean queue comparison]\label{prop:mean_queue}
At offered load $\varrho_0 < 1$ with $N$ workers:
\begin{align}
  \bar q_{\mathrm{DTA}} &= \frac{\varrho^*}{1-\varrho^*}
     \cdot \frac{1 - (\varrho^*)^H (H(1-\varrho^*)+1)}{1 - (\varrho^*)^{H+1}}
     \;\le\; \frac{\varrho^*}{1-\varrho^*}, \label{eq:q_dta}\\
  \bar q_{\mathrm{CQ}} &= \frac{\varrho_0/N}{1 - \varrho_0/N}
     \;+\; \frac{C(N,\varrho_0)\,\varrho_0/N}{N(1-\varrho_0/N)^2 \cdot N!}
     \;\approx\; \frac{\varrho_0}{N - \varrho_0}\quad(N \text{ large}), \label{eq:q_cq}\\
  \bar q_{\mathrm{Rand}} &= \frac{\varrho_0}{1 - \varrho_0}, \label{eq:q_rand}\\
  \bar q_{\mathrm{WS}} &= \frac{\varrho_0/N}{1-\varrho_0/N}
     \;\approx\; \frac{\varrho_0}{N - \varrho_0}
     \quad(\text{BoT, steady state}). \label{eq:q_ws}
\end{align}
Hence $\bar q_{\mathrm{CQ}} \approx \bar q_{\mathrm{WS}} \le \bar q_{\mathrm{DTA}} \le \bar q_{\mathrm{Rand}}$,
with DTA converging to CQ/WS as $\varrho_0 \to 1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
CQ and WS achieve global load balancing, giving effective per-worker load
$\varrho_0/N \ll \varrho^*$.  DTA's mean-field self-consistent load
$\varrho^* > \varrho_0/N$ but $< \varrho_0$ (deflection partially balances).
Rand has no rebalancing; per-worker load = $\varrho_0$.  The ordering follows
from the M/M/1 formula $\bar q = \varrho/(1-\varrho)$ applied to each regime.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Physical reading]
The mean queue ordering reflects
\physics{equilibration efficiency}{how well each strategy spreads load}.
CQ and WS achieve \physics{global thermodynamic equilibrium}
{perfect load balance across all workers}.
DTA achieves \physics{local equilibrium}{mean-field: each worker
statistically equivalent} with an $O(1/N)$ correction from pair correlations
(see \hyperref[chap:kinetic_theory]{Performance~II}, Level~1).
Rand is a \physics{non-interacting system}{no load balancing at all};
individual queues are i.i.d.\ and the system cannot exploit diversity.
\end{remark}

% ===========================================================================

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figure/burst_capacity_compare.png}
\caption{Burst capacity $B^*$ of DTA (base and with warehouse), CQ, Random, and Work-Stealing strategies versus offered load $\varrho_0$. DTA with warehouse uniquely exceeds CQ.}
\label{fig:burst_compare}
\end{figure}
\section{Burst Capacity $B^*$}
% ===========================================================================

\begin{definition}[Burst capacity]\label{def:burst}
For a strategy $\mathcal{S}$, the \emph{burst capacity} $B^*(\mathcal{S})$
is the maximum number of additional tasks that can be injected above the
steady-state arrival rate before the system saturates (any worker's queue
hits capacity, or the system becomes unstable).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Burst capacities]\label{prop:burst_compare}
At steady-state load $\varrho_0$ with per-worker capacity $L$ (where applicable):
\begin{align}
  B^*_{\mathrm{DTA}} &= N(L - \bar q_{\mathrm{DTA}}) + C_W \cdot K
     \;=\; N(L - \varrho^*/(1-\varrho^*)) + C_W \cdot K, \label{eq:B_dta}\\
  B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}} &= N \cdot L - \varrho_0/\mu \quad (\text{uniform buffer of } L \text{ per server}),
     \label{eq:B_cq}\\
  B^*_{\mathrm{Rand}} &= N(L - \varrho_0/(1-\varrho_0)), \label{eq:B_rand}\\
  B^*_{\mathrm{WS}} &\approx B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}} \quad (\text{no per-worker cap;  steals
     prevent local saturation}). \label{eq:B_ws}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
DTA: Each worker has $L - \bar q$ tasks of remaining capacity in steady state;
warehouse adds $C_W \cdot K$ overflow slots.  Sum over $N$ workers (Theorem~3.1
of \hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV}).  CQ: global buffer of $NL$ minus expected occupancy
$\varrho_0/\mu$ (Little's law).  Rand: per-worker M/M/1 queue with mean $\varrho_0/(1-\varrho_0)$;
same structure as DTA without deflection (no warehouse).  WS: effectively
infinite deque; burst capacity = total RAM capacity minus current load.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Comparison at fixed $\varrho_0$]\label{cor:burst_compare}
Define $B^*_{\mathrm{DTA,base}} = N(L - \bar q_{\mathrm{DTA}})$ (DTA without
the warehouse contribution).  Since $\varrho^*/(1-\varrho^*) \le \varrho_0/(1-\varrho_0)$,
we have $\bar q_{\mathrm{DTA}} \approx \bar q_{\mathrm{Rand}}$ (deflection has
exponentially small effect for large $H$), giving the \emph{base ordering}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:burst_order_base}
  B^*_{\mathrm{Rand}} \;\lesssim\; B^*_{\mathrm{DTA,base}} \;\le\; B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}} \approx B^*_{\mathrm{WS}}.
\end{equation}
However, DTA's \emph{total} burst capacity includes the warehouse bonus:
\[
  B^*_{\mathrm{DTA,total}} = B^*_{\mathrm{DTA,base}} + C_W \cdot K
  \;\ge\; B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}} \quad \text{whenever } C_W K \ge B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}} - B^*_{\mathrm{DTA,base}}.
\]
The warehouse is a unique architectural advantage of DTA unavailable in CQ or WS.
Numerically (Table~1 of \texttt{simulate\_competitive.py}): at $\varrho_0 = 0.5$, $N=16$,
$B^*_{\mathrm{DTA,total}} = 2244 > B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}} = 1760$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Physical analogy: network conductance]
The ordering in Equation~\eqref{eq:burst_order_base} mirrors the
\physics{network conductance hierarchy}{ability to redistribute load under stress}.
CQ/WS = fully connected network (any task can reach any server instantly);
DTA = ring network with bounded hop radius (short-range conductance);
Rand = disconnected islands (no conductance between workers).

The hop bound $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor$ in DTA is analogous to the
\physics{Cheeger constant of a ring graph}{connectivity bottleneck}~\cite{cheeger1970}.
For a ring of $N$ nodes with diameter $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor$, the Cheeger
constant scales as $\Phi \sim 2/N$, which by the Cheeger inequality
$\gamma \ge \Phi^2/2$ gives spectral gap $\gamma \ge 2/N^2$.
CQ has $\Phi = 1$ (complete graph); Rand has $\Phi = 0$ (no edges).
\end{remark}

% ===========================================================================

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figure/spectral_gap_compare.png}
\caption{Spectral gap $\gamma$ comparison across strategies. DTA exhibits slower mixing ($\gamma_{\mathrm{DTA}} < \gamma_{\mathrm{CQ}}$) but compensates via the warehouse burst buffer.}
\label{fig:spec_compare}
\end{figure}
\section{Spectral Gap and Mixing Time}
% ===========================================================================

\begin{definition}[Per-strategy spectral gap]
Let $\gamma(\mathcal{S})$ denote the spectral gap of the Markov chain
describing the queue-length process under strategy $\mathcal{S}$,
and $t_{\mathrm{mix}}(\mathcal{S}) = \log(1/\varepsilon)/\gamma(\mathcal{S})$
the $\varepsilon$-mixing time.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Mixing time comparison]\label{thm:mixing_compare}
At load $\varrho_0 < 1$ with $\mu = 1$:
\begin{align}
  \gamma_{\mathrm{DTA}} &\approx \mu(1 - \sqrt{\varrho^*})^2
     \quad(H \gg 1), \label{eq:gap_dta}\\
  \gamma_{\mathrm{CQ}} &= \mu(1 - \varrho_0/N)
     \quad(\text{M/M/}N \text{ birth-death, leading eigenvalue}), \label{eq:gap_cq}\\
  \gamma_{\mathrm{Rand}} &= \mu(1 - \varrho_0)
     \quad(\text{M/M/1, standard result}), \label{eq:gap_rand}\\
  \gamma_{\mathrm{WS}} &\approx \gamma_{\mathrm{CQ}}
     \quad(\text{BoT: equivalent to M/M/}N \text{ in steady state}). \label{eq:gap_ws}
\end{align}
Consequently, the mixing-time ordering is:
\[
  t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{\mathrm{CQ}} \;\approx\; t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{\mathrm{WS}}
  \;\le\; t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{\mathrm{DTA}}
  \;\le\; t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{\mathrm{Rand}},
\]
with equality approached as $N \to \infty$ (DTA) and $\varrho_0 \to 1$ (all strategies diverge).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:gap_dta}: from \hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV}, Theorem~2.1 (spectral
gap of M/M/1/$H$ mean-field chain, large-$H$ approximation).
Equation~\eqref{eq:gap_cq}: the M/M/$N$ generator on total occupancy $Q$
has birth rate $\lambda_0$ and death rate $\min(Q,N)\mu$; the spectral gap
of this birth-death chain is $\mu - \lambda_0/N = \mu(1-\varrho_0/N)$
(see \cite{fill1991}).
Equation~\eqref{eq:gap_rand}: each M/M/1 chain has gap $\mu(1-\varrho_0)$.
The mixing time for the $N$-dimensional product chain is the max of individual gaps.
The ordering follows from $\varrho^* > \varrho_0/N$ (DTA per-worker load higher
than CQ), so $\sqrt{\varrho^*} > \sqrt{\varrho_0/N}$ and $\gamma_{\mathrm{DTA}} < \gamma_{\mathrm{CQ}}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Critical slowing-down near saturation]
All strategies exhibit critical slowing-down as $\varrho_0 \to 1$:
\[
  t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{\mathcal{S}} \;\sim\; \begin{cases}
    (1 - \varrho_0/N)^{-1} & \mathcal{S} \in \{\mathrm{CQ, WS}\} \\
    (1 - \sqrt{\varrho^*})^{-2} & \mathcal{S} = \mathrm{DTA} \\
    (1 - \varrho_0)^{-1} & \mathcal{S} = \mathrm{Rand}
  \end{cases}
  \quad \text{as } \varrho_0 \to 1.
\]
The exponent $-2$ for DTA (vs. $-1$ for CQ) is the
\physics{critical slowing-down exponent}{mixing time power law near saturation}.
This is the signature of the NESS (non-equilibrium steady state) nature
of DTA: deflection violates detailed balance, introducing an extra
factor of $(1-\sqrt{\varrho^*})^{-1}$ relative to CQ.
\end{corollary}

% ===========================================================================

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figure/crash_time_compare.png}
\caption{First-passage crash time $T_{\mathrm{crash}}$ versus overload factor $\varrho_0$ for DTA and CQ. DTA maintains a finite crash time only when the warehouse is sufficiently large.}
\label{fig:crash_compare}
\end{figure}
\section{Adversarial Stability: First-Passage Crash Time}
% ===========================================================================

\begin{definition}[Crash time under adversarial injection]
An adversary injects tasks at rate $\lambda_0 + \epsilon$ for $\epsilon > 0$
starting from steady state.  The \emph{crash time} $T_{\mathrm{crash}}$ is
the first time any worker's queue (or warehouse) saturates.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Crash time comparison]\label{thm:crash_compare}
Under adversarial injection at rate $\lambda_0 + \epsilon$:
\begin{align}
  T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{DTA}} &= \frac{C_W}{\Lambda_W(\varrho_0+\epsilon/\mu) - \Lambda_D(\varrho_0)}
     \quad (\Lambda_W > \Lambda_D, \text{ Theorem~4.3 of \hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV}}),
     \label{eq:T_dta}\\
  T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{CQ}} &= \frac{N \cdot L - \varrho_0/\mu}{\epsilon/\mu}
     \quad (\text{linear drain of global buffer}), \label{eq:T_cq}\\
  T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{Rand}} &= \frac{L - \varrho_0/(1-\varrho_0)}{\epsilon/\mu}
     \cdot \frac{1}{N} \quad (\text{each worker absorbs } 1/N \text{ of excess}),
     \label{eq:T_rand}\\
  T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{WS}} &\approx T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{CQ}}
     \quad (\text{deque unbounded; crash occurs at memory limit}).
     \label{eq:T_ws}
\end{align}
Hence the ordering $T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{Rand}} \le T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{DTA}}
\le T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{CQ}} \approx T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{WS}}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
DTA: from \hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV}, Theorem~4.3.  The warehouse fills at rate
$\Lambda_W - \Lambda_D > 0$ when $\varrho_0 + \epsilon/\mu > \varrho_0^\dagger$
(L4 threshold); crash time $= C_W / (\Lambda_W - \Lambda_D)$.
CQ: the global buffer of $NL$ tasks absorbs the excess $\epsilon$ rate;
time to fill = remaining capacity / excess rate.
Rand: each worker independently absorbs $\epsilon/N$ of excess;
crash time is determined by the \emph{first} worker to saturate, which is
$N$ times slower than CQ (no cross-worker load transfer).
WS: equivalent to CQ for BoT since steals prevent local saturation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Architectural trade-off]
CQ achieves longer crash time $T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{CQ}} = N \cdot T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{Rand}}$
but at the cost of a \textbf{single point of failure}: the central queue.
DTA sacrifices a factor of $\approx \Lambda_W/\epsilon$ in crash time
but gains \textbf{fault tolerance} (no single queue, warehouse as safety valve)
and \textbf{lower coordination overhead} (SPSC vs.\ global lock).
WS achieves CQ-level crash time but requires \textbf{shared memory}
(CAS operations), making it unsuitable for distributed-memory environments.
\end{remark}

% ===========================================================================
\section{Cheeger Conductance: Physical Synthesis}
\label{sec:cheeger}
% ===========================================================================

The spectral gap $\gamma$ of a reversible Markov chain is bounded below by
the \emph{Cheeger constant} $\Phi$ via the Cheeger inequality:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cheeger}
  \frac{\Phi^2}{2} \;\le\; \gamma \;\le\; 2\Phi.
\end{equation}

In the context of load-balancing strategies, $\Phi$ measures the
\physics{conductance of the load-redistribution graph}{ability to move tasks between workers}:

\begin{proposition}[Conductance ordering]\label{prop:conductance}
Let $G_{\mathcal{S}}$ be the effective task-routing graph for strategy $\mathcal{S}$.
The Cheeger constants satisfy:
\[
  \Phi(\mathrm{Rand}) = 0 \;\le\; \Phi(\mathrm{DTA}) \;\le\; \Phi(\mathrm{CQ}) = \Phi(\mathrm{WS}) = 1.
\]
For DTA on a ring of $N$ workers with hop bound $h = \lfloor N/2 \rfloor$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cheeger_dta}
  \Phi(\mathrm{DTA}) \;=\; \frac{\min\text{-cut across ring}}{\text{volume of smaller partition}}
  \;\approx\; \frac{2}{N} \cdot p_d(1-p_d),
\end{equation}
where the factor $p_d(1-p_d)$ reflects that only deflected tasks contribute
to cross-worker flow.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
Rand has no edges ($G_{\mathrm{Rand}}$ = isolated nodes); $\Phi = 0$.
CQ and WS have the complete bipartite structure (any task reachable from any worker);
$\Phi = 1$ (connected, min-cut = 1 for any balanced partition).
DTA: the routing graph is a ring; balanced cut at the ring's diameter removes
2 edges.  The volume of each half is $N/2$ workers.  Flow through the cut
= rate of deflected tasks crossing the cut = $\lambda_0 p_d \cdot$ (fraction of ring
covered by hop bound).  For $h = N/2$, each task can reach the diametrically
opposite worker, giving $\Phi \approx 2p_d/N$ up to constants.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Equation~\eqref{eq:cheeger_dta} explains why DTA's spectral gap
$\gamma_{\mathrm{DTA}} \approx \mu(1-\sqrt{\varrho^*})^2$ is smaller than
$\gamma_{\mathrm{CQ}} = \mu(1-\varrho_0/N)$: the ring topology's bounded conductance
limits the speed of global load equilibration.  However, this \emph{locality}
also prevents the \physics{Braess paradox}{pathological degradation when adding
capacity in interconnected networks}: in fully-connected WS/CQ systems,
adding workers can paradoxically increase contention for the shared queue.
DTA's hop-bounded topology is immune to this failure mode.
\end{remark}

% ===========================================================================
\section{Summary Table}
% ===========================================================================

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lXXXX@{}}
\toprule
Metric & DTA & Central Queue & Random & Work-Stealing \\
\midrule
Mean queue $\bar q$
  & $\varrho^*/(1-\varrho^*)$
  & $\varrho_0/(N-\varrho_0)$
  & $\varrho_0/(1-\varrho_0)$
  & $\approx \bar q_{\mathrm{CQ}}$ \\
Burst capacity $B^*$
  & $N(L-\bar q)+C_W K$
  & $NL - \varrho_0/\mu$
  & $N(L-\bar q_{\mathrm{Rand}})$
  & $\approx B^*_{\mathrm{CQ}}$ \\
Spectral gap $\gamma$
  & $\mu(1-\sqrt{\varrho^*})^2$
  & $\mu(1-\varrho_0/N)$
  & $\mu(1-\varrho_0)$
  & $\approx \gamma_{\mathrm{CQ}}$ \\
Mixing time $t_{\mathrm{mix}}$
  & $\sim(1-\varrho^*)^{-2}$
  & $\sim(1-\varrho_0/N)^{-1}$
  & $\sim(1-\varrho_0)^{-1}$
  & $\approx t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{\mathrm{CQ}}$ \\
Crash time $T_{\mathrm{crash}}$
  & $C_W/(\Lambda_W-\Lambda_D)$
  & $N(L-\bar q_{\mathrm{CQ}})/\epsilon$
  & $(L-\bar q_{\mathrm{Rand}})/(N\epsilon)$
  & $\approx T_{\mathrm{crash}}^{\mathrm{CQ}}$ \\
Conductance $\Phi$
  & $\approx 2p_d/N$
  & $1$
  & $0$
  & $1$ \\
Fault tolerance
  & \textbf{Yes} (no SPOF)
  & No (single queue)
  & Yes
  & No (shared mem.) \\
DAG tasks
  & Not natively
  & No
  & No
  & \textbf{Yes} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\noindent
\textbf{Key takeaway.}
DTA occupies the middle of the performance spectrum: worse than
CQ/WS on pure steady-state metrics (mean queue, spectral gap, crash time),
but providing fault tolerance and distributed operation unavailable in CQ/WS.
It strictly dominates Rand on all metrics.  The architectural choice of
DTA is justified when (i) the deployment environment is distributed
(no shared memory), (ii) the task topology is BoT or near-BoT,
and (iii) load is heavy ($\varrho_0 \to 1$), where DTA's mean-field
optimality (Corollary~3.3 of \hyperref[chap:competitive_ratio]{the Competitive Analysis}) closes the
gap with CQ.


% ============================================================
\partbreak
\mypart{Information-Theoretic Analysis of Scheduler Cost}

% ============================================================
\chapter{Information-Theoretic Analysis}
\label{chap:info_theory}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Overview.}
The preceding parts establish that DTA's mean-field steady state, mixing
time, and adversarial makespan bound are broadly comparable to those of
Work Stealing (WS).  This chapter asks a sharper question: \emph{if the two
schedulers are this close on makespan, why does DTA still hold a
measurable information-acquisition-cost advantage over WS
(\hyperref[chap:empirical_validation]{Empirical Validation})?}
We argue that the conventional competitive-ratio criterion conflates two
orthogonal quantities --- how much a scheduler \emph{knows} about the
system, and how much it \emph{pays} to know it --- and introduce an
information-theoretic framework that separates them.  We prove that both
of these quantities tend to their best possible value for DTA as total
work $W \to \infty$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:dta_info_opt} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:dta_info_gap}): DTA achieves the best possible
makespan given its information set, \emph{and} the cost of that
restricted information set itself vanishes relative to the fully-informed
oracle in the heavy-traffic limit. This asymptotic equivalence does
\emph{not} explain the throughput gap observed at moderate, fixed task
counts, so we turn to a second, non-asymptotic quantity: the
information-\emph{acquisition} cost paid per unit time. We show that on a
NUMA topology this acquisition cost is $\Omega(\log N)$ times lower for
DTA than for WS, at any fixed system scale. On a representative
dual-socket system this cost advantage, not the asymptotic makespan
comparison, explains the observed throughput gap at moderate to large
task counts.

% ============================================================
\section{Scheduler Information Model}
\label{sec:info_model}
% ============================================================

\subsection{Information sets and staleness}

A \emph{distributed scheduler} on worker set $G$ assigns tasks to workers
based on locally observable quantities.  We formalise what each scheduler
can observe.

\begin{definition}[Information propagation delay]\label{def:tau}
Let $\tau_{gh} \geq 0$ be the time for a state update at worker $h$ to be
observable by worker $g$ (e.g.\ a cache-coherence propagation latency
or an SPSC round-trip).  Define the \emph{information cone} of worker $g$
at time $t$ as
\[
  \mathcal{L}^-(g,t)
  \;=\;
  \bigl\{(h,s) : s \leq t - \tau_{gh}\bigr\},
\]
the set of worker--time pairs whose state can influence $g$'s decision at $t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Scheduler information set]\label{def:info_set}
The \emph{information set} of scheduler $\pi$ at time $t$ is
\[
  \mathcal{I}_\pi(t)
  \;=\;
  \bigl\{\sigma_h(s) : (h,s) \in \mathcal{L}^-(g,t),\;
         \text{and } (h,s) \text{ was actively observed by } \pi\bigr\}.
\]
Scheduling decisions of $\pi$ must be measurable with respect to
$\mathcal{I}_\pi(t)$.
\end{definition}

For the two schedulers studied here:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{DTA.} Worker $g$ observes its own queue length $q_g(t)$
        continuously, and receives asynchronous SPSC notifications from
        its designated peers with delay $\tau \leq \delta_{\mathrm{inter}}/v$
        (where $v$ is the bus bandwidth).
        Formally, $\mathcal{I}_g^{\mathrm{DTA}}(t)
        = \{q_g(t)\} \cup \{\text{SPSC messages received up to }t\}$.

  \item \textbf{WS.} Worker $g$ observes $q_g(t)$ and, upon each steal
        attempt, reads the tail pointer of victim $h$'s deque with latency
        $\tau_{gh}$.  Between steals, $h$'s state is unobserved.
        Formally, $\mathcal{I}_g^{\mathrm{WS}}(t)
        = \{q_g(t)\} \cup \{q_h(t_{\mathrm{steal}}) : h \text{ stolen from at } t_{\mathrm{steal}} \leq t\}$.
\end{itemize}

The key structural difference: DTA receives a \emph{continuous, low-cost
stream} of local neighbourhood information; WS acquires \emph{point
observations} of arbitrary workers at the price of a CAS operation per
observation.

\subsection{Causal ordering and contention}

Two concurrent observations of the same memory location create a
\emph{causal collision}: their serialisation order is determined by the
memory subsystem, not the scheduler.  WS steal attempts can collide when
multiple workers target the same victim; DTA's SPSC channels are
\emph{causally ordered by construction} (single producer, single consumer),
so no collision is possible.  This distinction drives the cost analysis
in \S\ref{sec:info_cost}.

% ============================================================
\section{Information-Constrained Optimality}
\label{sec:info_optimality}
% ============================================================

\subsection{Constrained optimum and decomposition}

\begin{definition}[Information-constrained makespan]\label{def:Cstar}
For a scheduler $\pi$ with information set $\mathcal{I}_\pi$, the
\emph{information-constrained optimal makespan} is
\[
  C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)
  \;=\;
  \inf_{\tilde\pi\,\text{measurable w.r.t.}\,\mathcal{I}_\pi}
  \mathbb{E}\bigl[C_{\max}(\tilde\pi)\bigr].
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Two-factor decomposition]\label{thm:decomp}
For any scheduler $\pi$:
\[
  \frac{\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\pi)]}{C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}}}
  \;=\;
  \underbrace{\frac{C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)}{C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}}}}_{\displaystyle\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\pi)}\;
  \times\;
  \underbrace{\frac{\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\pi)]}{C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)}}_{\displaystyle\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\pi)},
\]
where $C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}}$ is the offline-optimal makespan.
We call $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\pi) \geq 1$ the \emph{information gap} (structural,
cannot be improved without enlarging $\mathcal{I}_\pi$) and
$\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\pi) \geq 1$ the \emph{utilisation ratio} (algorithmic,
measures how well $\pi$ exploits its information).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By definition, $C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi) \leq \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\pi)]$
and $C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}} \leq C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)$.
The identity follows from telescoping.
\end{proof}

The decomposition shifts the question from
``how close is $\pi$ to the oracle?''\ to
``(a) how much does $\pi$'s limited information cost it, and
(b) does $\pi$ make optimal use of what it knows?''
Both DTA and WS are judged on criterion (b) alone in \S\ref{sec:info_optimality_proof};
criterion (a) is analysed in \S\ref{sec:numa_concrete}.

\subsection{DTA is information-optimal}
\label{sec:info_optimality_proof}

We prove $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA}) \to 1$ under mild assumptions.

\begin{assumption}\label{ass:mmp}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\textup{A\arabic*})]
  \item\label{ass:ergodic}
        \textbf{[Ergodic workload.]}
        The arrival process is stationary ergodic with finite rate
        $\lambda$ and finite second moment; service times are i.i.d.\
        with finite mean $1/\mu$ and finite variance $\sigma^2 < \infty$;
        load $\rho = \lambda/(N\mu) < 1$.  (The M/M/1 model is a special
        case; the key property used is ergodicity and $\rho < 1$.)

  \item\label{ass:threshold}
        \textbf{[Threshold structure.]}
        For each worker $g$, the cost-optimal local admission policy
        --- acting only on $q_g$ --- is a monotone threshold policy:
        accept if $q_g \leq H_g^*$, deflect otherwise.
        This holds for \emph{any} GI/GI/1/$H$ queue with a convex,
        non-decreasing running cost (e.g.\ sojourn time); see
        Stidham~\cite{stidham1985} for M/M/1/$H$ and the extension via
        convexity of the value function.
        DTA's admission threshold is $H$, the per-worker local-queue
        high-water mark of \hyperref[chap:formal_model]{the Formal Model}
        (\emph{not} to be confused with the hop-count bound $h_{\max} =
        \lfloor N/2\rfloor$ of Definition~\ref{def:workers}, which
        governs how many times an already-deflected chunk may be
        relayed, not when a worker starts deflecting); the assumption
        reduces to $H \geq
        H_g^*$, which for the M/M/1/$H$ case ($H^* =
        \lceil\rho/(1-\rho)\rceil$) is equivalent to $\rho \leq H/(H+1)$.
        Since DTA's $H$ is large in every configuration used in this
        paper (production $H = 114{,}688$; benchmark/simulation
        $H \in [100, 200]$; see \S\ref{sec:validity}), this holds for
        $\rho$ up to at least $0.99$ even at the smallest simulated $H$,
        comfortably covering every load $\varrho_0 \in [0.6, 0.95]$ used
        in the benchmarks elsewhere in this paper --- independent of
        $N$.

  \item\label{ass:bounded_tau}
        \textbf{[Bounded mean staleness.]}
        Information staleness satisfies
        $\mathbb{E}[\tau_{gh}] \leq \bar\tau < \infty$ for all $g,h \in G$.
        (This is weaker than requiring a.s.\ bounded staleness;
        it holds whenever the observation rate is bounded below by
        $1/\bar\tau$.)
\end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

\begin{remark}[On Assumption~\ref{ass:threshold} and DTA's hop bound]
We stress that Assumption~\ref{ass:threshold} constrains the
\emph{admission} threshold $H$ (a queue-depth quantity, in the hundreds
to hundreds-of-thousands), not the \emph{hop-count} bound $h_{\max} =
\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ (a dimensionless integer, at most $N/2$). The
condition required, $\rho \leq H/(H+1)$, is on the \emph{system load}
$\rho$ relative to the admission threshold $H$, and is essentially
independent of $N$: since $H \geq 100$ in every configuration this
paper considers, the condition holds up to $\rho \leq 0.99$ regardless
of $N$, so Assumption~\ref{ass:threshold} is satisfied at every load
and worker count used elsewhere in this paper, including the $N=4$ CI
benchmark configuration.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[DTA utilisation efficiency]\label{thm:dta_info_opt}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:mmp}, for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists
$W_0$ such that for all total work $W \geq W_0$:
\[
  \eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA})
  \;=\;
  \frac{\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\mathrm{DTA})]}{C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}})}
  \;\leq\; 1 + \varepsilon.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We decompose the argument into three steps.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~1 (Local threshold optimality).}
Model the routing decision at each arrival epoch as a
\emph{partially observed Markov decision process} (POMDP):
observable state $q_g \in \{0,\ldots,H\}$, action space
$\{\mathrm{accept}, \mathrm{deflect}\}$, instantaneous cost
$c(q) = q$ (sojourn contribution, by Little's law~\cite{little1961}).
The function $c$ is convex and non-decreasing.
For any stable single-server queue with convex running cost observed
through its queue length alone, the optimal policy is a
\emph{threshold policy}~\cite{stidham1985}: accept iff $q \leq H^*$.
By Assumption~\ref{ass:threshold}, DTA sets $H \geq H^*$, so its
threshold-$H$ policy achieves the minimum expected cost within
$\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dta_local_opt}
  \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\mathrm{DTA}) \mid \mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}]
  \;=\;
  C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}) \;+\; \Delta_{\mathrm{mf}},
\end{equation}
where $\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}$ is the mean-field approximation error.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~2 (Mean-field error vanishes).}
The quantity $\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}$ in~\eqref{eq:dta_local_opt} is a gap
between two \emph{expected} costs (since $c(q)=q$ is linear, by Little's
law this is exactly a gap between mean queue lengths), so what is needed
here is convergence of the \emph{mean}, not the full distribution.
Proposition~\ref{prop:1overN} gives exactly this: the level-1-corrected
effective traffic intensity satisfies
$\rho^{(2)} = \rhostar_{\mathrm{MF}} + O(1/N)$. Since
$\rho \mapsto \rho/(1-\rho)$ is Lipschitz on any interval bounded away
from $1$ (with constant $(1-\rhostar_{\mathrm{MF}})^{-2}$, finite
whenever the system is not at the saturation boundary), the corrected
mean queue length satisfies
$\bar q^{(2)} = \bar q_{\mathrm{MF}} + O(1/N)$, and by
Assumption~\ref{ass:ergodic} (ergodicity) this $O(1/N)$ mean-field gap
is exactly the leading-order contribution to $\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}$ as
$N\to\infty$. By Assumption~\ref{ass:bounded_tau}, information staleness introduces
an additive bias $O(\bar\tau \cdot \lambda)$ to the effective arrival
rate seen by each worker, which is $o(1)$ for $\bar\tau \lambda \to 0$.
Combining this with the Gumbel-CLT fluctuation term (Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first},
makespan fluctuations scale as $O(W^{-1/2})$ relative to the mean), the two
error sources add:
\[
  \Delta_{\mathrm{mf}} \;=\; O\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\right) + O\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{W}}\right)
  \quad\text{as } W \to \infty \text{ at fixed } N,
\]
i.e.\ $\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}$ stays bounded (by an $N$-dependent constant) as
$W\to\infty$; it need not vanish on its own for Step~3 below to go through.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~3 ($\eta_{\mathrm{util}} \to 1$).}
The oracle makespan satisfies
$C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}) = \Theta(W/(N\mu))$, which grows
without bound as $W \to \infty$ at fixed $N$. Since $\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}$ is
bounded by Step~2, the ratio below vanishes regardless of whether
$\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}$ itself does. From~\eqref{eq:dta_local_opt}:
\[
  \eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA})
  \;=\;
  \frac{\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\mathrm{DTA})]}{C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}})}
  \;=\;
  1 + \frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{mf}}}{C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}})}
  \;=\;
  1 + O\!\left(\left(\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{W}}\right) \cdot \frac{N\mu}{W}\right)
  \;=\; 1 + O\!\left(\frac{1}{W}\right) + O\!\left(\frac{N}{W^{3/2}}\right),
\]
which tends to~$1$ as $W \to \infty$ at fixed $N$, establishing the theorem
for every $\varepsilon > 0$ by choosing $W_0 = W_0(N,\varepsilon)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The information gap also vanishes for DTA]
\label{cor:dta_info_gap}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst}
($\varrho_0 < 1$), $C_{\max}^{\mathrm{OPT}} = C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}}$
(the clairvoyant offline optimum of Definition~\ref{def:Cstar} and the
$C_{\max}^{\mathrm{OPT}}$ of \S\ref{chap:competitive_ratio} denote the
same quantity), so
\[
  \eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{DTA}) \;\leq\; 1 + \frac{N\lfloor N/2\rfloor\,\delta_{\mathrm{hop}}}{W}
  \;\xrightarrow{W\to\infty}\; 1.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:decomp}, $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{DTA})\cdot
\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA}) = \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\mathrm{DTA})]/
C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst} is a
\emph{pathwise} (deterministic, worst-case-over-$\sigma$) bound on
$C_{\max}^{\mathrm{DTA}}(\sigma)/C_{\max}^{\mathrm{OPT}}(\sigma)$, so it
also bounds the expectation of this ratio under the stationary ergodic
arrival process of Assumption~\ref{ass:mmp}(\ref{ass:ergodic}), giving
$\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{DTA})\cdot\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA})
\leq 1 + N\lfloor N/2\rfloor\delta_{\mathrm{hop}}/W$. Since
$\eta_{\mathrm{info}}, \eta_{\mathrm{util}} \geq 1$ by definition (Theorem~\ref{thm:decomp}),
dividing by $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA}) \geq 1$ can only decrease
the bound, so $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{DTA}) \leq
\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{DTA})\cdot\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA})
\leq 1 + N\lfloor N/2\rfloor\delta_{\mathrm{hop}}/W$, and the claim
follows since $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{DTA}) \geq 1$ always.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of Corollary~\ref{cor:dta_info_gap}]
This closes the gap left open by Theorem~\ref{thm:dta_info_opt} alone:
together, the theorem and corollary show that \emph{both} factors of the
two-factor decomposition tend to $1$ for DTA as $W \to \infty$, so DTA is
information-optimal in the strong sense (matches the fully-informed
offline oracle), not merely utilisation-optimal within its own
information set. This is a large-workload (heavy-traffic) asymptotic
statement, consistent with the regime in which
Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst} and Theorem~\ref{thm:dta_info_opt} are
themselves proved; it does not by itself imply anything about the
finite-$W$, finite-$N$ comparison against Work-Stealing, which is why
the acquisition-rate analysis of \S\ref{sec:info_cost}--\ref{sec:numa_concrete}
remains the operationally relevant comparison at fixed, moderate scale.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[WS also achieves $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}\to 1$]
\label{rem:ws_opt}
WS achieves $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{WS})\to 1$ by a
\emph{different} argument, since WS's information set
$\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}$ does not include individual queue lengths.
We argue in two parts.

\noindent\textbf{(a)~Random-victim optimality within $\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}$.}
At steady state, the non-idle workers' queue lengths form an
\emph{exchangeable} sequence: by symmetry of the mean-field dynamics
(Proposition~\ref{prop:mf-recovery}), all non-idle workers are
identically distributed.  Conditioned on $\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}$
(which contains no per-worker queue information), the marginal
distribution of $q_i$ is the same for every non-idle worker~$i$.
By de Finetti's theorem~\cite{norris1997}, any exchangeable prior
is a mixture of i.i.d.\ distributions, so for any Borel set $A$:
\[
  \mathbb{P}(q_i \in A \mid \mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}})
  \;=\;
  \mathbb{P}(q_j \in A \mid \mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}})
  \quad\text{for all non-idle }i,j.
\]
Therefore the expected steal yield $\mathbb{E}[q_{\mathrm{victim}} \mid
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}]$ is \emph{identical} for every victim choice;
random-victim selection is Pareto-optimal (no deterministic policy
within $\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}$ dominates it)~\cite{blumofe1999}.

\noindent\textbf{(b)~Makespan efficiency.}
Under the $\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}$ constraint, WS achieves
$\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\mathrm{WS})] \leq T_\infty + T_1/N$~\cite{blumofe1999},
where $T_\infty$ is the critical-path length and $T_1 = W/\mu$ is the
total work.  For bag-of-tasks workloads ($T_\infty \ll W/(N\mu)$),
this gives $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{WS}) \leq 1 + T_\infty N\mu/W
\to 1$ as $W \to \infty$.

By the same squeeze argument as Corollary~\ref{cor:dta_info_gap}
(using $C_{\max}^{\mathrm{oracle}} \geq T_1/N$ and part~(b) above in place
of Theorem~\ref{thm:cr_bot_worst}), $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}(\mathrm{WS})
\leq 1 + T_\infty N\mu/W \to 1$ as well. So, exactly as for DTA, WS's
information gap also vanishes in the heavy-traffic limit --- this is
expected, since \emph{any} work-conserving BoT scheduler recovers the
oracle to leading order once $W$ dominates the fixed routing/stealing
overhead.

Hence \emph{both} DTA and WS achieve $\alpha \to 1$ and, by
Corollary~\ref{cor:dta_info_gap} and the argument just given,
$\eta_{\mathrm{info}} \to 1$ for both, in the $W \to \infty$ limit. The
asymptotic decomposition therefore does \emph{not} distinguish the two
schedulers; what does distinguish them are (i) the \emph{rate} at which
each ratio approaches $1$ --- governed by $N\lfloor N/2\rfloor\delta_{\mathrm{hop}}$
for DTA versus $T_\infty N\mu$ for WS, which are generally incomparable
without further workload assumptions --- and (ii) the information
acquisition rate $\beta$ each scheduler pays \emph{per unit time} to
attain its information set, analysed for finite, fixed system scale in
\S\ref{sec:info_cost}--\ref{sec:numa_concrete}. It is this second,
non-asymptotic cost comparison, not the heavy-traffic limit above, that
is the paper's substantive claim about why DTA outperforms
work-stealing in practice.
\end{remark}

% ============================================================
\section{Information Acquisition Rate}
\label{sec:info_cost}
% ============================================================

\subsection{Continuous rate function}

We model information acquisition as a \emph{cost flow}.

\begin{definition}[Information acquisition rate]\label{def:beta}
The \emph{information acquisition rate} of scheduler $\pi$ is the
stationary cost per unit time incurred in obtaining the observations that
constitute $\mathcal{I}_\pi$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta_def}
  \beta_\pi
  \;=\;
  \lim_{T\to\infty}
  \frac{\mathcal{C}_\pi(T)}{T},
  \qquad
  \mathcal{C}_\pi(T)
  \;=\;
  \int_0^T \dot{\mathcal{C}}_\pi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,
\end{equation}
where the instantaneous rate is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cdot}
  \dot{\mathcal{C}}_\pi(t)
  \;=\;
  \sum_{g \in G}
  \Bigl[
    r_{\mathrm{SPSC}}^g(t)\cdot c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}
    \;+\;
    r_{\mathrm{CAS}}^g(t)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}\bigl(n_{\mathrm{conc}}^g(t)\bigr)
  \Bigr],
\end{equation}
with $r^g_{\cdot}$ the per-worker event rates and $n_{\mathrm{conc}}^g$
the number of workers concurrently competing for the same cache line as $g$.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Discrete-to-continuous correspondence}

In hardware terms, each observation is a discrete event: an SPSC
read costs $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}$ cycles; a CAS costs
$c_{\mathrm{CAS}}(n_{\mathrm{conc}})$ cycles depending on contention.
The correspondence is:
\[
  \mathcal{C}_\pi(T)
  \;=\;
  \#_{\mathrm{SPSC}}(T)\cdot c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}
  \;+\;
  \sum_{\text{CAS event }k} c_{\mathrm{CAS}}(n_k),
\]
where $\#_{\mathrm{SPSC}}(T)$ counts SPSC reads in $[0,T]$ and $n_k$
is the concurrency at CAS event $k$.
Dividing by $T$ and taking the limit recovers $\beta_\pi$
from~\eqref{eq:beta_def}.

\subsection{CAS contention lower bound}

\paragraph{Model (Slotted CAS contention).}
Time is divided into hardware \emph{slots} of duration $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$
(one cache-coherence round-trip).  In each slot:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  \item Each of the $m$ remaining workers independently attempts CAS
        with probability $p \in (0,1)$.
  \item If \emph{exactly one} worker attempts, the CAS succeeds for that
        worker (it exits the contention).
  \item If \emph{zero} workers attempt, the slot is idle; no state changes.
  \item If \emph{two or more} workers attempt simultaneously, the cache
        controller serialises them but all see a conflicting value
        and fail; each failed worker independently retries in a later slot
        according to its backoff schedule.
\end{enumerate}
Binary exponential backoff (BEB) sets $p = 2^{-k}$ after $k$ consecutive
failures.  After $r = \lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor$ rounds, all workers have
accumulated $r$ failures and transmit at rate $p_r = 2^{-r} \in [1/(2n), 1/n]$:
the \emph{steady-state regime}.  We analyse this regime directly.

\begin{lemma}[CAS contention cost]\label{lem:cas_contention}
Under the Slotted CAS model with $n$ workers transmitting at rate
$p = 1/n$ (the BEB steady-state rate), the expected per-worker
completion time satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cas_lb}
  H_n \cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}
  \;\leq\;
  E[T_{\mathrm{worker}}]
  \;\leq\;
  e \cdot H_n \cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)},
\end{equation}
where $H_n = \sum_{k=1}^n 1/k = \Theta(\log n)$.
In particular, $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}(n) = \Theta(\log n)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$,
and the \emph{lower bound} $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}(n) \geq H_n\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}
= \Omega(\log n)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$ holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $T_{m\to m-1}$ denote the number of slots to reduce the active
count from $m$ to $m-1$.  Given $m$ active workers each transmitting
with probability $p = 1/n$, a slot is successful (exactly one transmits)
with probability
\[
  p_m
  \;:=\;
  \binom{m}{1}\frac{1}{n}\!\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{\!m-1}
  \;=\;
  \frac{m}{n}\!\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{\!m-1}.
\]
Since $T_{m\to m-1}$ is geometrically distributed with success
probability $p_m$, we have $\mathbb{E}[T_{m\to m-1}] = 1/p_m$.

\noindent\textbf{Lower bound.}
The inequality $(1-1/n)^{m-1} \leq 1$ gives $p_m \leq m/n$, hence
\[
  \mathbb{E}[T_{m\to m-1}]
  \;=\; \frac{1}{p_m}
  \;\geq\; \frac{n}{m}.
\]
Summing over $m = 1,\ldots,n$ and dividing by $n$:
\[
  \mathbb{E}[T_{\mathrm{worker}}]
  \;=\;
  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[T_{m\to m-1}]
  \;\geq\;
  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=1}^{n}\frac{n}{m}
  \;=\;
  H_n.
\]

\noindent\textbf{Upper bound.}
For $1 \leq m \leq n$ and $n \geq 2$, the sequence $(1-1/n)^{m-1}$
is decreasing in $m$, so
$(1-1/n)^{m-1} \geq (1-1/n)^{n-1}$.
Since $(1-1/n)^{n-1} \geq 1/e$ for all $n \geq 1$
(the function $n\mapsto (1-1/n)^{n-1}$ decreases to $1/e$ from above),
we get $p_m \geq m/(en)$, hence
\[
  \mathbb{E}[T_{m\to m-1}]
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{en}{m}.
\]
Summing and dividing by $n$: $\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathrm{worker}}] \leq e\cdot H_n$.

\noindent\textbf{BEB overhead.}
The $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor$ rounds before the BEB steady-state add at
most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor \cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)} = O(\log n)$
to the per-worker cost, which is absorbed into the $\Theta(\log n)$
bound without affecting the constants in~\eqref{eq:cas_lb}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why the coupon-collector argument requires care]
A na\"\i{}ve application of the coupon-collector~\cite{mitzenmacher2017} to CAS conflates two
distinct models.  The coupon-collector (draw uniformly from $n$ items
\emph{with replacement} until all are drawn) applies when the hardware
\emph{resamples} from the entire pool every round, even after a worker
has succeeded --- a model without exit.  In the correct model (workers
\emph{exit} after success), the per-round success probability
$p_m = (m/n)(1-1/n)^{m-1}$ depends on $m$, and the tight bound
$H_n \leq \mathbb{E}[T_{\mathrm{worker}}] \leq e\cdot H_n$ follows from
the geometric-sum analysis above rather than from the coupon-collector
directly.
\end{remark}

% ============================================================
\section{DTA vs.\ WS: Abstract Comparison}
\label{sec:dta_ws_abstract}
% ============================================================

\begin{definition}[$(\alpha,\beta)$-information-competitive]\label{def:alpha_beta}
A scheduler $\pi$ is \emph{$(\alpha,\beta)$-information-competitive} if
\[
  \eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\pi) \leq \alpha
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  \beta_\pi \leq \beta.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[DTA vs.\ WS in the $(\alpha,\beta)$ plane]
\label{thm:alpha_beta}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:mmp} and Lemma~\ref{lem:cas_contention},
with $\lambda_{\mathrm{steal}} \approx \lambda_{\mathrm{deflect}} \eqqcolon \lambda$
at high load:
\begin{align}
  \text{DTA:}\quad &
    \bigl(1+\varepsilon,\;
      N\lambda\cdot c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}\bigr)\text{-info-competitive},
      \label{eq:dta_ab}\\[4pt]
  \text{WS:}\quad &
    \bigl(1+\varepsilon',\;
      N\lambda\cdot\bigl(\bar\delta + \Omega(\log N)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}\bigr)\bigr)
      \text{-info-competitive},
      \label{eq:ws_ab}
\end{align}
for every $\varepsilon, \varepsilon' > 0$ as $W \to \infty$.
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta_ratio}
  \frac{\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}}{\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}}
  \;=\;
  \frac{\bar\delta + \Omega(\log N)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}}{c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}}
  \;=\; \Omega(\log N).
\end{equation}
Both schedulers achieve $\alpha \to 1$; the cost advantage of DTA
is $\Omega(\log N)$ in $\beta$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The $\alpha$ bounds follow from Theorem~\ref{thm:dta_info_opt} and its
Remark (WS analogue).
For DTA: each task triggers at most one SPSC message of cost $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}$;
$r_{\mathrm{CAS}}^\mathrm{DTA} = 0$ (SPSC channels are contention-free), giving
$\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}} = N\lambda \cdot c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}$.
For WS: each steal attempt reads the victim's deque tail (latency $\delta(g,h)$,
average $\bar\delta$) and executes a CAS with $n_{\mathrm{conc}} \leq N$
concurrent contenders; by Lemma~\ref{lem:cas_contention},
$c_{\mathrm{CAS}}(N) = \Omega(\log N)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$.
Hence $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}} = N\lambda(\bar\delta + \Omega(\log N)\cdot c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)})$.
The ratio~\eqref{eq:beta_ratio} follows since $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}} \leq \delta_{\mathrm{intra}} \leq \bar\delta$.
\end{proof}

% ============================================================
\section{Concrete Analysis on a Dual-Socket NUMA Topology}
\label{sec:numa_concrete}
% ============================================================

We instantiate the abstract framework on the simplest non-trivial NUMA
topology: a two-socket system with $N/2$ cores per socket.

\subsection{Topology and parameters}

Set $G = \mathbb{Z}_2 \times \mathbb{Z}_{N/2}$ (cf.~\S\ref{sec:future_work},
Open Problem~2).  The intra-socket subgroup is
$H = \{0\} \times \mathbb{Z}_{N/2}$.  We use the following representative
order-of-magnitude parameters for a modern dual-socket server (e.g.\
AMD EPYC 7763 or Intel Xeon Platinum 8380, both of which match this
profile); these are illustrative estimates drawn from published
hardware latency figures, not latencies measured on our own testbed:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Parameter & Symbol & Typical value \\
\midrule
Intra-socket SPSC latency & $\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}$ & $80\;\mathrm{ns}$ \\
Cross-socket NUMA latency & $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}$ & $300\;\mathrm{ns}$ \\
Uncontended CAS latency   & $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$ & $100\;\mathrm{ns}$ \\
SPSC read cost            & $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}$       & $\delta_{\mathrm{intra}} = 80\;\mathrm{ns}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The average NUMA latency is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta_bar_numa}
  \bar\delta
  = \delta_{\mathrm{intra}}\cdot\frac{N/2-1}{N-1}
  + \delta_{\mathrm{inter}}\cdot\frac{N/2}{N-1}
  \;\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}\;
  \frac{\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}+\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}}{2}
  = 190\;\mathrm{ns}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Information acquisition cost at concrete $N$}

Definition~\ref{def:beta} defines $\beta_\pi$ as a $T \to \infty$
long-run average rate. The closed-form expressions below evaluate this
limit at a fixed observation rate $\lambda$ and worker count $N$, using
the steady-state per-observation costs derived in
\S\ref{sec:numa_concrete}; this substitution is exact once the system
has reached stationarity (the $T \to \infty$ limit exists and equals the
stationary expectation by the ergodic theorem under
Assumption~\ref{ass:ergodic}), so no additional finite-$T$ approximation
error is introduced here. The only approximation is in the per-observation
cost constants themselves ($\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}$, $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}$,
$c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$), which \S\ref{sec:numa_concrete} already flags
as order-of-magnitude, not measured, hardware figures.
From Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha_beta}:
\begin{align}
  \beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}(N) &= N\lambda\cdot 80\;\mathrm{ns}, \label{eq:beta_dta_num}\\
