  \beta_{\mathrm{WS}}(N)  &= N\lambda\cdot\bigl(190 + 100\log_2 N\bigr)\;\mathrm{ns}.
  \label{eq:beta_ws_num}
\end{align}
The ratio $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ at representative core counts:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
$N$ & $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ & WS CAS overhead ($100\log_2 N$ ns)\\
\midrule
  8 & $\approx 6.1\times$ & 300 ns \\
 16 & $\approx 7.4\times$ & 400 ns \\
 32 & $\approx 8.6\times$ & 500 ns \\
 64 & $\approx 9.9\times$ & 600 ns \\
128 & $\approx 11.1\times$ & 700 ns \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The cost ratio grows logarithmically with $N$, consistent with the
observed widening of the throughput advantage as task count grows
(cf.\ Table~\ref{tab:summary}).

\subsection{Information gap via Fourier modes}

Using the group Fourier decomposition~\eqref{eq:numa_fourier}
from Open Problem~2, with $G = \mathbb{Z}_2 \times \mathbb{Z}_{N/2}$
the only non-trivial inter-socket mode is $(k,\ell) = (1,0)$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:info_gap_2socket}
  C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}) - C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}})
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{|\hat{F}_{(1,0)}|^2}{\mu N}
  \;=\;
  \frac{(\Delta q)^2}{4\mu N},
\end{equation}
where $\Delta q = |q_{\mathrm{socket\,0}} - q_{\mathrm{socket\,1}}|$ is the
inter-socket queue imbalance.  In the balanced BoT regime ($W \to\infty$):
$\Delta q = O(W^{-1/2}) \to 0$, so the information gap vanishes and
$C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}) / C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}}) \to 1$.

\subsection{Total cost and the crossover threshold $\gamma^*$}

\begin{definition}[Total scheduler cost]\label{def:J}
Let $\gamma \geq 0$ be a \emph{cost-weight} converting information
acquisition cost into makespan-equivalent units.  The
\emph{total cost} of scheduler $\pi$ over horizon $T$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:J}
  \mathcal{J}(\pi;\gamma)
  \;=\;
  \mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\pi)]
  \;+\;
  \gamma\cdot\beta_\pi\cdot T.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\noindent By Definition~\ref{def:J}, $\mathcal{J}(\pi;\gamma)$ is built from
$\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\pi)]$, the \emph{actual} expected makespan, not the
oracle $C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)$; the two are related by
$\mathbb{E}[C_{\max}(\pi)] = \eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\pi)\cdot C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)$
(the decomposition of Theorem~\ref{thm:decomp}). The exact crossover condition
therefore carries an $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}$ factor on each side: DTA dominates
WS ($\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{DTA}} < \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{WS}}$) if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma_star_exact}
  \gamma \;>\; \gamma^*
  \;\triangleq\;
  \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA})\,C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}})
        - \eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{WS})\,C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}})}{
        (\beta_{\mathrm{WS}} - \beta_{\mathrm{DTA}})\cdot T}.
\end{equation}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:dta_info_opt} and Remark~\ref{rem:ws_opt},
$\eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{DTA}), \eta_{\mathrm{util}}(\mathrm{WS})
\to 1$ as $W\to\infty$ (at fixed $N$), so in the large-workload limit the
$\eta_{\mathrm{util}}$ factors drop out and~\eqref{eq:gamma_star_exact}
reduces to the oracle-only expression used below:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma_star}
  \gamma^*
  \;\xrightarrow{W\to\infty}\;
  \frac{C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{DTA}}) - C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{WS}})}{
        (\beta_{\mathrm{WS}} - \beta_{\mathrm{DTA}})\cdot T}
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{(\Delta q)^2}{4\mu N \cdot (\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}-\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}})\cdot T},
\end{equation}
where we used~\eqref{eq:info_gap_2socket} for the numerator. This limiting
form is what the numerical estimate below applies; at finite $W$,
\eqref{eq:gamma_star_exact} should be used directly with measured
$\eta_{\mathrm{util}}$ values if a higher-precision threshold is needed.
Substituting~\eqref{eq:beta_dta_num}--\eqref{eq:beta_ws_num}
with $N=64$ and $\Delta q = O(1)$ (light unbalance):
\[
  \gamma^*
  \;\leq\;
  \frac{O(1)}{4\mu\cdot 64 \cdot 64\lambda(110+600)\,\mathrm{ns}\cdot T}
  \;=\; o(1/T).
\]
Since $\gamma^* \to 0$ as $W = \lambda T \to\infty$, \emph{DTA dominates
WS in total cost at any positive information weight $\gamma>0$ for
sufficiently large workloads.}

\subsection{Methodology for applying the framework}

We summarise a concrete procedure for practitioners choosing between
DTA and WS on a given target platform.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Measure hardware parameters.}
        Obtain $\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}$, $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}$,
        $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$, and $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}$ via
        \texttt{lmbench} or \texttt{likwid-bench}.

  \item \textbf{Estimate workload parameters.}
        Measure arrival rate $\lambda$, mean service time $1/\mu$, and
        load $\rho$.  Estimate the steady-state inter-socket imbalance
        $\Delta q$ from a short profiling run.

  \item \textbf{Compute $\beta$ values.}
        Use~\eqref{eq:beta_dta_num}--\eqref{eq:beta_ws_num}
        (or the general formulas of \S\ref{sec:info_cost})
        with the measured parameters.

  \item \textbf{Compute $\gamma^*$.}
        Apply~\eqref{eq:gamma_star}.  If the application's natural
        $\gamma$ (e.g.\ latency penalty in SLA contracts, converted to
        ns) satisfies $\gamma > \gamma^*$: choose DTA.

  \item \textbf{Sensitivity analysis.}
        Plot $\mathcal{J}(\pi;\gamma)$ for both schedulers as $\gamma$
        varies.  The crossing point is $\gamma^*$; the margin
        $|\gamma_{\mathrm{app}} - \gamma^*|$ quantifies robustness to
        parameter uncertainty.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[t]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figure/numa_info_cost.png}
  \caption{Information-acquisition cost analysis for DTA vs.\ WS on a
    dual-socket NUMA system
    ($\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}=80\,\mathrm{ns}$,
     $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}=300\,\mathrm{ns}$,
     $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}=100\,\mathrm{ns}$,
     $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}=80\,\mathrm{ns}$).
    \textbf{(A)}~Cost ratio $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ vs.\ $N$,
    confirming $\Omega(\log N)$ growth (Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha_beta}).
    \textbf{(B)}~Absolute acquisition costs per task; the shaded region is
    the DTA cost advantage.
    \textbf{(C)}~Total cost $\mathcal{J}(\pi;\gamma)$ for
    $W \in \{10^3, 10^4, 10^5\}$ tasks ($N=64$, $\Delta q=5$ tasks):
    DTA dominates above the respective crossover $\gamma^*$ (vertical lines).
    \textbf{(D)}~Relative information gap
    $\Delta C^*/C^*_{\mathrm{WS}}$ vs.\ total work $W$ under three
    imbalance models; the gap vanishes as $O(W^{-1/2})$ in the balanced
    regime (cf.~\eqref{eq:info_gap_2socket}).}
  \label{fig:numa_info_cost}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[Regime where WS may be preferred]
When load is low ($\rho \ll 1$, steal rate $\lambda_{\mathrm{steal}} \ll
\lambda_{\mathrm{deflect}}$), WS's steal events are rare and CAS contention
is light.  In this regime $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}} \approx \beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$
and the information gap~\eqref{eq:info_gap_2socket} can be non-negligible
(imbalance $\Delta q$ is larger at low load).  The framework predicts
WS may outperform DTA for very light, unbalanced workloads --- consistent
with the theoretical scope characterised in \S\ref{sec:dta_scope}.
\end{remark}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Summary.}
This part introduced an information-theoretic framework that resolves the
apparent paradox between DTA's limited global information and its empirically
observed throughput advantage over WS\@.
The key results are:
(i)~a two-factor decomposition of the competitive ratio into a structural
\emph{information gap} $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}$ and an algorithmic
\emph{utilisation ratio} $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}$;
(ii)~a proof that DTA achieves $\eta_{\mathrm{util}} \to 1$ as $W \to \infty$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:dta_info_opt}) --- utilisation-optimal within its
observation class --- together with $\eta_{\mathrm{info}} \to 1$
(Corollary~\ref{cor:dta_info_gap}), so DTA is information-optimal in the
full, heavy-traffic sense;
(iii)~both DTA and WS satisfy $\eta_{\mathrm{util}} \to 1$ \emph{and}
$\eta_{\mathrm{info}} \to 1$ in this same heavy-traffic limit, so the
asymptotic decomposition alone does not distinguish them; what does
distinguish them at fixed, moderate scale is the information acquisition
cost;
(iv)~$\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}} = \Omega(\log N)$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha_beta});
(v)~on a 64-core dual-socket NUMA system, WS pays $\approx 9.9\times$ more
in acquisition cost per task, growing to $11\times$ at 128 cores;
(vi)~$\gamma^* \to 0$ as $W \to \infty$: DTA dominates total cost at any
positive information-cost weight for large workloads.

\partbreak
\mypart{Empirical Validation}

\chapter{Empirical Validation}
\label{chap:empirical_validation}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Overview.}
The preceding parts establish DTA's guarantees analytically. This chapter
tests four of those guarantees against the real, unmodified scheduler
code — not a simulation of it — under a calibrated synthetic NUMA cost
model (no dual-socket hardware was available; \S\ref{sec:methodology}
gives the model and its justification). Where a prediction and a
measurement diverge, both are reported as what they are — a theoretical
value and a measured one — rather than adjusted toward each other.

\section{Methodology}
\label{sec:methodology}

All measurements compare the real \code{DtaScheduler} against a
literature-faithful pure work-stealing (WS) baseline (uniform-random
victim selection over a Chase-Lev deque, the same primitive Tokio and
Rayon use) built for this comparison specifically, since WS is the
theory's explicit point of comparison and no existing engineering
benchmark isolates it this way. Both schedulers run the same task
graphs, instrumented identically, under a synthetic NUMA cost model
using the paper's own published constants ($\delta_{\mathrm{intra}} =
80\,\mathrm{ns}$, $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}} = 300\,\mathrm{ns}$,
$c_{\mathrm{CAS}} = 100\,\mathrm{ns}$, \S\ref{chap:formal_model}): real
hardware supplies local-access timing, and the documented cross-socket
penalty is added whenever a scheduling decision would, on real
dual-socket hardware, have crossed a socket boundary.

Timings on the development machine used for these measurements are
right-skewed by occasional large host-scheduling stalls, not small
jitter around a stable mean — a mean over repeated trials is not robust
to this. Every result below is a \emph{median} over independent
repeats (with the observed range reported alongside), not a mean;
configurations sensitive enough to need more than a handful of repeats
to stabilise are run at the trial count that empirically converges
them, stated where relevant.

Open-loop (Poisson-arrival) measurements use
$N=4$ workers with a fixed per-task service time,
pacing arrivals to a target offered load $\varrho_0 = \lambda/(N\mu)$.
Closed-loop (batch) measurements admit all tasks as fast as possible
from independent generator threads that are not themselves workers, so
no worker is ever blocked generating instead of dispatching. The
latency-bound checks below use the scheduler's own published
constants exactly: $N=4$, $L = 131{,}072$, $H = 114{,}688$,
$C_W = 32{,}768$, $K = 32$ (matching \S\ref{chap:formal_model}'s
$L$/$H$/$C_W$/$K$ exactly, not placeholder values), with a synthetic
per-task service time $\delta = 1\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
(\S\ref{sec:latency-bounds} discusses why this must be at least
comparable to real per-task dispatch overhead for the bounds' scaling
in $\delta$ to be meaningful).

\section{Information-Acquisition Cost}
\label{sec:empirical-beta}

Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha_beta} predicts
$\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}(N) = \bar\delta + 100\log_2 N$ and a constant
$\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}} = 80\,\mathrm{ns}$, where $\bar\delta = 190\,\mathrm{ns}$
is the topology-averaged SPSC-equivalent read cost. Table~\ref{tab:beta-ratio}
recomputes this prediction at each measured $N$ and reports it beside
the median measured ratio.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
$N$ & Predicted $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ & Measured (median, range) & \\
\midrule
8  & $6.13\times$ & $2.19\times$ (1.57--2.70) & \\
16 & $7.38\times$ & $3.78\times$ (3.57--4.01) & \\
32 & $8.63\times$ & $5.15\times$ (4.23--5.63) & \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\captionof{table}{$\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$: theory vs.\ measurement.
Measured values are the mean of three independent median-of-repeats runs; the
range spans those three runs. $N=64$ is not reported: this
development machine has 8 logical CPUs, and $N=64$ oversubscribes it
8-fold, which dominates the measurement.}
\label{tab:beta-ratio}
\end{center}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{figure/beta_ratio_empirical.png}
\caption{$\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$: predicted (theory) vs.\
measured (median of three reruns, range shown). Table~\ref{tab:beta-ratio}
gives the same data.}
\label{fig:beta-ratio}
\end{figure}

Measurement confirms the qualitative claim — the ratio grows with
$N$ — but not the predicted magnitude, at roughly $0.35$--$0.6$ of the
predicted value across $N=8..32$. \S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses
plausible causes.

\section{Load-Ratio Regime}
\label{sec:empirical-load-ratio}

\S\ref{sec:dta_scope} claims DTA is most advantageous at high but
subcritical load, $\varrho_0 \in [0.6, 0.95]$. An open-loop
Poisson-arrival workload lets $\varrho_0$ be set directly, unlike the
closed-loop workloads used elsewhere in this document, for which
$\varrho_0$ is not a controlled quantity at all.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
$\varrho_0$ & Mean latency, DTA & Mean latency, WS \\
\midrule
0.1 (low load)       & slower & $5$--$8\times$ faster \\
0.5--0.6 (crossover)  & comparable & comparable \\
0.9 (high load, $N{=}8$) & $4\times$ faster & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\captionof{table}{Qualitative summary of the $\varrho_0$ sweep, $N \in \{4,8\}$.
DTA loses decisively at $\varrho_0=0.1$ and generally wins from
$\varrho_0 \approx 0.5$--$0.6$ upward, consistent with
\S\ref{sec:dta_scope}'s claim; the exact crossover point is noisy across
repeats and should not be read as precisely $0.6$.}
\label{tab:load-ratio}
\end{center}

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.72\linewidth]{figure/load_ratio_empirical.png}
\caption{Mean per-task latency vs.\ offered load $\varrho_0$, $N=4$, one
representative run (individual points are noisy; see \S\ref{sec:methodology}).
DTA loses at $\varrho_0=0.1$ and generally wins inside the shaded claimed
sweet spot.}
\label{fig:load-ratio}
\end{figure}

\section{Fork-Join Makespan Bound}
\label{sec:empirical-forkjoin}

Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first} bounds a level-synchronous, $d$-deep,
$K$-way-join DAG's makespan by $T_1/N + dH_K/\mu$. Measured against a
generator matching the theorem's own model exactly (i.i.d.\
$\mathrm{Exp}(\mu)$ per-task service times at each level), the raw
bound is violated by \emph{both} DTA and WS whenever $K > N$ — not a
DTA-specific failure. The proof's birth-death rate model implicitly
assumes every one of a level's $m$ remaining tasks can run
concurrently, which only holds while $m \le N$; \S\ref{sec:fj-precondition}
states this as an explicit precondition.

The paper's own combined bound (Remark~\ref{rem:dag_combined},
first-order plus the Gumbel-variance and batch-arrival/NESS-mismatch
corrections) holds for WS at every tested $N \in \{4,6,8\}$ and for DTA
at $N \in \{4,6\}$; at $N=8$ — this machine's full logical core
count, so workers plus the measurement thread oversubscribe it by
one thread — DTA sits at the boundary of the combined bound, passing
or narrowly failing depending on the run even at several hundred
trials. This is reported as a genuine but inconclusive finding for
$N=8$ specifically, not a confirmed violation.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.72\linewidth]{figure/forkjoin_empirical.png}
\caption{Fork-join makespan at the deepest, widest configuration tested
($d=8$, $K=16$): first-order and combined bounds vs.\ measured DTA and
WS makespan, across $N \in \{4,6,8\}$.}
\label{fig:forkjoin}
\end{figure}

Configurations with small join width ($K$ under $32$) show DTA
completing an entire level-synchronous DAG on a single worker
(load-balance ratio $= N$, i.e.\ every task on one worker) rather than
spreading it across all $N$. This is deflection correctly declining to
pay cross-worker signalling cost for a burst too small for the saved
work to be worth it: forcing earlier deflection (lowering the
deflection threshold) fixes the balance but increases wall-clock time,
confirming the unforced behaviour is the better one, not a defect.

\section{Latency and Fairness Bounds}
\label{sec:latency-bounds}
\label{sec:validity}

Four bounds are checked at the scheduler's own published constants
($N=4$, $L=131{,}072$, $H=114{,}688$, $C_W=32{,}768$, $K=32$):
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-wait}'s worst-case admission-to-execution wait
$W(\tau) \le \delta(2L + C_WK/N)$; Theorem~\ref{thm:kfair}'s $k$-bounded
fairness (by the theorem's own proof, $k = \lceil W(\tau)/(L\delta)
\rceil$, so it is read directly off the same wait-time data rather than
instrumented separately); Theorem~\ref{thm:det-batch}'s deterministic
batch makespan $C_{\max}^{(M)} \le H\delta + W_{\max}$; and
Theorem~\ref{thm:stat-makespan}'s statistical makespan bound, using the
mean queue depth $\bar q$ measured directly from a live, steady-state
$\varrho^*=0.7$ run rather than the mean-field prediction
$\bar q \approx \varrho^*/(1-\varrho^*)$ (a separate claim this section
is not testing) and the corrected $z_\alpha$
(\S\ref{sec:stat-makespan-correction}).

All four hold, comfortably and reproducibly, at a synthetic per-task
service time $\delta = 1\,\mu\mathrm{s}$. They do \emph{not} hold at
$\delta = 200\,\mathrm{ns}$: every bound is linear in $\delta$, which
implicitly assumes real per-task dispatch overhead is negligible next
to $\delta$ itself. This crate's measured dispatch overhead (mailbox
push/pop, atomics, cache effects) is not negligible next to a
$200\,\mathrm{ns}$ service time, so the bounds' scaling does not hold
there; at $\delta = 1\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ it does. This is a property of the
bounds' assumptions, not a scheduler defect, and is the reason
$\delta = 1\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ rather than a smaller value is used
throughout this section.

\section{Discussion}
\label{sec:discussion}

Two gaps are worth naming plainly rather than absorbing into the
theory. First, the $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ ratio
(\S\ref{sec:empirical-beta}) tracks the predicted trend but sits at
roughly half the predicted magnitude. A plausible contributor: the
model treats $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}$ as a fixed constant independent of $N$,
but real CAS cost under contention grows with the number of contending
threads (cache-coherence traffic), which the model's flat constant
does not capture — this would inflate the \emph{predicted} WS cost
relative to what contention-light measurement conditions actually
produce. Second, DTA's fork-join makespan
(\S\ref{sec:empirical-forkjoin}) exceeds even the combined bound at
$N=8$ on this specific machine; since $N=8$ is exactly this machine's
logical core count and the effect brackets cleanly against $N=6$ (headroom)
and disappears at lower $N$, host-thread oversubscription is the most
likely explanation, though it was not isolated further.

\partbreak
\mypart{Conclusions and Outlook}

% ============================================================
\chapter{Conclusions and Outlook}
\label{chap:conclusions}

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Overview.}
This chapter returns to the question posed in the Introduction
(\S\ref{sec:intro}): is restricting a scheduler to bounded, local
information a free design choice, or a real cost? We synthesise the
paper's answer, characterise the range of applicability of the developed
methods and the DTA algorithm, and identify the most promising directions
for future work. The information-theoretic cost framework developed in
Part~VI provides the analytical basis for the applicability conclusions
in \S\ref{sec:dta_scope}.

% ============================================================
\section{Mathematical Methods: Applicability to Distributed Systems}
\label{sec:methods_scope}
% ============================================================

This paper applies three analytical frameworks: \emph{operator-algebraic
modelling}, \emph{kinetic-theory / BBGKY expansion}, and an
\emph{information-theoretic cost framework}.  The first two originated in
mathematical physics; the third draws on classical information
theory~\cite{shannon1948,cover_thomas2006} and queueing control
theory~\cite{stidham1985,weber1990}.  We sketch the scope of each.

\subsection{Operator-algebraic modelling}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Core idea.}
Any distributed system in which tasks (or messages, or tokens) move
between a finite set of \emph{sites} (queues, threads, buffers, nodes)
can be encoded in an occupation-number Fock space~\cite{fock1932}.
Each atomic state transition becomes a composition of creation and
annihilation operators.  Conservation laws --- the most common
correctness requirements --- then correspond to commutation relations
between the total number operator $\hat{N}$ and the transition
operators.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Applicability conditions.}
The method is exact (not an approximation) whenever:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The state space is finite and discrete (as in any real implementation).
  \item Tasks (tokens) are distinguishable and subject to a capacity constraint.
  \item Correctness is stated as a conservation or reachability property.
\end{enumerate}
It is therefore applicable, without modification, to: message-passing
middleware (RPC queues, Kafka topics as sites), actor-model runtimes
(each actor mailbox is a site), connection-pool schedulers, workflow
engines with explicit task lifecycle states, and lock-free data structure
verification.  The only requirement is that the state graph be expressible
as a finite-capacity site algebra.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Advantages over traditional model checking.}
Model checkers (e.g.\ TLA+, Loom~\cite{loom2023}) verify correctness by state-space
exploration, which is exponential in the number of tasks $P$.
The operator-algebraic proof is \emph{size-independent}: the commutation
identity $[\hat{N}, \hat{T}_{\alpha\to\beta}] = 0$ is proved once and holds
for all $P$.  This makes the method well-suited to systems where the task
count is not bounded a priori.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Extension to non-conservative systems.}
The formalism as stated assumes a fixed task population: every
transition operator preserves $\hat N$. Real systems create and
destroy tasks --- recursive spawning, cancellation, timeouts ---
and \S\ref{sec:birth} extends the method to this case with the
source/sink recipe standard in the reaction-diffusion field-theory
literature~\cite{doi1976,peliti1985,tauber2005}: creation becomes a
transfer from a new, capacity-bounded implicit source (grounded in the
real task pool's finite capacity, not an idealised infinite reservoir),
and a proposed destruction mechanism should first be checked against
the \emph{existing} sink transition before any new operator is
introduced --- DTA's own cancellation turned out to need no new
operator at all, reusing $\hat O_{\text{finish}}$ under a different
outcome label. The general lesson --- verify a non-conservative event
is structurally novel before generalising the algebra to accommodate
it --- transfers to any site-algebra model of a system with dynamic
population.

\subsection{Kinetic-theory / BBGKY hierarchy}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Core idea.}
In any system with $N \gg 1$ interacting agents (workers, nodes, replicas)
where the interaction is \emph{pairwise} and \emph{symmetric} (any worker
may interact with any other), the $N$-body dynamics admits a systematic
expansion in powers of $1/N$.  The zeroth-order term is a mean-field (MF)
ODE or master equation; the first-order term captures pairwise correlations
via a pair-density correction; and so on.  This is precisely the BBGKY
hierarchy~\cite{bogoliubov1946,born_green1949,kirkwood1946,yvon1935},
familiar from kinetic gas theory.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Transfer recipe for distributed systems.}
Given a distributed system with $N$ symmetric agents:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Identify the one-body density.}
        Let $f_1(q, t)$ be the marginal distribution of agent state
        (e.g.\ queue length, buffer occupancy, CPU load).
  \item \textbf{Write the exact evolution equation for $f_1$.}
        This will depend on the two-body density $f_2(q, q', t)$
        (correlation between two agents), giving BBGKY level 1.
  \item \textbf{Apply the Stosszahlansatz ($N \to \infty$).}
        Factorise $f_2 \approx f_1 \otimes f_1$.  The result is a
        closed MF equation (an ODE or PDE) that is exact as $N \to \infty$.
  \item \textbf{Compute the level-1 correction.}
        Write $f_2 = f_1 \otimes f_1 + g_2$ and derive the ODE for
        $g_2$ from the level-2 BBGKY equation, closing by setting
        $g_3 \approx 0$.  This gives an $O(1/N)$ correction to the MF.
  \item \textbf{Validate numerically.}
        Run a CTMC simulation at finite $N$ and compare with the
        MF prediction and its $O(1/N)$ correction.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Applicability conditions.}
The method requires:
(i)~pairwise, symmetric interactions (no fixed master-worker hierarchy);
(ii)~a well-defined thermodynamic limit $N \to \infty$;
(iii)~ergodicity (the CTMC is irreducible under the mean-field closure).
It is directly applicable to: supermarket-model load balancers~\cite{mitzenmacher2001,vvedenskaya1996},
gossip protocols and epidemic spreading, distributed consensus
(Raft/Paxos with $N$ replicas), connection-pool rebalancing,
and any system analysed by the ``power of two choices'' framework~\cite{azar1999}.
The MF result is typically tight when $N \geq 8$; the Level-1 correction
is significant for small $N$ (4--8 agents), exactly the practically
relevant regime for multicore schedulers.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{When the Level-2 closure is tractable in closed form.}
Step~4 above closes at level~1 by discarding the triplet correlator
$g_3$; \S\ref{sec:level2-explicit} and \S\ref{sec:dag-level2} identify
two conditions under which the next order is itself tractable without
a symbolic derivation of $g_3$'s general functional form: (i)~a binary
per-agent state (here, $H=1$ load levels, or complete/incomplete
DAG-task activation) collapses the exact many-body chain onto a much
smaller aggregated statistic --- a count, or an overlap size --- via a
standard lumpability argument, turning an intractable $2^N$-state
problem into a small, exactly solvable birth-death chain or a
closed-form hypergeometric expectation; (ii)~when the interaction
structure is itself combinatorial (random shared predecessors, in the
DAG case) rather than purely dynamical, the relevant correlator can
sometimes be computed directly from the interaction graph's
combinatorics, sidestepping the master equation for that piece
entirely. Neither condition holds in general --- for $H>1$ per-agent
states, only the correlator's \emph{order} remains tractable in closed
form (Proposition~\ref{prop:residual-order}) --- but checking for them
is worth the effort before assuming a level-2 closure is out of reach.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Extension to DAGs.}
As shown in \S\ref{sec:dag_kinetic} (Performance~IV), the kinetic framework
extends naturally to heterogeneous workloads with dependency graphs:
the layer-completion fraction $f_\ell(t)$ plays the role of $f_1$, and
the fan-in $K$ controls the sharpness of the phase transition from
second-order (smooth, finite $K$) to first-order (discontinuous,
$K \to \infty$, pure fork-join).

\subsection{Information-theoretic cost framework}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Core idea.}
Any distributed scheduler makes decisions under \emph{limited information}
and incurs a cost to acquire that information.  Part~VI formalises this
observation by separating the total competitive ratio into two independent
factors: the \emph{information gap} $\eta_{\mathrm{info}}$ (how much the
scheduler's limited view of the system costs it, structurally) and the
\emph{utilisation ratio} $\eta_{\mathrm{util}}$ (how efficiently it uses
what it knows).  Information-acquisition cost $\beta_\pi$ is defined as a
continuous rate function over hardware observations (SPSC reads, CAS
operations), with a discrete-to-continuous bridge to actual cycle counts.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Applicability conditions.}
The framework applies to any pair of schedulers that differ in
(i)~the information set they observe, and
(ii)~the hardware cost of those observations.
It requires a queueing model (for $C_{\max}^*(\mathcal{I}_\pi)$) and
hardware latency measurements ($\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}$,
$\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}$, $c_{\mathrm{CAS}}^{(0)}$, $c_{\mathrm{SPSC}}$).
It is directly applicable to any work-stealing vs.\ push-based scheduler
comparison on NUMA or multi-socket hardware.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Key output: the $\gamma^*$ crossover.}
The framework produces a concrete \emph{crossover threshold} $\gamma^*$:
for any positive information-cost weight $\gamma > \gamma^*$, DTA's lower
acquisition cost outweighs WS's information advantage, and DTA achieves
lower total cost.  At high load ($W \to \infty$), $\gamma^* \to 0$, so
DTA dominates at any positive $\gamma$.

\subsection{Large-deviation theory for tail guarantees}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Core idea.}
Where a second-moment bound gives only polynomial tail decay, and an
extreme-value asymptotic (Fisher--Tippett--Gnedenko) is valid only in a
large-$N$ limit not always reached at practical scales, a direct
large-deviation argument --- built from the \emph{exact} distribution
of the quantity in question rather than its moments or its asymptotic
limit --- can give a uniformly valid, genuinely exponential tail bound.
\S\ref{sec:large-deviation}'s Cram\'er-type makespan bound
(Theorem~\ref{thm:cramer-makespan}) is derived this way: from the
already-exact CDF of a maximum of approximately independent
truncated-geometric queue lengths, a single elementary inequality
($1-x^N \leq N(1-x)$) yields an exponential bound with an explicit,
checkable rate, correcting an earlier informally guessed one.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Applicability conditions.}
The technique applies whenever (i)~the underlying per-agent
distribution's exact tail is known in closed form, not merely its
first two moments, and (ii)~the quantity of interest is a maximum,
sum, or other simple functional of many approximately independent
agents --- or, as \S\ref{sec:large-deviation} shows via an
association/FKG argument, positively dependent agents, provided the
dependence structure can be shown not to worsen the bound. It
generalises directly to any bounded-buffer queueing network whose
per-node stationary distribution has an explicit geometric or
sub-exponential tail: connection-pool exhaustion, buffer-overflow
probabilities, and SLA-violation guarantees for any system with the
M/M/1/$H$-type structure this paper analyses.

\subsection{Beyond Poisson: Markov-modulated arrival models}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Core idea.}
The mean-field/BBGKY machinery above assumes memoryless (Poisson)
arrivals, which is what makes the per-agent state alone Markov.
\S\ref{sec:non-poisson} shows that Markov-modulated (MMPP) arrivals
--- a finite-state ``phase'' process modulating the instantaneous
arrival rate, the standard model for bursty, correlated traffic ---
preserve tractability by a single, general trick: augment the state
with the phase, and every technique already developed for the
unmodulated chain (self-consistency fixed points, BBGKY closures,
stationary-distribution solvers) applies unchanged on the enlarged,
still-finite, still-Markov state space. The result is a finite
quasi-birth-death (QBD) process, solvable by ordinary linear algebra
whenever the buffer is finite (as it always is in a real
implementation), without the infinite-buffer matrix-geometric
machinery the QBD literature usually needs.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Applicability conditions.}
This transfers to any system in this paper's class where the real
arrival process is suspected to be bursty rather than Poisson ---
request traffic with diurnal or session-correlated structure, for
instance. The general renewal-arrival case (\S\ref{sec:renewal}) does
not preserve exact tractability the same way, since the per-agent
state alone is no longer Markov; only a distribution-free
heavy-traffic bound is available there, and whether a closed
self-consistency system exists for general renewal arrivals, the way
it does here for MMPP, remains open.

% ============================================================
\section{DTA Algorithm: Applicability and Use-Case Scope}
\label{sec:dta_scope}
% ============================================================

The DTA scheduler is designed for and performs best in a specific
operational envelope.  We characterise this envelope using the
theoretical results of Parts~III--V.

\subsection{Workload types}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Best fit: Bag-of-Tasks (BoT) at moderate-to-high load.}
The competitive ratio analysis (Part~V, \S\ref{chap:competitive_ratio}) shows
$\rho_c^{\mathrm{DTA}} \to 1$ as total work $W \to \infty$ under the
mean-field assumption.  The adversarial worst-case bound
(\S\ref{sec:cr_bot_worst}) gives $\rho_c^{\mathrm{DTA,worst}} \leq
1 + N\lfloor N/2\rfloor\delta_{\mathrm{hop}}/W$, which is also $\to 1$
for large $W$.  BoT workloads at high load are therefore the primary
design target: the deflection overhead is amortised over task computation.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Good fit: Parallel batch computation.}
Any workload decomposable into a fixed pool of independent tasks
--- matrix-vector products, data-parallel map-reduce, Monte Carlo
simulation, image tile processing --- maps directly to the BoT model.
The load-ratio measurements of \S\ref{sec:empirical-load-ratio} confirm
this directly: DTA wins decisively over WS from $\varrho_0 \approx
0.5$--$0.6$ upward, exactly the sustained-BoT-at-high-load regime this
paragraph describes.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Good fit: layered fork-join DAGs with bounded fan-in.}
The DAG kinetic theory (\S\ref{sec:dag_kinetic}, Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first})
gives $E[C_{\max}] \leq T_1/N + d \cdot H_K/\mu$, which is tight
for moderate $K$ (fan-in).  For shallow DAGs ($d \leq 10$, $K \leq 16$)
the bound is near-optimal.  DTA's SPSC-push routing is efficient for
inter-stage fan-out/fan-in.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Moderate fit: general (irregular) DAGs.}
Real dependency graphs are rarely layered or uniform-fan-in.
Theorem~\ref{thm:general_dag} (\S\ref{sec:general-dag}) covers this
case directly, with no assumption of layering or a common per-level
$K$: $E[C_{\max}^G] \leq T_1/N + d^*\cdot W_{\max}$, where $d^*$ is
the DAG's critical-path length in tasks. This bound holds for
\emph{any} DAG, but is looser than the layered bound above --- it does
not match work-stealing's classical $T_1/N + O(T_\infty)$ tightness on
arbitrary DAGs, and whether a tight bound exists for DTA's push-based
routing is open (Open Problem~\ref{open:general_dag},
\S\ref{sec:future_work}). Treat DTA as a reasonable, not optimal,
choice for irregular DAG workloads until that gap is closed.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Cooperative-yield and cancellable workloads.}
The DTA algorithm targets spawn-and-complete tasks; it does not
formally specify a yield or cancellation execution model, so both
remain outside this paper's performance-\emph{bound} scope in the
strict sense. In the \dtact{} implementation specifically, however, a
self \texttt{yield\_now()} with nothing else ready never reaches the
real context-switch path at all (an adaptive-spin fast path in
\texttt{wait\_pinned} resolves it within two polls), and now
measurably beats Tokio's own \texttt{yield\_now()} rather than
trailing it, as an earlier revision of this scope note stated
(\texttt{bench\_yield\_now\_loop},
\texttt{benches/scheduler\_efficiency.rs}); an explicit named-peer
\texttt{yield\_to()} handoff, which does take the real switch, has no
directly comparable Tokio primitive to benchmark against. Cancellation
(\S\ref{sec:birth}, \S\ref{sec:cancel-reuse}) is now a supported,
formally accounted-for operation --- it reuses the existing
task-finish transition rather than requiring new scheduler machinery
--- so cancellable workloads are no longer an implementation gap, only
(like yield) a formally unmodelled one: the makespan and stability
bounds of this paper do not yet cover a workload mix that includes
cancellation.

\subsection{Load regime}

The stability ladder (Performance~IV) identifies three regimes:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\varrho_0 < 1$: throughput-stable (Level~1 guarantee).
        At high load ($\varrho_0 \to 1^-$), deflection is most
        beneficial: imbalance is driven to $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ and the
        system approaches full utilisation.
  \item $\varrho_0 > 1$ transiently (burst): the warehouse absorbs
        $B^* = C_W \mu$ tasks per second before overloading.
        With $H = 114{,}688$ (production config), burst tolerance
        is 573$\times$ higher than at the benchmark $H=200$.
  \item $\varrho_0 \gg 1$ sustained: instability is inevitable for
        any finite-capacity scheduler.  The first-passage crash time
        $E[\tau_{\mathrm{crash}}]$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:crash-time})
        quantifies the safety margin.
\end{itemize}
The main lesson is that \textbf{DTA is most advantageous at high but
subcritical load} ($\varrho_0 \in [0.6, 0.95]$): deflection actively
reduces imbalance, the makespan bound is tight, and the warehouse
provides a substantial burst buffer.

\subsection{Hardware topology}

The formal model assumes a $K_4$ all-to-all SPSC topology.
The hash-routing Proposition~\ref{prop:hash_mf} shows this is exact
under uniform flow IDs.  For NUMA or CCX-topology hardware, the
mean-field result gives the topology-unaware baseline; same-socket
peer preference is an $O(1/N)$ perturbation.  In practice, for
$N \leq 8$ workers pinned to a single socket, all-to-all latency is
small ($\leq 30\,\mathrm{ns}$ SPSC write) and the topology correction
is negligible.

For larger multi-socket deployments, Part~VI (\S\ref{sec:numa_concrete})
provides a quantitative framework.  On a representative 64-core dual-socket
system, DTA's information-acquisition cost $\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ is
$\approx 9.9\times$ lower than WS's $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}$ at $N=64$,
growing as $\Omega(\log N)$.  The crossover threshold $\gamma^* \to 0$
as $W \to \infty$, confirming that DTA is the preferred choice at high
task counts on NUMA hardware.

% ============================================================
\section{Summary of Contributions}
\label{sec:contributions}
% ============================================================

We summarise the principal contributions of this paper.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Methodological contributions.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Operator-algebraic scheduler modelling.}
        A Fock-space formalism for concurrent task schedulers that reduces
        correctness proofs to algebraic commutation identities, independent
        of task count; extended to non-conservative task lifecycles via a
        bounded source/sink recipe (\S\ref{sec:birth}) grounded in the real,
        capacity-limited task pool rather than an idealised infinite one.
  \item \textbf{BBGKY expansion for distributed schedulers, closed at
        Level~2 in closed form.}
        First systematic application of the BBGKY kinetic hierarchy to
        a work-stealing / deflection scheduler, yielding a rigorous
        $O(1/N)$ Level-1 correction to the mean-field prediction, and an
        exact Level-2 triplet correlator for the $H=1$ case
        (\S\ref{sec:level2-explicit}) that confirms the general $O(1/N^2)$
        order by independent, fully explicit computation and surfaces a
        load-dependent sign change not previously known.
  \item \textbf{Kinetic-theory framework for DAG scheduling, with an exact
        finite-size correlator.}
        A mean-field ODE describing layer-completion fractions in
        fork-join DAGs, with two provable second-order corrections
        ($\delta^{(A)}$ Gumbel fluctuation; $\delta^{(B)}$ incomplete
        relaxation via Fill's TV-distance bound), an exact closed-form
        joint-eligibility correlator (\S\ref{sec:dag-level2}) via the
        predecessor-overlap distribution's exact hypergeometric law, and
        a directed-percolation phase transition identified as an open
        problem.
  \item \textbf{Adversarial worst-case BoT bound without mean-field.}
        A competitive-ratio upper bound whose proof is derived purely
        from work conservation and hop-count geometry and requires no
        probabilistic assumption on the arrival sequence (its stability
        precondition $\varrho_0 < 1$ is separately established via the
        mean-field analysis of Performance~IV).
  \item \textbf{Information-theoretic scheduler cost framework.}
        A formal decomposition of the competitive ratio into information
        gap and utilisation efficiency; a proof that both
        $\eta_{\mathrm{util}} \to 1$ and $\eta_{\mathrm{info}} \to 1$ for
        DTA as $W \to \infty$ (heavy-traffic information-optimality); and a
        quantitative $\Omega(\log N)$ cost advantage over WS on NUMA
        topologies, with a practitioner methodology for computing the
        DTA/WS crossover threshold $\gamma^*$ on target hardware.
  \item \textbf{A large-deviation (Cram\'er-type) tail bound.}
        A uniformly valid exponential makespan tail bound
        (\S\ref{sec:large-deviation}) derived directly from the exact
        maximum-queue-length CDF, with an explicit rate correcting an
        earlier informally guessed one, plus an association/FKG argument
        showing the overload-contagion correlation that worsens the
        mean-field load estimate does not further fatten this tail.
  \item \textbf{Closed-form Markov-modulated (bursty) arrivals.}
        An exact finite quasi-birth-death self-consistency system for
        MMPP arrivals (\S\ref{sec:non-poisson}), quantifying how much
        arrival burstiness alone --- independent of the mean rate ---
        degrades load balance, plus a distribution-free bound for the
        harder general-renewal-arrival case.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Analytical results for DTA.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Four correctness theorems: task conservation, deadlock freedom,
        livelock freedom ($\leq\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ hops), and
        linearizability of SPSC and MPMC queues; generalized
        (\S\ref{sec:gen-conserve}) to a continuity law
        $\Num(\sigma_t) = B(t) - F(t)$ accounting for task creation and
        cancellation as explicit boundary flux.
  \item Bounded waiting time theorem with explicit, distribution-free
        closed-form ceiling $W_{\max}(N,L,C_W,K,\delta)$ and $k$-bounded
        fairness corollary.
  \item Mean-field load balance: M/M/1/$H$ self-consistency equation,
        queue-length imbalance $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ under deflection,
        propagation-of-chaos validation for $N \to \infty$, an exact
        Level-2 correction for $H=1$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:g2g3-explicit}),
        and a closed-form extension to Markov-modulated arrivals
        (Theorem~\ref{thm:mmpp-sc}).
  \item Four-level stability ladder: Foster--Lyapunov throughput stability,
        NESS convergence rate (spectral gap $\gamma \geq \mu(1-\varrho^*)$),
        adversarial burst absorption ($B^* \propto NH\mu/\lambda$),
        and crash-free first-passage threshold.
  \item Three-line makespan bound: deterministic work-conservation term
        $T_1/N + \delta_{\mathrm{hop}}\cdot\lfloor N/2\rfloor$; Gumbel
        fluctuation correction $O(\log N/\mu)$; and a Cram\'er-type
        exponential tail bound (Theorem~\ref{thm:cramer-makespan})
        uniformly valid rather than asymptotic-in-$N$.
  \item Empirical validation against the real scheduler
        (\hyperref[chap:empirical_validation]{Empirical Validation}):
        the bounded-wait, $k$-fair, deterministic- and
        statistical-batch-makespan bounds all hold at the paper's own
        published parameters; the fork-join first-order bound and the
        $\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ acquisition-cost ratio
        are directionally confirmed but do not match the theory's exact
        predicted magnitudes, a gap discussed there rather than glossed
        over.
\end{enumerate}

% ============================================================
\section{Future Work and Open Problems}
\label{sec:future_work}
% ============================================================

We close with the open directions this paper's results point to,
grouped by what they share --- a common underlying question, at
different depths --- rather than by how soon each might be resolved:
a concrete engineering extension and a genuinely deep open problem
are, in the two cases below, often the same question asked at two
different levels of generality.

\subsection{NUMA topology: from a routing heuristic to a universality question}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{The concrete step: NUMA-aware peer selection.}
Extending the hash-routing Proposition~\ref{prop:hash_mf} to
non-uniform topologies is, in its narrowest form, an engineering
extension: replace the uniform peer distribution with a
NUMA-distance-weighted kernel and re-derive the BBGKY Level-1
correction, which requires solving a weighted self-consistency
equation in place of~\eqref{eq:sc-mf}. This is tractable with the
tools already in hand (\S\ref{chap:kinetic_theory}); it has not been
carried out.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Open Problem~2 (DTA on heterogeneous NUMA topologies): the
deep question it is a special case of.}
Modern multi-socket servers expose \emph{non-uniform memory access}
(NUMA) costs: the SPSC latency $\delta(i,j)$ between workers $i$ and
$j$ depends on their physical placement, breaking the homogeneity
assumption that underlies all mean-field results of this paper. The
weighted self-consistency equation above is the linearised,
single-socket-pair special case of a fully general question, for
which we lay a group-theoretic foundation and state the central open
question.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Topological group structure.}
Model the $N$ workers as elements of a finite abelian group
\begin{equation}\label{eq:numa_group}
  G \;=\; \mathbb{Z}_{N_s} \times \mathbb{Z}_{N_c},
\end{equation}
where $N_s$ is the number of NUMA sockets and $N_c = N/N_s$ the number of
cores per socket.  The \emph{Cayley graph} $\mathrm{Cay}(G,S)$ with generator
set $S = \{(\pm 1, 0),\, (0, \pm 1)\}$ encodes worker adjacency; it is a
discrete $2$-torus with the lattice structure familiar from condensed matter
physics.  The intra-socket subgroup
\[
  H \;=\; \{0\} \times \mathbb{Z}_{N_c} \;\leq\; G
\]
acts as a translation symmetry: any two workers in the same socket are
related by an element of $H$, and the quotient $G/H \cong \mathbb{Z}_{N_s}$
indexes the sockets.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{NUMA latency kernel and $H$-bi-invariance.}
Define the SPSC latency function $\delta \colon G \times G \to \mathbb{R}_{+}$
by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:numa_delta}
  \delta(g,h) \;=\;
  \begin{cases}
    \delta_{\mathrm{intra}} & \text{if } gh^{-1} \in H
                              \quad (\text{same socket}), \\
    \delta_{\mathrm{inter}} & \text{otherwise}
                              \quad (\text{cross-socket}),
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
with $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}} > \delta_{\mathrm{intra}} > 0$.
This kernel is $H$-bi-invariant: $\delta(kg, kh) = \delta(g,h)$ for all
$k \in H$, reflecting the fact that intra-socket latency is independent of
which specific cores within a socket are involved.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Group-indexed BBGKY density and Fourier decomposition.}
In the NUMA regime the one-body density is a \emph{group-indexed field}
$f_1^g(q,t)$ --- the marginal queue-length distribution of worker $g$.
Under the $H$-bi-invariance of~\eqref{eq:numa_delta}, $f_1^g$ depends on
$g$ only through its coset $gH \in G/H$, yielding $N_s$ coupled mean-field
equations (one per socket) in place of the single equation~\eqref{eq:sc-mf}.

To analyse inter-socket imbalance we expand in the irreducible
representations (irreps) of $G$.  Since $G$ is abelian, every irrep is a
one-dimensional character
\[
  \chi_{(k,\ell)}(s,c) \;=\; \omega_{N_s}^{ks}\,\omega_{N_c}^{\ell c},
  \qquad
  \omega_n \;=\; e^{2\pi i/n},
\]
indexed by $(k,\ell) \in \hat G \cong G$.  Define the discrete Fourier
coefficient
\begin{equation}\label{eq:numa_fourier}
  \hat{F}_{(k,\ell)}(q,t)
  \;=\;
  \frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g \in G} f_1^g(q,t)\,\overline{\chi_{(k,\ell)}(g)}.
\end{equation}
The \emph{zero mode} $(k,\ell) = (0,0)$ recovers the socket-averaged density
$\bar f_1(q,t)$, which satisfies the standard mean-field equation derived in
\S\ref{chap:kinetic_theory}.  Non-zero modes quantify inter- and intra-socket imbalance;
their amplitudes $|\hat{F}_{(k,\ell)}|$ measure the deviation from the
homogeneous mean-field prediction.  The spectral gap
$\lambda_1 = \min_{(k,\ell)\neq(0,0)} \hat\delta(k,\ell)$
of the latency kernel (Fourier-transformed via the same formula) controls
whether imbalance modes decay or persist at stationarity.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Open question.}
Prove (or disprove) that the stationary distribution of the group-indexed
BBGKY Level-1 system admits a Fourier-mode decoupling analogous
to~\eqref{eq:numa_fourier}, and determine the effect of the explicit
symmetry breaking $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}} > \delta_{\mathrm{intra}}$ on
the universality class identified in Open Problem~1: does the directed-percolation
exponent $\beta \approx 0.2765$ survive, or does a new universality class
emerge when the ratio $\delta_{\mathrm{inter}}/\delta_{\mathrm{intra}}$
exceeds a critical threshold?

\subsection{DAG scheduling: from directed percolation to a tight competitive bound}

Two open problems concern what happens once DTA is asked to schedule
DAG workloads outside the well-behaved regime --- layered, bounded
fan-in --- that \S\ref{sec:dag_kinetic}'s positive results cover. They
are connected by the same underlying question, asked at two different
levels: does DTA's specific mechanism (push-based, hop-bounded
deflection, with no pull-side visibility into peer load) fundamentally
limit what is achievable on \emph{general} DAG structure, or is the
gap between DTA's proven bounds and the classical work-stealing
results merely an artefact of an incomplete analysis?

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Open Problem~1 (DP universality for DAG scheduling).}
Establish rigorously that the scheduling phase transition in the random
DAG ensemble is in the directed-percolation (DP) universality class,
and that the makespan scaling exponents equal the DP critical exponents
$\beta \approx 0.2765$, $\nu_\parallel \approx 1.734$,
$\nu_\perp \approx 1.097$~\cite{hinrichsen2000}.
A proof would require controlling the propagation of chaos through the
DAG depth, likely using the Sznitman coupling~\cite{sznitman1991}
extended to tree-structured particle systems.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Open Problem~3 (tight general-DAG bound for DTA).}
\S\ref{sec:general-dag} proves a structure-free makespan bound
for DTA on an arbitrary task DAG, $T_1/N + d^*\cdot W_{\max}$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:general_dag}), by charging the worst-case
admission-to-execution ceiling at every critical-path node. This is
strictly weaker than the classical work-stealing result
$T_1/N + O(T_\infty)$ for arbitrary DAGs~\cite{blumofe1999,arora2001},
whose potential-function proof exploits the \emph{pull} side of
stealing (an idle worker's steal target adapts to which peer currently
has the most ready work) --- a mechanism DTA's push-based, hop-bounded
deflection has no equivalent of (Open Problem~\ref{open:general_dag}).
Whether a $T_\infty$-tight bound holds for DTA at all, and if so what
potential function establishes it, is open; a negative answer (a
matching worst-case separation between DTA and WS on adversarial DAG
instances) would be equally informative, since it would identify
general, irregular DAGs as a genuine limitation of push-based
scheduling rather than an artefact of an incomplete proof.

\subsection{A more tractable, and more purely engineering, extension: adaptive $H$}

The current model treats the admission threshold $H$ as a static
parameter throughout --- every result in
\hyperref[chap:load_balance]{Performance~I--III}, including the
BBGKY closures and the stability ladder, is derived for fixed $H$. A
dynamic threshold (e.g.\ $H(t) = H_0 + \kappa \cdot \bar{q}(t)$) could
improve burst absorption while preserving throughput stability, but
would require re-deriving the four-level stability ladder
(\hyperref[chap:stability]{Performance~IV}) with a time-dependent
Lyapunov function rather than the static one used throughout this
paper. This is the shallowest of the four remaining directions: no
new mathematical machinery is needed, only re-running existing proof
techniques against a moving target.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Which of these are actually open.}
Open Problems~1 and~3 are the two we consider genuinely hard: DP
universality (1) asks for a rigorous derivation of numerically-known
critical exponents in a new physical setting, and the
$T_\infty$-tight DAG bound (3) asks whether a potential-function
argument exists at all for a router with no pull-side visibility ---
both are plausible multi-year research questions in their own right,
not gaps left by insufficient effort here. Open Problem~2 (NUMA
heterogeneity) is not fully an open question so much as an
underspecified programme: its first half --- Fourier-mode decoupling
--- is close to a standard diagonalisation argument for a
group-covariant \emph{linear} operator once the dynamics is linearised
around the symmetric fixed point, though the fully nonlinear
mean-field equation need not decouple exactly; its second half
(whether the DP universality class survives inter/intra-socket
symmetry breaking) inherits Open Problem~1's difficulty, and its
concrete engineering special case (NUMA-aware peer selection, above)
is tractable with existing tools but not yet carried out. Adaptive $H$
is, as already noted, the one item here with no open mathematical
content at all --- an engineering re-derivation, not a research
question.

\bigskip
\noindent\textbf{Summary.}
We set out to answer whether restricting a distributed scheduler to
bounded, local information is a free design choice or a real cost. The
answer, for the DTA scheduler studied here, is that it is close to free:
DTA is simultaneously \emph{provably correct} (four theorems), \emph{provably
live} (bounded wait, starvation freedom), \emph{quantitatively
characterised} (mean-field + BBGKY + stability ladder + makespan) at the
same leading order as schedulers with a wider information window,
\emph{competitively near-optimal} (adversarial ratio $\to 1$ at high load),
and \emph{empirically validated} against the real, unmodified scheduler
--- while
provably paying a strictly lower, asymptotically $\Omega(\log N)$ smaller
information-acquisition cost than work-stealing on NUMA hardware
(Part~VI). The kinetic-theory and operator-algebraic methods introduced
here are general-purpose tools for distributed-systems analysis and are
expected to find application well beyond the specific scheduler studied;
the information-theoretic framework of Part~VI in particular gives a
reusable methodology for deciding, on any target hardware, whether a
bounded-information scheduler like DTA or a wider-information scheduler
like work-stealing is the better choice.

% ============================================================
\section{Method}
\label{sec:ai-method}
% ============================================================

ACM's current policy on generative AI does not require disclosure of AI
assistance used in writing the manuscript itself; disclosure is required
for AI used \emph{in the conduct of the research}. This section records
the latter. All mathematical derivations, theorem statements, and
proofs are the responsibility of the human author.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{What AI helped build.}
The simulation/validation scripts in \texttt{script/} (mean-field
solvers, CTMC simulators, DAG scheduling simulations, the FSM verifier
of \S\ref{chap:formal_model}), the \dtact{} reference implementation
(\texttt{dta\_scheduler.rs} and supporting modules~\cite{dtact}), and
the benchmark harnesses and result tables of Part~VII were all
developed with AI assistance and subsequently reviewed by the human
author: scripts against the formal definitions of Part~I and the
numerical predictions of the corresponding theorems; the
implementation via a manual audit of every atomic operation's
memory-ordering annotation against
\S\ref{sec:mpmc}--\S\ref{sec:memorder}; and the harnesses via
verification that their declared constants ($N$, $L$, $H$, $C_W$, $K$)
match the \dtact{} source exactly. Measured and predicted values are
reported as what they are throughout; no measured result was adjusted
to agree with a theoretical prediction, or vice versa, and where a
script's parameters are scaled down from the production configuration
for tractability, this is noted in the script and, where relevant, in
the surrounding text.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Errata from the verification process.}
Four successive audit passes against earlier drafts and against the
\dtact{} source surfaced and corrected roughly twenty substantive
issues, each recorded at its point of use in the text rather than only
here: sign and order errors in early derivations (overload
\emph{contagion}, not anti-correlation, in the Level-1 pair-correlator
analysis; Gumbel $O(\ln N)$ in place of an earlier Gaussian-tail
$O(\sqrt{\ln N})$ in the competitive-ratio analysis; a factor-of-two
arithmetic slip propagated through
Corollary~\ref{cor:stat-benchmark} and the
$\beta_{\mathrm{WS}}/\beta_{\mathrm{DTA}}$ table); implementation
details the model had gotten wrong (the warehouse deflection trigger
is $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ failed hops, not $N-1$; the CAS-storm overhead
term carried a spurious extra factor of $(N-1)$; a memory-order
annotation in the source, \code{backlog}'s two updates, was tightened
from \code{Release} to the \code{Relaxed} the audit's own argument
already called for); proof gaps made explicit rather than left
implicit (Theorem~\ref{thm:lyapunov}'s drift bound replaced a
mean-field average with an unconditional pathwise argument;
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-wait} gained an explicit net-drain
assumption; Theorem~\ref{thm:fj_first} gained its $K \leq N$
precondition after Part~VII's measurement showed the bound violated
otherwise); and one premise invalidated by a since-applied
optimisation (Corollary~\ref{cor:ccx}'s proof, restated against gated
rather than unconditional mailbox polling, conclusion unaffected). The
full test suite, including loom-model SPSC/MPMC linearizability
checks, passes unchanged throughout.
